By bounding the support of the average, we get that

R\tilde{\psi}-iT\tilde{\psi}\ll_{f}\sqrt{\sum_{n}T^{2}\cdot T^{-1}}\ll T^{3/4}.

This and similar computations now show that
\tilde{\psi}
is
O(T^{1/2})
-localised at
\tau
.
In fact, the smooth average gives us localisation to arbitrary order.

3.3
The Op-calculus

We now turn to the issue of producing microlocalised vectors in a general way.
We would also like to decompose vectors into microlocalised ones, following the orbit method heuristic where a basis of localised vectors formally indicates the shape of the Kirillov formula, as in (
3.5
).

The commonly used device for these purposes is an operator calculus.
It was first developed in a quantitative form in
[
NV21
]
and then refined in
[
Nel23b
]
.
Many of its basic properties are described very efficiently in
[
Nel26
, Sec. 6.7]
.

Recall that a representation
\pi
should informally have a basis of vectors
v_{i}
, microlocalised at points
\lambda_{i}\in\mathcal{O}_{\pi}
.
These vectors correspond to pieces
B_{i}\subset\mathcal{O}_{\pi}
of unit volume in the symplectic measure, such that
\lambda_{i}\in B_{i}
.

Now, to suitable functions
a
on
\mathcal{O}_{\pi}
, or more generally on
\mathfrak{g}^{\wedge}
, we would like to assign an operator
\operatorname{Op}(a)
, an endomorphism of
\pi
, such that

(3.10)

\operatorname{Op}(a)\approx\sum_{i}a(\lambda_{i})\cdot\operatorname{Proj}_{v_{i}},

where
\operatorname{Proj}_{v_{i}}
is the orthogonal projection onto
v_{i}
.
Producing a microlocalised vector would then amount to taking
a
to be a bump function on some
B_{i}
.

One of the most important properties of these operators is that they formalise the orbit method heuristic by rephrasing the Kirillov formula (
3.4
) as

\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{Op}a)\approx\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\pi}}a.

Other crucial properties are approximate
G
-equivariance and approximate multiplicativity, that is,

\operatorname{Op}(a)\cdot\operatorname{Op}(b)\approx\operatorname{Op}(a\cdot b).

The meaning and precise form of these properties are described in the following sections.
However, we note already that the approximation signs above become accurate only for functions
a
and
b
that are roughly constant at Planck scale.
This covers the case of bump functions on the pieces
B_{i}
mentioned above.

3.3.1
Operators

We selected the Haar measure
dX=dA\wedge dB\wedge dC
on
\mathfrak{g}
in Section
3.1
, and we choose a dual Haar measure
d\xi
on
\mathfrak{g}^{\wedge}
.
More precisely, if
a
and
\phi
are Schwartz functions on
\mathfrak{g}^{\wedge}
and
\mathfrak{g}
, respectively, then the Fourier transforms

a^{\vee}(X)=\int_{\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{\wedge}}a(\xi)e^{-\langle\xi,X\rangle}\,d\xi,\quad\phi^{\wedge}(\xi)=\int_{X\in\mathfrak{g}}\phi(X)e^{\langle\xi,X\rangle}\,dX,

should be mutually inverse.

Now consider the example of a linear function
a(\xi)=\langle\xi,X\rangle
, for some fixed
X\in\mathfrak{g}
, e.g. one of the basis elements.
For
v
localised at
\tau
, the desideratum for the operator calculus is then that
\operatorname{Op}(a)v\approx\langle\tau,X\rangle v=a(\tau)v
.
From the definition of localisation, this suggests that
\operatorname{Op}(a)
should be the differential operator defined by
X
.

However, to make the approximation sign more meaningful, we perform the following renormalisation, similar to the beginning of Section
3.2.1
on limits to localisation.
Assume that
v
is
R
-localised at
\tau
, and, to have errors in approximations tend to
0
rather than just be smaller in order of magnitude, we introduce the parameter
\mathrm{h}=1/R^{2}
.
Indeed, as in the discussion in Section
3.2.1
, we can think of
\mathrm{h}
as a very small quantity.
If we define
a_{\mathrm{h}}(\xi)=a(\mathrm{h}\,\xi)
, then
\operatorname{Op}(a_{\mathrm{h}})
should act as
\mathrm{h}\cdot X
, and we assume this for our discussion.
Microlocalisation now means that

\operatorname{Op}(a_{\mathrm{h}})v=\mathrm{h}X\cdot v=a_{\mathrm{h}}(\tau)\cdot v+O(\mathrm{h}^{1/2}).

In this normalisation, the error term can be thought of as
o(1)
, as we will generally consider the limiting behaviour as
R\rightarrow\infty
.
It is therefore practical to work with the quantity
\mathrm{h}
, reminiscent of the Planck constant, and this is the common way of phrasing the microlocal calculus.

Remark 3.5
.

We stress that, from now on, we work with a renormalised picture.
We will generally have symbols
a
concentrated around some
bounded
element
\tau\in\mathfrak{g}^{\wedge}
, but
\operatorname{Op}(a_{\mathrm{h}})
will pick up vectors localised at
\mathrm{h}^{-1}\tau
.
Thus, in practice, instead of working geometrically on coadjoint orbits getting bigger and bigger, we can “fix” the orbit, e.g. a hyperboloid, and consider different representations by varying the parameter
\mathrm{h}
.

Recalling the standard properties of the Fourier transform, where differentiation corresponds to multiplication by a linear function on the dual side, we are now led to consider the following pseudo-differential operators.
First, let
\chi
be a nice cutoff function on
G
, as in
[
Nel23b
, Sec. 8.2]
, that is a smooth, symmetric bump function in a small neighbourhood of the identity.
For
\mathrm{h}>0
and
a
a Schwartz function on
\mathfrak{g}^{\wedge}
, define the operator
\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)
on a unitary representation
\pi
of
G
by

\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)v=\int_{X\in\mathfrak{g}}\chi(X)a_{\mathrm{h}}^{\vee}(X)\pi(e^{X})v\,dX,

for
v\in\pi
, and we often write
\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a:\pi)
to make the representation explicit.
We include the truncation
\chi
in this definition because of the use of the exponential map, but the operators we obtain are not very sensitive to the precise choice (see
[
NV21
, Sec. 5.4]
).
This allows us to describe
\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}
as a convolution operator at the group level, which will thus guide our choice of test function in the spectral expansion (
2.2
) in the introduction.
Indeed, we have that

\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a:\pi)=\pi(\widetilde{\operatorname{Op}}_{\mathrm{h}}(a))=\int_{G}\widetilde{\operatorname{Op}}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)(g)\pi(g)\,dg,

as a distribution, where

\widetilde{\operatorname{Op}}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)(\exp(X))=j(X)^{-1}\chi(X)a_{\mathrm{h}}^{\vee}(X).

These are the type of functions that we will use in the pretrace formula (see Section
3.4.2
).

We make a few easy observations (see
[
NV21
]
).
First, if
a\equiv 1
is the constant function, then
\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)
is the identity operator.
Second, if
a
is a real function, then
\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)
is self-adjoint.
The latter is a consequence of the more general fact that the adjoint
\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)^{*}
is given by
\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(\overline{a})
, by our assumption that
\chi(-X)=\chi(X)
.
Finally, ignoring the truncation function
\chi
, if
v
is localised at
\tau
, then we have the desired action

\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)v\approx\int_{X\in\mathfrak{g}}a_{\mathrm{h}}^{\vee}(X)\pi(e^{X})v\,dX\approx\int a_{\mathrm{h}}^{\vee}(X)e^{\langle\tau,X\rangle}v=a_{\mathrm{h}}(\tau)v.

3.3.2
Symbols and star products

The main properties of the Op-calculus are given by approximate identities and their accuracy depends heavily on the functions
a
that we consider, commonly called
symbols
.
The sizeable Section 9 of
[
Nel23b
]
discusses the relevant classes of symbols.
The calculus developed there and in
[
NV21
]
allows for varying degrees of regularity and employs asymptotic notation from nonstandard analysis (see the remarks in
[
Nel23b
, Sec. 9.3]
).
We simplify this by focusing on the types of symbols used in our applications.

Fix a Schwartz function
\psi
on
\mathfrak{g}^{\wedge}
with values in the interval
[0,1]
.
For
\delta\geq 0
and
\tau\in\mathfrak{g}^{\wedge}
define

\psi^{\tau}_{\delta}(\xi)\colon=\psi\left(\frac{\xi-\tau}{\mathrm{h}^{\delta}}\right).

Thus,
\psi^{\tau}_{\delta}
can be interpreted as a bump function on a ball of radius
h^{\delta}
around
\tau
.
One can check that such a function belongs to the class
S_{\delta}^{-\infty}
as defined in
[
Nel23b
, Sec. 9.3]
.
We do not define the latter precisely, but encourage the reader to think of functions of the form
\psi_{\delta}^{\xi}
for some bounded
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{\wedge}
and
\phi
in some fixed bounded subset of the Schwartz functions.
We also often multiply such classes by scalars, such as
\mathrm{h}
and powers thereof, analogously to subsets of
\mathbb{R}
-vector spaces.

To motivate the following construction, we recall from Section
2
that our strategy involves using the operator
\pi(f)
(see (
2.2
)) for a test function
f
that is a convolution of two functions.
We shall specify the latter as
\widetilde{\operatorname{Op}}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)
for some well-chosen symbols
a
.
Applying one of our main tools, the Kirillov formula, requires writing

\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)\cdot\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(b)\overset{!}{=}\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a\star_{\mathrm{h}}b)

as a single operator, for some operation
a\star_{\mathrm{h}}b
, or

\widetilde{\operatorname{Op}}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)\ast\widetilde{\operatorname{Op}}_{\mathrm{h}}(b)\overset{!}{=}\widetilde{\operatorname{Op}}_{\mathrm{h}}(a\star_{\mathrm{h}}b),

equivalently.
7
7

7

To be precise, the way we define
a\star_{\mathrm{h}}b
gives that
\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)\cdot\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(b)=\operatorname{Op}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{h}}(a\star_{\mathrm{h}}b)
, where
\operatorname{Op}^{\prime}
is defined with respect to a different truncation function
\chi^{\prime}
(see
[
Nel23b
, Sec. 8.7]
). However, we noted that this only introduces negligible errors (see e.g.
[
Nel26
, (6.8)]
).

To make this explicit, write
e^{X\ast Y}=e^{X}\cdot e^{Y}
, at least for small
X,Y\in\mathfrak{g}
, as in the BCH formula (
3.6
).
Ignoring truncation, we formally have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)\cdot\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(b)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\int_{Y}a^{\vee}_{\mathrm{h}}(X)b^{\vee}_{\mathrm{h}}(Y)\pi(e^{X})\pi(e^{Y})

\displaystyle=\int_{Z}\int_{X\ast Y=Z}a^{\vee}_{\mathrm{h}}(X)b^{\vee}_{\mathrm{h}}(Y)\pi(e^{Z})=\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a\star_{\mathrm{h}}b).

We use Fourier inversion to deduce that

a\star_{\mathrm{h}}b(\tau)=\int_{X}\int_{Y}a^{\vee}_{\mathrm{h}}(X)b^{\vee}_{\mathrm{h}}(Y)e^{\langle\tau/\mathrm{h},X\ast Y\rangle}.

Now, if
\mathfrak{g}
were commutative,
X\ast Y
would be equal to
X+Y
and the above would reduce to standard convolution, meaning that
a\ast_{\mathrm{h}}b=a\cdot b
.
However, we recall that

X\ast Y=X+Y+\frac{1}{2}[X,Y]+\ldots.

By expanding
e^{\langle\tau,X\ast Y-X-Y\rangle}
into a power series, we obtain

a\star_{\mathrm{h}}b(\tau)=\int_{X}\int_{Y}a^{\vee}_{\mathrm{h}}(X)b^{\vee}_{\mathrm{h}}(Y)e^{\langle\tau/\mathrm{h},X\rangle}e^{\langle\tau/\mathrm{h},Y\rangle}(1+\tfrac{1}{2}\langle\tau/\mathrm{h},[X,Y]\rangle+\ldots).

The term corresponding to
1
in the expansion gives precisely
a(\tau)\cdot b(\tau)
.

We sketch the computation for the term corresponding to
\frac{1}{2}\langle\tau,[X,Y]\rangle
.
To compute the integrals, we can parametrise them using our basis
(A,B,C)
for
\mathfrak{g}
.
We then write
[X,Y]
using this basis for all
X,Y\in\{A,B,C\}
.
For example,
[B,C]=2A
and also
[C,B]=-2A
.
Thus, along
X=tB
and
Y=uC
, we have
\frac{1}{2}\langle\tau,[X,Y]\rangle=\tau(A)tu
.
By standard properties of Fourier transforms (here simply by partial integration), we thus arrive at

\displaystyle\int_{X}\int_{Y}a^{\vee}_{\mathrm{h}}(X)b^{\vee}_{\mathrm{h}}(Y)e^{\langle\tau/\mathrm{h},X\rangle}e^{\langle\tau/\mathrm{h},Y\rangle}\tfrac{1}{2}\langle\tau/\mathrm{h},[X,Y]\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathrm{h}}\tau(A)\cdot\left(\partial_{B}a_{\mathrm{h}}\cdot\partial_{C}b_{\mathrm{h}}-\partial_{C}a_{\mathrm{h}}\cdot\partial_{B}b_{\mathrm{h}}\right)(\tau/\mathrm{h})+\ldots

(3.11)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{h}\tau(A)\cdot\left(\partial_{B}a\cdot\partial_{C}b-\partial_{C}a\cdot\partial_{B}b\right)(\tau)+\ldots

where we continue with the other possibilities for
X
and
Y
.
This last expression, excluding
\mathrm{h}
, is often called the Poisson bracket
\{a,b\}
.

In short, we have

a\star_{\mathrm{h}}b=a\cdot b+\mathrm{h}\{a,b\}+\ldots,

where the omitted terms involve higher order derivatives.
A generalised version of this computation is the content of
[
NV21
, Thm. 4.5]
or
[
Nel23b
, Thm. 9.1]
.

Consider now the case of
a
and
b
of the form
\psi^{\tau}_{\delta}\in S_{\delta}^{-\infty}
as above.
Differentiating each function once, we observe that
\{a,b\}
lies in
\mathrm{h}^{-2\delta}S_{\delta}^{-\infty}
and therefore

(3.12)

a\star_{\mathrm{h}}b=a\cdot b+\mathrm{h}^{1-2\delta}S_{\delta}^{\-\infty}.

This is a special case of Theorem 9.5 in
[
Nel23b
]
.
For this statement to be useful, we must assume that
\delta\in[0,1/2)
.
Upon letting
\mathrm{h}=T^{-1}
, this limitation of the calculus with symbols corresponds exactly to the limits to localisation of Section
3.2.1
.
Put another way, we must work with bump functions on balls of radius at least
T^{-1/2}
(recall Remark
3.5
and note that
T^{-1/2}=T^{1/2}\cdot\mathrm{h}
).
Under such conditions, we also have an informal approximation

(3.13)

\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)\cdot\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(b)\approx\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a\cdot b).

For brevity, we refer to Section 10 of
[
Nel23b
]
for more details on this last statement.

If we let
a=\psi_{1/2-\varepsilon}^{\tau}
, then (
3.13
) implies that
\operatorname{Op}_{h}(a)
is an approximate idempotent.
Thus, it is an approximate orthogonal projector and the image consists of microlocalised vectors, as desired in (
3.10
).
For more details on this intuition, see
[
NV21
, Rem. 6.6]
.

The rescaled version of Kirillov’s formula is given in
[
Nel23b
, Thm. 10.11]
.
For tempered
\pi
and
a\in S_{\delta}^{-\infty}
, with
\delta<1
, a simplified version states that, for any fixed
J\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, there exist differential operators
\mathcal{D}_{j}
for
j\in\{1,\ldots,J\}
such that

(3.14)

\mathrm{h}^{2}\operatorname{tr}\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)=\int_{\mathrm{h}\mathcal{O}_{\pi}}a+\sum_{j=1}^{J-1}\mathrm{h}^{j}\int_{\mathrm{h}\mathcal{O}_{\pi}}\mathcal{D}_{j}a+O(\mathrm{h}^{(1-\delta)J}).

Here, the
2
in the exponent of
\mathrm{h}
on the left-hand side is the dimension of the orbit
\mathcal{O}_{\pi}
.
Notice that, although our calculus generally requires
\delta<1/2
, Kirillov’s formula is more flexible and this is very important.

Indeed, one of the main new tools in
[
Nel23b
]
is an extension of (
3.12
) and (
3.13
) that allows symbols
a
and
b
localising at some
\tau\in\mathfrak{g}^{\wedge}
to have less regularity in the direction of the centraliser
\mathfrak{g}_{\tau}
.
This implements the observations in Section
3.2.1
.

3.3.3
Tailored coordinates and refined symbols

To talk about stronger or weaker localisation at
\tau\in\mathfrak{g}^{\wedge}
along different directions, we generally need to introduce new coordinates, as in
[
Nel23b
, Sec. 9.4]
.
If
\tau
is regular, meaning non-zero in our case, then its centraliser
\mathfrak{g}_{\tau}\subset\mathfrak{g}
is a subalgebra of minimal dimension, namely
1
.
For example, if
\tau\in i\mathbb{R}A^{\ast}\setminus 0
, then
\mathfrak{g}_{\tau}=\mathbb{R}A
.

We now define
\mathfrak{g}_{\tau}^{\flat}\subset\mathfrak{g}
to be the orthogonal complement of
\mathfrak{g}_{\tau}
, using the fixed inner product, e.g. the Frobenius inner product.
Dualising, this gives a decomposition

\mathfrak{g}^{\wedge}=\mathfrak{g}_{\tau}^{\perp}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{\tau}^{\perp\flat},

where
\mathfrak{g}_{\tau}^{\perp}
are the elements that send
\mathfrak{g}_{\tau}
to
0
.
If
\tau=iA^{\ast}
, then
\mathfrak{g}_{\tau}^{\perp}
is the span of
iB^{\ast}
and
iC^{\ast}
, while
\mathfrak{g}_{\tau}^{\perp\flat}=i\mathbb{R}A^{\ast}
.
Notice also that
\mathfrak{g}_{\tau}^{\perp}
is isomorphic to the tangent space at
\tau
of the orbit of
\tau
.

These decompositions give coordinates

X=(X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime})\in\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{\tau}^{\flat}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{\tau}

and

\xi=(\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime})\in\mathfrak{g}^{\wedge}=\mathfrak{g}_{\tau}^{\perp}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{\tau}^{\perp\flat},

tailored to our chosen element
\tau
.
We have
\langle\xi,X\rangle=\langle\xi^{\prime},X^{\prime}\rangle+\langle\xi^{\prime\prime},X^{\prime\prime}\rangle
.

Now let
0<\delta^{\prime}<\delta^{\prime\prime}<2\delta^{\prime}<1
and, for a Schwartz function
\psi
on
\mathfrak{g}^{\wedge}
, define

\psi_{\delta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime}}^{\tau}\colon=\psi\left(\frac{\xi^{\prime}}{\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime}}},\frac{\xi^{\prime\prime}-\tau}{\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime\prime}}}\right)

using
\tau
-tailored coordinates.
This is the prototype of a function in the class
S^{\tau}_{\delta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime}}
of
[
Nel23b
, Sec. 9.4.3]
.
It is a bump on a
‘cylinder’
or rectangle around
\tau
, with width
\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime}}
in the
\mathfrak{g}_{\tau}^{\perp}
-direction and width
\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime\prime}}
in the
\mathfrak{g}_{\tau}^{\perp\flat}
-direction, i.e.
\tau
-direction.
Our goal is to take
\delta^{\prime}
close to
1/2
and
\delta^{\prime\prime}
close to
1
, in which case we have a bump on a
coin-shaped region
, as described by Nelson.
We also recall again the renormalisation we are making, as opposed to the introduction, e.g. Section
2.5.2
, and refer to Remark
3.5
.

Figure 6:
The coin-shaped region (in pink) containing the support of
\psi^{\tau}_{1/2,1}
for
\tau=TA
and
\mathrm{h}=T^{-1}

The analogue of (
3.12
) for symbols in
S^{\tau}_{\delta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime}}
is given in Theorem 9.12 of
[
Nel23b
]
.
In particular, it states that

(3.15)

a\star_{\mathrm{h}}b=a\cdot b+\mathrm{h}^{1-2\delta^{\prime}}S_{\delta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime}}^{\tau},

for
a,b\in S^{\tau}_{\delta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime}}
.
The
\operatorname{Op}
-composition formula (
3.13
) also holds analogously by
[
Nel23b
, Thm. 10.9]
.

We sketch the idea for the proof, which is based on an analysis of the BCH formula, in the case
\tau=iA^{\ast}
, essentially reiterating some observations from Section
3.2.1
.
Here we can work directly with the formula for the Poisson bracket given in (
3.11
), since the coordinates
(A,B,C)
are tailored for
\tau
, as noted above.
Since
\tau
vanishes on
B
and
C
, we have

\{a,b\}=\tau(A)(\partial_{B}a\cdot\partial_{C}b-\partial_{C}a\cdot\partial_{B}b)(\tau)

with no additional terms.
We are done by noting that derivatives in the
B
and
C
directions only introduce factors of
\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime}}
.

3.3.4
Relative character estimates

Turning back to our application, we indicate now that our strategy involves applying the relative pretrace inequality (
2.4
) with
\omega=\widetilde{\operatorname{Op}}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)
where
a\in S_{\delta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime}}^{\tau}
.
Rewriting the inequality without microlocalised vectors (thus making it more precise), observe that we require a lower bound for

\sum_{v\in\mathcal{B}(\pi_{\lambda})}Q(\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)v),

where

Q(v)=\int_{K}\langle\pi(k)v,v\rangle\,dk.

To apply Kirillov’s formula, the expression above needs to be rewritten as a trace.
We record here the formal computations that are described in more detail in
[
Nel23b
, Sec. 12.2, Sec. 12.3]
.
For this, write
T=\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)
for short and decompose
Tv
as

Tv=\sum_{v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(\pi_{\lambda})}\langle Tv,v^{\prime}\rangle v^{\prime}=\sum_{v^{\prime}}\langle v,T^{\ast}v^{\prime}\rangle v^{\prime}.

Then

\displaystyle\sum_{v}Q(Tv)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v}\int\langle\pi(k)Tv,\sum_{v^{\prime}}\langle v,T^{\ast}v^{\prime}\rangle v^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{v}\sum_{v^{\prime}}\overline{\langle v,T^{\ast}v^{\prime}\rangle}\int\langle\pi(k)Tv,v^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{v^{\prime}}\sum_{v}\langle T^{\ast}v^{\prime},v\rangle\int\langle v,T^{\ast}\pi(k)^{\ast}v^{\prime}\rangle.

Applying the same decomposition argument on
T^{\ast}v^{\prime}
backwards, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{v}Q(Tv)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v^{\prime}}\int\langle T^{\ast}v^{\prime},T^{\ast}\pi(h)^{\ast}v^{\prime}\rangle

(3.16)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v^{\prime}}\int_{K}\langle\pi(k)TT^{\ast}v^{\prime},v^{\prime}\rangle\,dk=\colon\mathcal{H}(TT^{\ast}).

This last expression is the relative trace of the operator
TT^{\ast}
.

To apply the Kirillov formula to relative traces, one introduces the notion of relative coadjoint orbits and stability.
The latter notion is particularly important in the subconvexity problem, where it is related to the conductor dropping phenomenon (see
[
NV21
, Sec. 15.4]
).

For this, we generally first define a map
\mathfrak{g}^{\ast}\rightarrow\operatorname{Lie}(K)^{\ast}
, denoted
\xi\mapsto\xi_{K}
, by restriction of linear forms.
Since
\mathfrak{k}\colon=\operatorname{Lie}(K)=\mathbb{R}C
, this is simply the projection onto the
c
-coordinate used in the discussion of orbits.

As in geometric invariant theory, we say that
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{\ast}
is
stable
with respect to
K
, which acts as usual on
\mathfrak{g}^{\ast}
, if it has finite stabiliser in
K
and a closed
K
-orbit.
We now recall that
K
acts by rotation around the
C
-axis (see Section
3.1.3
).
Thus, in our case, all elements
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{\ast}\setminus\mathbb{R}C
are stable.
We can obviously extend this discussion to
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{\wedge}
by the usual isomorphism
\mathfrak{g}^{\wedge}\cong\mathfrak{g}^{\ast}
.

More generally, one can talk about pairs
(\lambda,\mu)\in[\mathfrak{g}^{\wedge}]\times[\mathfrak{k}^{\wedge}]
.
These are called stable if the eigenvalues of
\lambda
and those of
\mu
are disjoint.
Here, if
\lambda
is the class of
2TA^{\ast}
for
T\in i\mathbb{R}
, the eigenvalues (independent of representative) are
\{-T,T\}
, i.e. those of
TA
.
We also view
\mathfrak{k}^{\wedge}
inside
\mathfrak{g}^{\wedge}
to use the same definition for
\mu
, which we can therefore construe as
TC
for some
T\in i\mathbb{R}
.

Now take
\pi
and
\sigma
tempered irreducible unitary representations of
G
and
K
.
Let
\mathcal{O}_{\pi}
and
\mathcal{O}_{\sigma}\subset\mathfrak{k}^{\wedge}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{\wedge}
be the corresponding orbits (or multiorbits).
Since
K
is abelian, in our case
\sigma
is a character
\exp(\phi C)\mapsto e^{ik\phi}
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.
One can easily check that, following an analogous setup as for
G
, the orbit
\mathcal{O}_{\sigma}=\{i\tfrac{k}{2}C\}
is a singleton.

Define now the
relative coadjoint orbit

\mathcal{O}_{\pi,\sigma}=\{\xi\in\mathcal{O}_{\pi}\colon\xi_{K}\in\mathcal{O}_{\sigma}\}.

As in
[
Nel23b
, Sec. 11.2]
, if
(\lambda_{\pi},\lambda_{\sigma})
is stable, then
\mathcal{O}_{\pi,\sigma}
is either empty or a
K
-torsor, meaning a closed
K
-invariant subset on which
K
acts simply-transitively.

Take for example
\pi=\mathcal{P}(iT)
and
\sigma
the character
\exp(\phi C)\mapsto e^{ik\phi}
.
Then the eigenvalues of
\lambda_{\pi}
are
\{-iT,iT\}
and the eigenvalues of
\lambda_{\sigma}
are
\{-k/2,k/2\}
.
Thus, if
T>0
, this pair is always stable.
This corresponds to the obvious visual feature of one-sheeted hyperboloids: the level sets of the projection to the
c
-coordinate (the height coordinate) are always circles and, in fact,
K
-torsors.

Figure 7:
The relative coadjoint orbits for
\pi=\mathcal{P}(iT)
and the characters
e^{ik\phi}
on
K

We have a contrasting picture if we take
\pi=\mathcal{D}(k+1)
.
Here, the eigenvalues coincide, and this corresponds to the fact that
i\tfrac{k}{2}C\in\mathcal{O}_{\pi}
is fixed by
K
.
Even if we take a different
\sigma
to have stability, it is visually clear that there are relative coadjoint orbits which are
K
-torsors, but also some which are empty, since
\mathcal{O}_{\pi}
is a double-sheeted hyperboloid.

Let us now assume that
\mathcal{O}_{\pi,\sigma}
is a
K
-torsor and equip it with the pushforward
\mu_{K}
of our fixed Haar measure on
K
.
Let
\sigma
be the character
\exp(\phi C)\mapsto e^{il\phi}
on
K
and, for
\delta<1/2
, let
a\in S_{\delta}^{-\infty}
be supported on stable pairs.
Then one of the main local results of
[
NV21
]
, as stated in
[
Nel23b
, Thm. 12.2]
, is that

(3.17)

\sum_{v\in\mathcal{B}(\pi)}\int_{K}\langle\pi(k)\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)v,v\rangle\cdot\overline{\sigma(k)}\,dk=\int_{\mathrm{h}\mathcal{O}_{\pi,\sigma}}a\,d\mu_{K}+O(\mathrm{h}^{1-2\delta}).

Notice that, for
l=0
, we obtain a formula for
\mathcal{H}(\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a))
.
Looking towards computing the quantity in (
3.16
), we can use the composition rule (
3.13
) to obtain the analogous formula

(3.18)

\mathcal{H}(\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)^{\ast})\approx\int_{\mathrm{h}\mathcal{O}_{\pi,\sigma}}\overline{a(\xi)}\cdot a(\xi)\,d\mu_{K}(\xi).

Remark 3.6
.

This is part of the orbit method philosophy, where restricting a representation to a subgroup corresponds to disintegration along relative adjoint orbits (see
[
NV21
, Sec. 1.9]
).
For example, the more general estimate for any character
\sigma
of
K
would be useful for studying the sup-norm problem for non-spherical Maaß forms (see e.g.
[
Blo+22
]
).

The motivation behind (
3.17
) is sketched in
[
NV21
, (19.2), (19.3)]
, and we provide here similar computations.
First, take the left-hand side of the equation, and write the integral over
K
using its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{k}
and ignoring the Jacobian factor, giving

\sum_{v\in\mathcal{B}(\pi)}\int_{Y\in\mathfrak{k}}\langle\pi(e^{Y})\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)v,v\rangle\cdot\overline{\sigma(e^{Y})}.

Insert the definition of
\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)
, ignoring the truncation, to have

\sum_{v\in\mathcal{B}(\pi)}\int_{Y\in\mathfrak{k}}\int_{X\in\mathfrak{g}}a_{\mathrm{h}}^{\vee}(X)\langle\pi(e^{Y}\cdot e^{X})v,v\rangle\cdot\overline{\sigma(e^{Y})}.

Put the sum over
v
inside the integrals to obtain

\int_{Y\in\mathfrak{k}}\int_{X\in\mathfrak{g}}a_{\mathrm{h}}^{\vee}(X)\chi_{\pi}(e^{Y}\cdot e^{X})\cdot\overline{\sigma(e^{Y})}.

Assuming that
e^{X}e^{Y}\approx e^{X+Y}
and ignoring the Jacobian again, the Kirillov formula (
3.4
) gives

\int_{Y\in\mathfrak{k}}\int_{X\in\mathfrak{g}}a_{\mathrm{h}}^{\vee}(X)\int_{\tau\in\mathcal{O}_{\pi}}e^{\langle\tau,X\rangle}e^{\langle\tau,Y\rangle}\cdot\overline{\sigma(e^{Y})}.

Move the integrals over
X
and
Y
inside and simply apply Fourier inversion to compute the
X
-integral and obtain

\int_{\tau\in\mathrm{h}\mathcal{O}_{\pi}}a(\tau)\int_{Y\in\mathfrak{k}}e^{\langle\tau,Y\rangle}\cdot\overline{\sigma(e^{Y})}.

At this point, one disintegrates the symplectic measure on
\mathcal{O}_{\pi}
along the projection to the
c
-coordinate.
As noticed above, if
\pi
is a principal series representation, for instance, then all the fibres of this projection are circles.
They become equipped with measures that can be normalised to be the pushforward of the measure on
K
.
Finally, we use character orthogonality through the integral over
\mathfrak{k}
, since
e^{\langle\tau,Y\rangle}
gives a character of
\mathfrak{k}
when fixing the
c
-coordinate of
\tau
.

3.4
Test functions

We are now ready to define a test function for our relative trace formula application, following
[
Nel23b
, Sec. 14]
, where we choose the representation
\sigma
of
K
to be the trivial character.
Let
\pi=\mathcal{P}(iT)
for some large
T\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and let
\mathrm{h}=T^{-1}
and
\tau=2iA^{\ast}\in\mathfrak{g}^{\wedge}
.
Recall here that our usual
(a,b,c)
-coordinates are
\tau
-tailored coordinates, where the
a
-coordinate is the
\xi^{\prime\prime}
-coordinate.

3.4.1
The lower bound

Let
0<\delta^{\prime}<\delta^{\prime\prime}<2\delta^{\prime}<1
, with
\delta^{\prime}\approx 1/2
and
\delta^{\prime\prime}\approx 1
, and let
a=\psi^{\tau}_{\delta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime}}
for a smooth bump function
\psi
with values in the interval
[0,1]
.
More precisely, assume that
a(\tau+\xi)\neq 1
implies that
\xi^{\prime}\gg\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime}}
or
\xi^{\prime\prime}\gg\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime\prime}}
, and also that
a(\tau+\xi)\neq 0
implies that
\xi^{\prime}\ll\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime}}
and
\xi^{\prime\prime}\ll\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime\prime}}
.
Thus,
a
is a bump on a coin-shaped region, of width
\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime}}
in the
B
- and
C
-directions, and width
\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime\prime}}
in the
A
-direction.

In an ideal world, we would apply (
3.18
) to
a
.
Recall that

\mathcal{O}_{\pi,\sigma}=\{T(\cos\phi,\sin\phi,0)\mid\phi\in[0,2\pi]\}

(since
\sigma
here is the trivial representation), with the obvious measure coming from
\operatorname{PSO}(2)
.
We would then have

\mathcal{H}(\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)^{\ast})\approx\int_{\mathrm{h}\mathcal{O}_{\pi,\sigma}}a(\xi)^{2}\,d\mu_{K}(\xi).

Estimating this integral boils down to computing the intersection of
\mathrm{h}\mathcal{O}_{\pi,\sigma}
with the coin-shaped region that forms the support of
a
.

Notice that, if
\xi=(\cos\phi,\sin\phi,0)\in\mathrm{h}\mathcal{O}_{\pi,\sigma}
, then
\xi^{\prime}=(0,\sin\phi,0)
and
\xi^{\prime\prime}=(\cos\phi,0,0)
.
Recall that
a(\xi)=\psi^{\tau}_{\delta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime}}(\xi)=1
if
\xi^{\prime}\ll\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime}}
and
\xi^{\prime\prime}-1\ll\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime\prime}}
.
For
\mathrm{h}
small, we have

\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert\asymp\phi,\quad\lvert{\xi^{\prime\prime}-1}\rvert\asymp\phi^{2}.

By our assumptions on
\delta^{\prime}
and
\delta^{\prime\prime}
, we obtain a lower bound on the integral above of the shape

\mathcal{H}(\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a)^{\ast})\gg\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime}}\gg\mathrm{h}^{1/2}.

In fact, analogous computations also give an upper bound of
\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime}}
by considering the support of
a
.

However, this argument is not valid as it is, since (
3.18
) only applies to
a\in S^{-\infty}_{\delta}
with
\delta<1/2
.
In
[
Nel23b
, Sec. 14]
, this is handled by defining the symbol
a
to first be a bump function on the larger region
\{\tau+\xi\mid\xi\ll\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime}}\}
.
This region can be partitioned into the coin-shaped region
\{\tau+\xi\mid\xi^{\prime}\ll\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime}},\xi^{\prime\prime}\ll\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime\prime}}\}
, on which we take a bump function
a^{\prime}
exactly as before, and the complement
\{\tau+\xi\mid\xi^{\prime}\ll\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime}},\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime\prime}}\ll\xi^{\prime\prime}\ll\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime}}\}
with corresponding bump function
a^{\prime\prime}
.
We can then assume that
a=a^{\prime}+a^{\prime\prime}
.

The arguments above are now valid for
a
.
To deduce the advertised bound for
\mathcal{H}(\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a^{\prime})\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a^{\prime})^{\ast})
, we show that
\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a^{\prime\prime})
is negligible on
\pi
.
For this, the crucial observation is that the support of
a^{\prime\prime}
is disjoint from
\mathrm{h}\mathcal{O}_{\pi}
.
In that case, we are done using the Kirillov formula (
3.14
), expanding to arbitrary degree.

To see the statement about the support of
a^{\prime\prime}
, let
\tau+\xi\in G\cdot\tau
and
\xi\ll\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime}}
.
Thus,
\tau+\xi
lies in the intersection of
\mathrm{h}\mathcal{O}_{\pi}
and the support of
a
.
Using our parametrisation of the orbit, if
\tau+\xi=\tau_{v,\phi}
, then we can approximate

\tau+\xi\approx(1+v^{2}/2-\phi^{2}/2,\phi,v)

by Taylor expansion and the fact that
v
and
\phi
are small, since we assume that
\xi
is small.
It follows that
v,\phi\ll\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime}}
and, therefore, the
a
-coordinate of
\xi
, approximated by
v^{2}/2-\phi^{2}/2
is bounded by
\mathrm{h}^{2\delta^{\prime}}
and we are done.
The same argument, using Taylor expansion, works more generally to understand the curvature of orbits in tailored coordinates, as in
[
Nel23b
, Lemma 13.10]
.

3.4.2
Definition of the test function

The conclusion of our arguments is as follows.
Let
f=\widetilde{\operatorname{Op}}_{\mathrm{h}}(a^{\prime})
, meaning that
\pi(f)=\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a^{\prime})
.
Then we have

(3.19)

\sum_{v\in\mathcal{B}(\pi_{\lambda})}Q(\pi(f)v)=\mathcal{H}(\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a^{\prime})\operatorname{Op}_{\mathrm{h}}(a^{\prime})^{\ast})\gg\mathrm{h}^{1/2}=T^{-1/2}.

This is the precise version of (
2.5
) in our sketch argument and can be found as a special case of
[
Nel23b
, (4.11)]
.

We recall that there is a truncation function
\chi
in the definition of
\widetilde{\operatorname{Op}}_{\mathrm{h}}(a^{\prime})
.
This can be replaced, with negligible error, so that
f
is truncated to the essential support of
(a^{\prime}_{\mathrm{h}})^{\vee}
.
We assume this from now on, referring to Section 14.6 in
[
Nel23b
]
for details, and study the support and size of
f
below.

3.4.3
The upper bound

We start by making the test function more explicit.
We have

f(\exp X)=j(X)^{-1}\chi(X)(a^{\prime}_{\mathrm{h}})^{\vee}(X),

where
\chi(X)
is a truncation function and
(a^{\prime}_{\mathrm{h}})^{\vee}
is the Fourier transform of
a^{\prime}_{\mathrm{h}}
.
Recall now from Section
3.3.3
that we have
\tau
-tailored coordinates
(X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime})
on
\mathfrak{g}
.
For
\tau=2iA^{\ast}
, our choice above,
X^{\prime\prime}
is the
A
-direction and
X^{\prime}
the
B
- and
C
-direction.
Since
a^{\prime}(\xi)=\psi^{\tau}_{\delta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime}}
, by standard properties of the Fourier transform, we see that

\lvert{(a^{\prime}_{\mathrm{h}})^{\vee}(X)}\rvert=\mathrm{h}^{-3}\cdot\mathrm{h}^{2\cdot\delta^{\prime}}\cdot\mathrm{h}^{\delta^{\prime\prime}}\cdot\psi^{\vee}\left(\frac{X^{\prime}}{\mathrm{h}^{1-\delta^{\prime}}},\frac{X^{\prime\prime}}{\mathrm{h}^{1-\delta^{\prime\prime}}}\right).

Here, the first factor comes from rescaling
a^{\prime}
to
a^{\prime}_{\mathrm{h}}
.

It follows that the essential support of
(a^{\prime}_{\mathrm{h}})^{\vee}
is
X^{\prime}\ll\mathrm{h}^{1-\delta^{\prime}}
and
X^{\prime\prime}\ll\mathrm{h}^{1-\delta^{\prime\prime}}
.
Exponentiating and recalling that
\delta^{\prime}\approx 1/2
and
\delta^{\prime\prime}\approx 1
, this is the same as the set
\mathcal{U}
in the introduction, Section
2.5.2
.
A priori,
(a^{\prime}_{\mathrm{h}})^{\vee}
is certainly not compactly supported, but as mentioned before, we can assume that
f
is truncated to this essential support.

Furthermore, it is clear from its abstract or explicit form (computed in Section
3.1
) that
j(X)\gg 1
for small
X
.
Therefore, we conclude that

f(X)\ll\mathrm{h}^{-1-\varepsilon}=T^{1+\varepsilon}.

Taking into account the oscillation of
f
described by
\tau
, this is essentially the same test function
\omega
as in Section
2.5.2
.

Continuing to follow Section
2
, we are left with bounding certain integrals of
f
from above.
More precisely, we concentrate on the bound (
2.8
) that states

\int_{K}\int_{K}[f\ast f^{\ast}](x^{-1}\gamma y)\,dx\,dy\ll\frac{T^{1/2-\delta}}{d(\gamma)}

for any
\gamma\in G\setminus K
.
The proof in our case follows the sketch given in Section
2.8
quite closely, but we remark that it overly simplifies the more general problem tackled by Nelson in
[
Nel23b
]
.
In higher rank, the proof is considerably more intricate and interesting and relies on reductions to some Lie-algebraic results (see Sections 16 and 17 in
[
Nel23b
]
) that are trivial in our situation.

First, notice that the support of
f\ast f^{\ast}
is essentially the same as that of
f
.
This is because, if
X=(X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime})
and
Y=(Y^{\prime},Y^{\prime\prime})
satisfy that
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\ll\mathrm{h}^{1-\delta^{\prime}}
and
X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime}\ll\mathrm{h}^{1-\delta^{\prime\prime}}
, then so do
-X
and
X+Y
.
We call this support
\mathcal{U}
and note that it is of the shape

(D+O(T^{-1/2-\varepsilon}))\cap(1+O(T^{-\varepsilon})).

Using this and the bound above, we can follow the first few paragraphs of Section
2.8
and reduce the problem to showing that

\operatorname{vol}\{y\in K\mid\gamma y\in K\mathcal{U}\}\ll T^{-\delta}/d(\gamma)

for some
\delta>0
.
This is done more generally and precisely in Sections 15.3-5 in
[
Nel23b
]
.
As mentioned in Section
2.8
, we are also allowed to assume that
\gamma\in D
, and this reduction is done in Section 16.2 in
[
Nel23b
]
.

We again pass to the coadjoint orbit by noting that
\gamma y\in K\mathcal{U}
implies that
\dist(\gamma y\tau,K\tau):=\min_{k\in K}\left\lVert\gamma y\tau-k\tau\right\rVert\ll T^{-1/2+\varepsilon}
.
Instead of an ad-hoc argument with angles and the size of hypotenuses, we now follow Nelson by reducing the volume estimate to the size estimate

(3.20)

\left\lVert y-1\right\rVert\cdot\left\lVert\operatorname{Ad}(\gamma)-1\right\rVert\ll\dist(\gamma y\tau,K\tau),

as in
[
Nel23b
, Theorem 16.2]
.
This separates the two variables
y
and
\gamma
and allows us to compute the integral over
y
directly.

Before that, we only need the remark that the operator norm of
\operatorname{Ad}(\gamma)-1
is roughly equal to
d(\gamma)
for
\gamma\in D
.
Indeed, if
\gamma=\operatorname{diag}(e^{t},e^{-t})
, then
\operatorname{Ad}(\gamma)
acting on
\mathfrak{g}
has eigenvalues
1
,
e^{2t}
, and
e^{-2t}
.
Therefore,
\left\lVert\operatorname{Ad}(\gamma)-1\right\rVert\asymp\lvert{2t}\rvert
for small
t
(we can assume
t
is small since
f
is supported on a small neighbourhood).
On the other hand, it is well-known that the hyperbolic distance between
\gamma i=e^{2t}i
and
i
, by definition
d(\gamma)
, is
\lvert{2t}\rvert
.

Therefore, we are computing the volume of the set of
y\in K
such that

\left\lVert y-1\right\rVert\cdot d(\gamma)\asymp\left\lVert y-1\right\rVert\cdot\left\lVert\operatorname{Ad}(\gamma)-1\right\rVert\ll\dist(\gamma y\tau,K\tau)\ll T^{-1/2+\varepsilon}.

This volume is bounded by
T^{-1/2}/d(\gamma)
, since
K
is one-dimensional, and this is the desired bound.

We now give some indications regarding the proof of (
3.20
), following Section 16.7 in
[
Nel23b
]
.
Take
k\in K
and note that

\left\lVert\gamma y\tau-k\tau\right\rVert=\left\lVert\gamma y\gamma^{-1}\tau-k\tau\right\rVert=\left\lVert\operatorname{Ad}(\gamma)(y)\tau-k\tau\right\rVert,

since
\gamma
lies in the stabiliser of
\tau
.
Since
\gamma\in\exp(\mathbb{R}A)
and
y\in\exp(\mathbb{R}C)
, we must have that the logarithm of
\operatorname{Ad}(\gamma)y
(we may assume here that all elements are small enough) lies in the subspace
\mathfrak{g}_{\tau}^{\flat}=\mathbb{R}B+\mathbb{R}C
.
The space
\mathfrak{g}_{\tau}^{\flat}
is a complement (in fact, the orthogonal complement) to
\mathfrak{g}_{\tau}
.
This observation can be used to show that the exponential map on small elements of
\mathfrak{g}_{\tau}^{\flat}
preserves distances when considering the action on
\tau
(see Section 16.6 in
[
Nel23b
]
– there, one should consider
W=\mathfrak{g}_{\tau}^{\flat}
).
More precisely, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\operatorname{Ad}(\gamma)(y)\tau-k\tau\right\rVert

\displaystyle\asymp\left\lVert\operatorname{Ad}(\gamma)(\log y)\tau-\log(k)\tau\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert[\operatorname{Ad}(\gamma)(\log y),\tau]-[\log(k),\tau]\right\rVert,

essentially by plugging in the first two terms in the series expansion of the exponential.
The square brackets denote that we now think of all elements in the space
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Now,
\log(k)\in\mathbb{R}C
and therefore
[\log(k),\tau]\in i\mathbb{R}B
, since we identify
\tau
as usual with
iA
.
If we let
p_{\tau}
be the orthogonal projection inside
\mathfrak{g}^{\wedge}
onto the space spanned by
iA
and
iC
, we have that

\left\lVert[\operatorname{Ad}(\gamma)(\log y),\tau]-[\log(k),\tau]\right\rVert\geq\left\lVert p_{\tau}([\operatorname{Ad}(\gamma)(\log y),\tau])\right\rVert.

Next,
\log y
is of the form
\alpha\cdot C
, where
\alpha=\left\lVert\log(y)\right\rVert/\left\lVert C\right\rVert
.
For small
y
, we approximate
\log y\asymp\left\lVert y-1\right\rVert
.
By linearity,

\left\lVert p_{\tau}([\operatorname{Ad}(\gamma)(\log y),\tau])\right\rVert\asymp\left\lVert y-1\right\rVert\cdot\left\lVert p_{\tau}([\operatorname{Ad}(\gamma)C,\tau])\right\rVert.

To prove that the last factor is approximated by
\left\lVert\operatorname{Ad}(\gamma)-1\right\rVert
, Nelson uses a version of the implicit function theorem and a crucial Lie-theoretic result, namely Theorem 16.3 in
[
Nel23b
]
.
The latter states in our case that
[A,[C,\tau]]\notin[\mathbb{R}\cdot C,\tau]
.
For us, this is a trivial computation, yet in more general situations, especially in higher rank, this is an interesting problem, and it is solved in Section 17 of
[
Nel23b
]
.

Since
[A,[C,\tau]]=[[A,C],\tau]
and
[C,A]=2B
, the Lie-theoretic result states that
p_{\tau}([\operatorname{Ad}(\gamma)(\log y),\tau])
is not zero, and this is an algebraic way of describing transversality as in Figure
2
.
We can directly compute that

\displaystyle p_{\tau}([\operatorname{Ad}(\operatorname{diag}(e^{t},e^{-t})C,\tau])

\displaystyle=ip_{\tau}([-\sinh(2t)B+\cosh(2t)C,A])

\displaystyle=-2i\sinh(2t)C

This is essentially the same computation as in Remark
3.1
, used to quantify transversality in the introductory sketch in the last part of Section
2
.
As above,
\left\lVert\operatorname{Ad}(\gamma)-1\right\rVert\asymp\lvert{2t}\rvert\asymp\sinh(2t)
, and we are done.

3.5
The main archimedean estimates

All the considerations above can now be condensed into the following theorem, a special version of
[
Nel23b
, Thm. 4.2]
.

Theorem 3.7
.

Let
T
be a large positive real number and let
\pi=\mathcal{P}(iT)
be a principal series representation.
For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a test function
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G)
, supported on a small neighbourhood of the identity, such that:

1.

for any orthonormal basis
\mathcal{B}(\pi)
of
\pi
, we have

\sum_{v\in\mathcal{B}(\pi_{\lambda})}Q(\pi(f)v)\gg T^{-1/2};

2.

we have

\int_{K}\lvert{f\ast f^{\ast}}\rvert\ll T^{1/2+\varepsilon};

3.

for any
\gamma\in G\setminus K
, we have

\int_{K}\int_{K}\lvert{f\ast f^{\ast}(x^{-1}\gamma y)}\rvert\,dx\,dy\ll\frac{T^{\varepsilon}}{d(\gamma)}.

We can compare these estimates to those provided by Iwaniec and Sarnak in Lemma 1.1 of
[
IS95
]
.
First, notice that the latter provides a function
k
that is bi-
K
invariant.
As such, it should be compared to the function

g\mapsto\int_{K}\int_{K}f\ast f^{\ast}(x^{-1}gy)\,dx\,dy.

Scaling
k
to
T^{-1/2}k
, we now have similar behaviour on the spectral side.
Indeed, as sketched out in Section
2
, both test functions pick out a family of representations with spectral parameter in an
O(T^{\varepsilon})
-ball around
T
.
The contribution of our automorphic form of interest,
\phi_{\lambda}
, is at least
T^{-1/2}
(see
[
IS95
, (1.6)]
).

On the geometric side, the trivial bound (i.e. part (2) in Theorem
3.7
) is essentially given by
T^{1/2}
in both cases.
However, Iwaniec and Sarnak manage to prove stronger decay properties.
Indeed, in their notation we have
u(\gamma,i)\asymp d(\gamma,i)^{2}
for small
d(\gamma,i)=d(\gamma)
(see Section
7.2
for more details), and their bound is of the shape

k(\gamma)\ll d(\gamma)^{-1/2},

a big improvement over the bound achieved above.

Of course, the reason for having a weaker bound is the fact that we put absolute values inside the two
K
-integrals.
Since our test function oscillates, there is room for improvement, as mentioned in Remark 4.3 of
[
Nel23b
]
.
We will observe a similar phenomenon in the
p
-adic case. See Table
1
below.

However, Nelson observes
[
Nel23b
, Rem. 1.4]
that optimising the entire argument would give the same bound as Iwaniec and Sarnak’s, which is indeed the case.
More precisely, the optimisation can be performed in the counting methods, as we discuss in Remark
7.11
and the sections following it.

4
Non-archimedean localisation

In the
p
-adic setting, versions of the orbit method were already developed very early on by Howe in
[
How77
]
or
[
How77a
]
, for example.
Modern applications of ideas along these lines have been given in
[
HNS19
,
Nel18
,
HN20
,
HN23
,
Mar23
]
, among others.

We take a slightly different approach than in Section
3
and work mostly at the group level instead of the Lie algebra.
One reason for doing so is that microlocalisation can be achieved in a much stronger sense when doing
p
-adic analysis.
It can be stated in simpler terms that are perfectly adequate for practical applications, and we prefer to do so in this paper.
Besides, twisting the
p
-adic picture to fit its much more pleasantly visual real counterpart seemed to offer more confusion than helpful intuition.

To further simplify computations, we eventually assume that the representations we are considering have a level with exponent divisible by
4
. Similar assumptions are made in
[
Mar23
,
HN23
]
. Treating general levels would require us to consider more cases and greatly complicate the notation. While for supercuspidal representations all the ingredients we need are developed in
[
HN20
]
, the case of principal series would require some additional computations. Note that our method will fail for representations with small level such as the Steinberg representation (and quadratic twists thereof) or principal series with little ramification.

The main goal of this section is thus to complement the discussion Section
3
with the natural
p
-adic analogues. However, we are doing so with our application to the sup-norm problem in mind. To make the navigation of this section easier we will now present a little summary of the key points in each section:

•

In Section
4.1
we introduce relevant groups and measures over non-archimedean local fields. We also briefly talk about the Lie algebra.

•

Section
4.2
contains a crash course in the theory of admissible smooth representations of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}
over non-archimedean fields. The main goal is to provide the language necessary for our discussion of
p
-adic microlocalisation. On the way we provide results that are indispensable for our application to the sup-norm problem. Most notably, Convention
4.1
contains the exact local description of the
translated newform
that plays a key role in the global application. Furthermore, Remark
4.6
classifies the representations that can appear as local constituents of (cuspidal) automorphic representations of level
p^{4n}
and with trivial central character. Finally, in Section
4.2.4
we discuss properties of truncated matrix coefficients that will be used when constructing the test function in the global application.

•

The conceptual core of this chapter is contained in Section
4.3
. Indeed, Definition
4.1
is a precise definition of what we mean by
p
-adic localisation. We then discuss several basic consequences of this definition. Section
4.3.1
is analogous to Section
3.2.1
and answers the question:
What is the smallest scale at which localised vectors can exist?
Finally, in Section
4.3.2
we establish orthogonality properties of localised vectors. The corresponding archimedean theory was contained in Section
3.2.2
. The last part of this section links back to the discussion in Section
4.2.4
. Here we formulate a heuristic expectation, see (
4.11
) and Remark
4.16
, concerning the shape of matrix coefficients associated to localised vectors.

•

The abstract notion of localised vectors will be made concrete in Section
4.4
, where we will present two important examples, namely
minimal vectors
and
microlocal lift vectors
. We describe their microlocal support (see Definition
4.2
and Definition
4.4
respectively) and compute (the support of) their matrix coefficients (see Lemma
4.18
and Lemma
4.28
respectively). The latter will be the basis for the geometric estimates that drive our global application. Further, we compute representations of these localised vectors in the Kirillov model and relate them to the generalised new-vector
v^{\circ}
(see Corollary
4.20
and Corollary
4.32
respectively). This will be needed in order to control the spectral side of the relative trace formula, which appears in our application.

•

In Section
4.5
we use the theory developed so far, in particular the connection between our examples of localised vectors with generalised new-vectors, to estimate an important local period. Indeed, the local period
Q
defined in (
4.22
) appears as a constituent in the factorization of the global period from (
6.4
). The most important result of this section is Lemma
4.35
, which is the
p
-adic counterpart to the first statement of Theorem
3.7
.

•

Finally, in Section
4.6
we establish
p
-adic volume bounds analogous to the archimedean estimates from Section
3.4.3
. The main technical result is Theorem
4.40
below. These volume bounds can be used to estimate the local orbital integrals defined in (
4.24
). These appear on the geometric side of the relative trace formula from Proposition
6.1
. In practice, we will use the bound stated in Corollary
4.41
, which can be seen as a
p
-adic version of the second and third part of Theorem
3.7
. Let us stress that the key point of this section is that we can control the non-archimedean orbital integral using relatively soft geometric ideas. We end this chapter with Section
4.6.5
, where we compare our bounds to the results obtained by Saha and Hu in
[
HS20
]
. The latter bounds are slightly stronger, but their proof relies on hard computations using the
p
-adic method of stationary phase.
8
8

8

We have been informed by Paul Nelson in private communication that our soft geometric tools can be pushed further to fully recover the bounds from Saha and Hu.

All the computations here are local in nature, and it will be notationally convenient to work directly over some non-archimedean local field
F
of characteristic
0
.
We let
\mathcal{O}
be the valuation ring of
F
and we denote its unique maximal ideal by
\mathfrak{p}
.
The residual field is defined as
\mathfrak{k}=\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}
and we assume that
\operatorname{char}(\mathfrak{k})\neq 2.

Further we put
q=\#\mathfrak{k}
, choose a uniformiser
\varpi
, let
\nu
be the corresponding valuation, and normalise the absolute value on
F
by
|\varpi|=q^{-1}
.
Of course, in our setting, the relevant field is
F=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, where
q=p
and we assume
p>2
.

The local zeta-factor is given by

\zeta_{q}(s)=(1-q^{-s})^{-1}.

If we equip
(F,+)
with a Haar measure normalised
dy
such that
\operatorname{vol}(\mathcal{O})=1
, then we have

\int_{\mathcal{O}^{\times}}\frac{dy}{|y|}=\zeta_{q}(1)^{-1}.

Recall that
\frac{dy}{|y|}
is a Haar measure for
F^{\times}
.
Finally, we fix an unramified additive character
\psi\colon F\to S^{1}
.

4.1
Groups, Lie algebras and measures

Departing slightly from the notation in the previous part of this paper, we work with the group
G=\operatorname{GL}_{2}(F)
. We will start by introducing relevant subgroups, measures as well as the
p
-adic version of the Lie algebra.

4.1.1
Subgroups and measures

The diagonal torus in
G
is

D=\{\operatorname{diag}(y_{1},y_{2})\colon y_{1},y_{2}\in F^{\times}\}.

Note that
D\cong F^{\times}\times F^{\times}
and we obtain a Haar measure by setting

\int_{D}f(t)\,dt=\zeta_{q}(1)^{2}\cdot\int_{F^{\times}}\int_{F^{\times}}f(\operatorname{diag}(y_{1},y_{2}))\,\frac{dy_{1}\,dy_{2}}{|y_{1}y_{2}|}.

Denote
a(y)=\operatorname{diag}(y,1)
and
a(y_{1},y_{2})=\operatorname{diag}(y_{1},y_{2})
.

Next we define

N=\left\{n(x)=\left(\begin{matrix}1&x\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)\colon x\in F\right\}.

Since
N\cong F
, it inherits the Haar measure from
F
.
Similarly, we can lift the character
\psi
from
F
to
N
by setting

\boldsymbol{\psi}^{(a)}(n(x))=\psi(a\cdot x).

The standard Borel subgroup of
G
is given by
B=DN
.

Finally, let
K_{0}=\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O})
.
9
9

9

In the global setting we will usually write
K_{p}=\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. However, in this setting the notation
K_{0}
will be more convenient.

For
n>0
, we will also encounter the Hecke congruence subgroups

(4.1)

K_{H}(n)=\{k\in K_{0}\colon k_{2,1}\in\mathfrak{p}^{n}\},

where
k_{2,1}
is the lower left entry.
We equip
K_{0}
with the probability Haar measure
\mu_{K_{0}}
.

Using the Iwasawa decomposition we find that the Haar measure
\mu
of
G
can be written as

\int_{G}f(g)\,d\mu(g)=\zeta_{q}(1)^{2}\cdot\int_{F}\int_{F^{\times}\times F^{\times}}\int_{K_{0}}f(n(x)a(y_{1},y_{2})k)\,d\mu_{K_{0}}(k)\,\frac{dy_{1}\,dy_{2}}{|y_{1}|^{2}}\,dx.

4.1.2
The Lie algebra

The Lie-algebra of
G(F)
is given by
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\operatorname{Mat}_{2\times 2}(F)
and the adjoint action is simply conjugation
\operatorname{Ad}_{g}(X)=gXg^{-1}
.
Neighbourhoods of
0\in\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
are given by

L(n)=\operatorname{Mat}_{2\times 2}(\mathfrak{p}^{n})\text{ for }n\in\mathbb{Z}

Note that if
\|X\|=\max_{i,j}|X_{i,j}|
is the maximum norm on
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
, then

L(n)=\{X\in\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\colon\|X\|\leq q^{-n}\}

is nothing but a ball of radius
q^{-n}
. We also set

K(n)=1+L(n)\subseteq K_{0}

for
n\in\mathbb{N}
. As for the exponential map, we define
e\colon L(1)\to K(1)\subseteq G(F)
by

e(X)=I_{2}+X\text{ for }X\in L(1),

which is a good approximation in practice.

Remark 4.1
.

Let
\alpha_{F}
be the smallest integer such that
|\varpi^{\alpha_{F}(p-1)}|<|p|
, where
p\mid q
is the characteristic of
\mathfrak{k}
.
For instance, if
F=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and
p>2
, then
\alpha_{F}=1
.
Then we can define

\exp\colon L(\alpha_{F})\to K(\alpha_{F})\text{ and }\log\colon K(\alpha_{F})\to L(\alpha_{F})

via the standard Taylor expansions. These maps will define homeomorphisms and satisfy the Baker–Campbell–Hausdorff formula

\exp(X)\exp(Y)=\exp\left(X+Y+\frac{1}{2}[X,Y]+O(X^{2}Y,XY^{2})\right)

see
[
HN20
, Definition 3.28]
. One compares this to (
3.6
).

Remark 4.2
.

If we equip
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong F^{4}
with the obvious measure, then we can use the approximate exponential function
e
to pull this measure back to
K(1)\subseteq K_{0}
. More precisely, we get

\int_{K(1)}f(k)\,d\mu_{K_{0}}(k)=\zeta_{q}(1)\zeta_{q}(2)\int_{L(1)}f(1+X)\,dX.

To see that this is the correct normalisation we note that
\operatorname{vol}(L(1))=q^{-4}
. On the other hand, since
K_{0}/K(1)=\operatorname{GL}(\mathfrak{k})
, we have

\operatorname{vol}(K(1))=[\sharp\operatorname{GL}(\mathfrak{k})]^{-1}=q^{-4}\zeta_{q}(1)\zeta_{q}(2).

As in the archimedean case we identify
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
with its dual via the trace pairing
(X,Y)=\operatorname{tr}(X\cdot Y)
.
More precisely, let
(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})^{\wedge}
be the Pontryagin dual of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
.
The identification
\Psi\colon\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\to(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})^{\wedge}
is defined by

\Psi(Y)X=\psi((Y,X))=\psi(\operatorname{tr}(Y\cdot X)).

Given a lattice
\Lambda\subseteq\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
we associate the usual dual lattice and denote it by
\Lambda^{\wedge}
. Using
\Psi
we can identity
\Lambda^{\wedge}
with a lattice in
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
. We set
\Lambda^{\ast}=\Psi^{-1}(\Lambda^{\wedge})
. Spelling out this identification concretely shows that

(4.2)

\Lambda^{\ast}=\{x\in\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\colon\operatorname{tr}(xy)\in\mathcal{O}\text{ for all }y\in\Lambda\}

It is now easily checked that
L(m)^{\ast}=L(-m)
.

4.2
Background on non-archimedean representations

We will now recall some important results concerning the representation theory of
\operatorname{GL}(2)
over
F
. Focusing on the theory of smooth admissible representations of
G
with trivial central character.
10
10

10

Such representations factor through the canonical projection
G\to\operatorname{PGL}=Z\backslash G
and all representations of
\bar{G}
arise this way.
We will start by recalling the main classification result for such representations in Section
4.2.1
. Next, in Section
4.2.2
we will introduce the theory of new-vectors. This is a local version of the classical Atkin-Lehner theory. Section
4.2.3
contains some background on Whittaker and Kirillov models. Finally, Section
4.2.4
contains a short discussion of (truncated) matrix coefficients. The results obtained here will later be essential for the definition of the global test function in Section
6.3
below.

4.2.1
The classification

Let
\pi
be an irreducible unitary smooth representation of
G(F)
with trivial central character.
If
\pi
is infinite dimensional, it is isomorphic to one of the following:

1.

an irreducible principal series representation
\chi\boxplus\chi^{-1}
for a unitary character
\chi\colon F^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
;

2.

a quadratic twist of a spherical irreducible complementary series representation
\omega|\cdot|^{\alpha}\boxplus\omega|\cdot|^{-\alpha}
, where
\alpha\in(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
and
\omega\colon F^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is a quadratic character;

3.

a quadratic twist of the Steinberg representation:
\omega\cdot\textrm{St}
, where
\omega\colon F^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is a quadratic character (these representations are sometimes called special representations);

4.

a supercuspidal representation with trivial central character. These are parametrised by conjugacy classes of cuspidal types
(\mathfrak{A},J,\Lambda)
, where
\mathfrak{A}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is a hereditary order,
J
is a subgroup of the centraliser of
\mathfrak{A}
and
\Lambda
is a smooth representation of
J
. Given such a cuspidal type we obtain a supercuspidal representation by compact induction:
\textrm{c-Ind}_{J}^{G}(\Lambda)
.

This general classification can be found in
[
BH06
, Section 9.11]
.
Supercuspidal representations are then discussed in more detail in
[
BH06
, Section 15]
.
Alternatively, properties of parabolically induced representations of
G
are discussed in
[
Bum97
, Theorem 4.5.1, 4.5.2 and 4.5.3]
.

Remark 4.3
.

Since we are assuming that
q
is odd all supercuspidal representations are dihedral and can be constructed from the Weil representation.
The construction is carried out in
[
Bum97
, Theorem 4.8.6]
, for example.
Exhaustion then follows from
[
Bum97
, Proposition 4.9.3]
and the local Langlands correspondence.

Remark 4.4
.

In
[
Hu18
]
representations of
\operatorname{PGL}_{n}(F)
with generic induction datum are considered.
See
[
Hu18
, Definition 3.12 and 3.15]
for precise definitions.
In our case, i.e.
n=2
and
q
odd, all supercuspidal representations have generic induction datum.
This follows from the discussion in
[
BH06
, Section 19.2]
.
Also, principal series
\chi\boxplus\chi^{-1}
where
\chi|_{\mathcal{O}^{\times}}
is not quadratic have a generic induction datum.

Spherical, or unramified, representations are either of type (1) with unramified
\chi
or of type (2) with unramified
\omega
.
These feature a unique spherical, i.e.
K_{0}
-fixed, vector and are particularly easy to understand.
Their theory will play a role for the amplification method, see Section
7
below.
For the moment, we postpone their discussion and study ramified representations in what follows.

4.2.2
New-vector theory

The
level
of a representation
\pi
of
G
is defined by

a(\pi)=\min\{m\colon\pi^{K_{H}(m)}\neq\{0\}\}.

We call the integer
a(\pi)
the exponent conductor of
\pi
.
It is a consequence of classical Atkin-Lehner theory, see for example
[
Cas73
,
Sch02
]
in the local setting, that the space
\pi^{K_{H}(a(\pi))}
is one dimensional.
Up to a choice of normalisation, a non-zero element of the latter is referred to as the
new-vector
.
For the types of representations listed above the level and the new-vector can be determined quite explicitly.

For
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
with
n=m+m^{\prime}>0
it will be convenient to consider the generalised subgroups

(4.3)

K_{H}(m,{m^{\prime}})=\left\{\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}a&\varpi^{m^{\prime}}b\\
\varpi^{m}c&d\end{matrix}\right)\colon a,b,c,d\in\mathcal{O}\text{ and }\det(\gamma)\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}\right\}.

All these are conjugate to the usual Hecke congruence subgroups
K_{H}(n)
. Indeed, we have

a(\varpi^{-m^{\prime}})K_{H}(m,{m^{\prime}})a(\varpi^{m^{\prime}})=K_{H}(m+m^{\prime}).

Following P. Nelson, see
[
Nel18
]
, we call a non-zero vector
v\in\pi
a
generalised new-vector
if it is
K_{H}(m,{m^{\prime}})
-invariant and
m+m^{\prime}=a(\pi)
.
All of these are translates of the usual new-vector.
Indeed, if
v_{\textrm{new}}\in\pi^{K_{H}({a(\pi)})}
is the standard new-vector, then
\pi(a(\varpi^{m}))v_{\textrm{new}}
is invariant under
K_{H}({a(\pi)-m},m)
and thus a generalised new-vector.
It is clear that all generalised new-vectors are of this form. We finally caution that, if
n=2m
, then we almost always work with the
K_{H}(m,m)
-invariant new-vector. For later reference we introduce the following convention.

Convention 4.1
.

If
n=2m
, then we write
v^{\circ}
for the
K_{H}(m,m)
-invariant new-vector. This is often referred to as simply the new-vector.

We do give a detailed discussion of the general new-vector theory. Instead, we will discuss the special case where
\pi=\chi\boxplus\chi^{-1}
for
\chi\colon F^{\times}\to S^{1}
with
a(\chi)=m
. In this case the generalized new-vector
v^{\circ}
(i.e. the unique
K_{H}(m,m)
-invariant vector) can be constructed rather explicitly, and we hope that this provides some useful intuition. Let us write
\chi_{B}\colon B\to S^{1}
for the character given by

(4.4)

\chi_{B}\left(\left(\begin{matrix}t_{1}&x\\
0&t_{2}\end{matrix}\right)\right)=\chi(t_{1}/t_{2}).

Thus, the action of
\pi
is given quite explicitly on functions
f\colon K_{0}\to\mathbb{C}
satisfying

f(bk)=\chi_{B}(b)f(k)\text{ for }k\in K_{0}\text{ and }b\in B(\mathcal{O}).

This is called the compact model of
\pi
, and we will denote it by
I(\chi_{B})
.

Note that the generalised new-vector
v^{\circ}
is per definition
K_{H}(m,m)=D(\mathcal{O})K(m)
invariant. Thus, in order to understand
v^{\circ}
in the compact model, we use the double coset decomposition

(4.5)

K_{0}=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{m}B(\mathcal{O})\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\varpi^{i}&1\end{matrix}\right)K_{H}(m,m)\sqcup\bigsqcup_{j=0}^{m}B(\mathcal{O})\left(\begin{matrix}0&-1\\
1&\varpi^{j}\end{matrix}\right)K_{H}(m,m).

This is analogous to the corresponding decomposition for
K_{H}(m)
given in
[
Sch02
, Lemma 2.1.1]
. For completeness, we will sketch the proof. Let
k=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in K
and define
(i,j)=(v(c),v(d))
. If
0<i
, then
j=0
and we can simply write

k=\left(\begin{matrix}\frac{\det(k)\varpi^{i}}{c}&b\\
0&d\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\varpi^{i}&1\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}\frac{c}{\varpi^{i}d}&0\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right).

This accounts for the first part of the decomposition. From now on let us assume that
i=0
. In this case we can simply write

k=\left(\begin{matrix}\frac{\det(k)\varpi^{j}}{d}&a\\
0&c\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}0&-1\\
1&\varpi^{j}\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&\frac{d}{\varpi^{j}c}\end{matrix}\right)

accounting for the second part of the decomposition. Disjointness is easily checked by computing the valuations of the entries in the bottom row of each cell.

With this decomposition at hand we can compute an exact expression for the generalised new-vector in the induced model:

Lemma 4.5
.

In the compact model, the generalised new-vector
v^{\circ}
is given by

f_{\circ}(k)=\begin{cases}\chi(t_{1}/t_{2})&\text{ if }k\in\left(\begin{matrix}t_{1}&\mathcal{O}\\
0&t_{2}\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}0&-1\\
1&1\end{matrix}\right)K_{H}(m,m),\\
0&\text{ else.}\end{cases}

Proof.

We will follow the argument from
[
Sch02
, p.9]
and deduce this from the double coset decomposition (
4.5
).

Let
\gamma_{i}=\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\varpi^{i}&1\end{matrix}\right)
and
\tilde{\gamma}_{j}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&-1\\
1&\varpi^{j}\end{matrix}\right).
Recall that we are trying to construct a
K_{H}(m,m)
-invariant element in the compact model for
\pi=\chi\boxplus\chi^{-1}
. In view of (
4.5
), we can attempt to define functions
f_{\gamma}\colon K_{0}\to\mathbb{C}
via

f_{\gamma}(k)=\begin{cases}\chi_{B}(b)&\text{ if }k=b\gamma k_{1}\text{ for }b\in\mathcal{O}\text{ and }k_{1}\in K_{H}(m,m),\\
0&\text{ else.}\end{cases}

Here,
\gamma=\gamma_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,m
or
\gamma=\tilde{\gamma}_{j}
for
j=0,\ldots,m
. For this to be well-defined, we need to check that
\chi_{B}
is trivial on
B(\mathcal{O})\cap\gamma K_{H}(m,m)\gamma^{-1}
. We do so by considering different cases:

•

If
\gamma=\gamma_{i}
for
1\leq i<m
, then we can take
k=\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
c\varpi^{m}&1+\varpi^{m-i}c\end{matrix}\right)\in K_{H}(m,m)
, for
c\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
. We compute that

\chi_{B}(\gamma_{i}k\gamma_{i}^{-1})=\chi_{B}\left(\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&1+\varpi^{m-i}c\end{matrix}\right)\right)=\chi^{-1}(1+c\varpi^{m-i}).

Since the conductor of
\chi
is
m
we can find
c
with
\chi_{B}((\gamma_{i}k\gamma_{i}^{-1})\neq 1
. We conclude that
f_{\gamma_{i}}
is not well-defined for
i=1,\ldots,m-1
.

•

If
\gamma=\gamma_{m}
, then we observe that
\gamma_{m}K_{H}(m,m)\gamma_{m}^{-1}=K_{H}(m,m)
. However,
\chi_{B}
is a non-trivial character of
B(\mathcal{O})
. Thus
f_{\gamma_{m}}
is not well-defined.

•

If
\gamma=\tilde{\gamma}_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq m
, then we can take
k=\left(\begin{matrix}1&b\varpi^{m}\\
0&1+b\varpi^{m-j}\end{matrix}\right)\in K_{H}(m,m)
. For this choice we check that

\chi_{B}(\tilde{\gamma}_{j}k\tilde{\gamma}_{j}^{-1})=\chi_{B}\left(\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&1+\varpi^{m-j}b\end{matrix}\right)\right)=\chi^{-1}(1+b\varpi^{m-j}).

Again we can arrange
b\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
such that
\chi_{B}(\tilde{\gamma}_{j}k\tilde{\gamma}_{j}^{-1})\neq 1
. This shows that
f_{\tilde{\gamma}_{j}}
is not well-defined.

•

Finally, if
\gamma=\tilde{\gamma}_{0}
, then we compute that

B(\mathcal{O})\cap\tilde{\gamma}_{0}K_{H}(m,m)\tilde{\gamma}_{0}^{-1}=\left\{\left(\begin{matrix}a&x\\
0&d\end{matrix}\right)\colon x\in\mathfrak{p}^{m},\,a,d\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}\text{ and }a\equiv d\text{ mod }\mathfrak{p}^{m}\right\}.

Because
\chi
has conductor
m
, we see that
\chi_{B}
is trivial on this subgroup. This ensures that
f_{\tilde{\gamma}_{0}}
is well-defined.

Thus we have seen that
f_{\circ}=f_{\tilde{\gamma}_{0}}
is (up to multiplication by constants) the only possible element in the compact model for
\pi=\chi\boxplus\chi^{-1}
, which is right
K_{H}(m,m)
invariant. This completes the proof.
∎

Let us return to our general discussion where
\pi
is an arbitrary irreducible admissible smooth representation of
G(F)
. We call
\pi

twist minimal
if
a(\pi)\leq a(\chi\cdot\pi)
for all characters
\chi\colon F^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}.

Note that the principal series representation
\pi=\chi\boxplus\chi^{-1}
is not twist minimal (as a representation of
G
), since
a(\pi)=2a(\chi)
and

a(\chi^{-1}\cdot\pi)=a(1\boxplus\chi^{-2})=a(\chi^{-2}).

In general, we have
2a(\chi)>a(\chi^{-2}).

On the other hand, if
\pi
is supercuspidal and has trivial central character, then
\pi
is twist minimal.
This is a special case of
[
HNS19
, Lemma 2.1]
.

Remark 4.6
.

For simplicity, we will later restrict to representations
\pi
with exponent conductor
a(\pi)=4n
. By going through the list of Subsection
4.2.1
and checking the conductors, which have been computed in
[
Sch02
]
for example, we see that there are only two possibilities:

1.

\pi=\chi\boxplus\chi^{-1}
with
a(\chi)=2n
;

2.

\pi
is supercuspidal with trivial central character and conductor
a(\pi)=4n
.

4.2.3
Whittaker and Kirillov models

A linear functional
\Lambda\colon\pi\to\mathbb{C}
is called a
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{(a)}
-Whittaker functional if it satisfies

\Lambda(\pi(n)v)=\boldsymbol{\psi}^{(a)}(n)\cdot\Lambda(v)\text{ for all }n\in N(F)\text{ and }v\in\pi.

A representation is called generic if it features a non-trivial
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{(a)}
-Whittaker functional. It is an easy fact that if
\pi
has a non-trivial
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{(a)}
-Whittaker functional, then it has a non-trivial
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{(a^{\prime})}
-Whittaker functional for all
a^{\prime}\in F^{\times}
. In particular, the notion of genericity does not depend on the particular choice of
a\in F^{\times}
. We will thus specialize to
a=1
for simplicity and drop the prefix
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{(1)}
from the term Whittaker functional.

It turns out that all the representations listed above are generic. Indeed, all smooth admissible irreducible infinite dimensional representations of
G
are generic (see
[
Bum97
, Theorem 4.4.3]
). Furthermore, a Whittaker functional is always unique up to scaling (see
[
Bum97
, Theorem 4.4.1]
) and gives rise to the unique Whittaker model
\mathcal{W}(\pi,\psi)
. More precisely, if
\Lambda_{\pi}
denotes the Whittaker functional, then

\mathcal{W}(\pi,\psi)=\{W_{v}(g)=\Lambda_{\pi}(\pi(g)v)\colon v\in\pi\}.

Note that by construction
W_{v}(n(x)g)=\psi(x)W_{v}(g)
. In this model,
G
acts by right translation.

Finally, note that the Kirillov model is given by

\mathcal{K}(\pi,\psi)=\{f(y)=W(a(y))\colon W\in\mathcal{W}(\pi,\psi)\}.

It is a well-known fact that
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(F^{\times})\subseteq\mathcal{K}(\pi,\psi)
for all
\pi
. Furthermore, the action of
DN
is quite explicitly given by

(4.6)

[g.f](y)=\psi\left(\frac{by}{d}\right)\cdot f\left(\frac{ay}{d}\right)\text{ for }g=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\\
0&d\end{matrix}\right)\in DN\text{ and }f\in\mathcal{K}(\pi,\psi).

If
\pi
is unitary, then the
G
-invariant inner product in the Kirillov model is given by

\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle=\zeta_{q}(1)\int_{F^{\times}}f_{1}(y)\overline{f_{2}}(y)\,\frac{dy}{|y|},

for
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{K}(\pi,\psi)
. We have the following basis of
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(F^{\times})
. Given a character
\chi\colon\mathcal{O}^{\times}\to S^{1}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
we set

\xi_{\chi}^{(m)}(x)=\begin{cases}\chi(x^{\prime})&\text{ if }x=\varpi^{-m}x^{\prime}\text{ for }x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}^{\times},\\
0&\text{ else.}\end{cases}

We see directly that

\langle\xi_{\chi}^{(m)},\xi_{\chi^{\prime}}^{(m^{\prime})}\rangle=\delta_{(\chi,m)=(\chi^{\prime},m^{\prime})}.

Furthermore,
a(\varpi^{s}).\xi_{\chi}^{(m)}=\xi_{\chi}^{(m+s)}
. These are convenient elements to use for computations in the Kirillov model. To illustrate this, we note that the new-vector in the Whittaker model (i.e. as a function on
G(F)
) can be very complicated. See for example
[
Ass19a
]
. On the other hand, it has a very nice description in the Kirillov model:

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\pi
be a generic irreducible smooth admissible representation with trivial central character and
a(\pi)=2m
, for
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Then the
L^{2}
-normalized new vector
v_{\textrm{new}}
is given by
\xi_{1}^{(0)}=\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{O}^{\times}}
in the Kirillov model. Furthermore,
v^{\circ}
is given by
\xi_{1}^{(a(\chi))}
in the Kirillov model.

Proof.

The formula for the new-vector in the Kirillov model can be found in
[
Sch02
, Section 2]
. Note that our assumption on the level implies in particular that
a(\pi)\geq 2
. This excludes unramified twists of
\widetilde{S}
. The corresponding formula for
v^{\circ}=\pi(a(\varpi^{m}))v_{\textrm{new}}
in the Kirillov model follows directly from (
4.6
).
∎

4.2.4
Matrix coefficients of unitary representations

In this section, let
\pi
be a smooth unitary irreducible representation of
G
with trivial central character.
Further, let
v\in\pi
be a
K_{0}
-finite vector, and we associate the (truncated) matrix coefficient

\varphi_{v}(g)=\frac{\langle v,\pi(g)v\rangle}{\|v\|^{2}}\cdot\mathbb{1}_{ZK_{0}}(g).

Here we recall some important properties of the test functions
\varphi_{v}(g)
and the corresponding operators
\pi(\varphi_{v})
. The material is standard and is used for example in
[
Mar16
]
, as well as in
[
Sah17
]
.

First, recall the orthogonality relations

(4.7)

\int_{K_{0}}\langle\tau(k)v_{1},v_{2}\rangle\overline{\langle\tau(k)v_{3},v_{4}\rangle}dk=\frac{\langle v_{1},v_{3}\rangle\overline{\langle v_{2},v_{4}\rangle}}{\dim(V)},

where
(\tau,V)
is an irreducible representation of
K_{0}
and
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\in V
. These follow from Schur’s orthogonality relations for finite fields as stated in
[
Mar16
, Lemma 4]
for example.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
(\tau,V)
denote the
K_{0}
-representation generated by
v
. Then we have

\varphi_{v}=\dim(V)\cdot\varphi_{v}\ast\varphi_{v}.

Proof.

Recall that the convolution is defined by

\displaystyle[\varphi_{v}\ast\varphi_{v}](h)

\displaystyle=\int_{Z\backslash G}\varphi_{v}(g^{-1})\varphi_{v}(gh)dg

\displaystyle=\mathbb{1}_{ZK_{0}}(h)\int_{K_{0}}\varphi_{v}(k^{-1})\varphi_{v}(kh)dk.

Writing the
K_{0}
-integral out yields

\int_{K_{0}}\varphi_{v}(k^{-1})\varphi_{v}(kh)dk=\frac{1}{\|v\|^{4}}\int_{K_{0}}\langle\pi(k)v,v\rangle\cdot\langle v,\pi(kh)v\rangle dk.

For
h\in K_{0}
one uses (
4.7
) with
v=v_{1}=v_{2}=v_{4}
and
v_{3}=\pi(h)v
to get

\int_{K_{0}}\varphi_{v}(k^{-1})\varphi_{v}(kh)dk=\frac{1}{\dim(V)\|v\|^{2}}\langle v,\pi(h)v\rangle=\frac{1}{\dim(V)}\varphi_{v}(h).

This completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 4.9
.

The operator
\pi(\varphi_{v})
is self-adjoint and non-negative.

Proof.

Note that
\varphi_{v}(g^{-1})=\overline{\varphi_{v}(g)}
, so that
\pi(\varphi_{v})
is self-adjoint. Non-negativity follows directly from the previous lemma.
∎

Finally, we observe that
\pi(\varphi_{v})
acts as a projection.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
w\in\pi
be any
K_{0}
-finite vector. Then

\pi(\varphi_{v})w=\frac{1}{\dim(V)}\frac{\langle w,v\rangle}{\|v\|^{2}}\cdot v,

where
(\tau,V)
is the
K_{0}
-representation generated by
v
.

Proof.

First note that
(G,K_{0})
is a strong Gelfand pair.
11
11

11

We say that
(G,H)
is a strong Gelfand pair if
H\subseteq G
and we have
\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{H}(\pi|_{H},\sigma)\leq 1
for all irreducible representations
\pi
of
G
and
\sigma
of
H
.
Given any
w^{\prime}\in\pi
we can compute

\displaystyle\langle\pi(\varphi_{v})w,w^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\|v\|^{2}}\int_{K_{0}}\langle v,\pi(k)v\rangle\cdot\langle\pi(k)w,w^{\prime}\rangle dk

\displaystyle=\frac{\langle w,v\rangle\cdot\langle w^{\prime},v\rangle}{\|v\|^{2}\cdot\dim(V)}.

In the last step we have used the orthogonality relation from (
4.7
). If
w^{\prime}
is orthogonal to
v
, then we find that
\langle\pi(\varphi_{v})w,w^{\prime}\rangle=0
. This allows us to conclude that
\pi(\varphi_{v})w=C\cdot v
for some
C\in\mathbb{C}
. To determine this constant we observe that

C=\frac{\langle\pi(\varphi_{v})w,v\rangle}{\|v\|^{2}}=\frac{\langle w,v\rangle}{\|v\|^{2}\dim(V)}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.11
.

If
\pi
is supercuspidal, then the matrix coefficients
\langle v,\pi(g)v\rangle
are compactly supported (modulo centre) and they satisfy typical orthogonality relations. See for example
[
Car79
, Theorem 1.1 and 1.3]
. Thus, in principle we do not need to truncate them to have support in
ZK_{0}
.

4.3
Microlocalisation: definition and consequences

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\pi
be an irreducible unitary smooth representation of
G(F)
, let
C\subseteq G(F)
be a subgroup and let
\tau\in\widehat{C}
be a character.
We say that a non-zero vector
v\in\pi
is
localised at

(\tau,C)
if

\pi(c)v=\tau(c)\cdot v

for all
c\in C
.

Remark 4.12
.

We have stated this definition at the group level for convenience.
Many examples arise from the level of the Lie algebra, as follows.
Given
C=\exp(L)
for a suitable lattice
L\subseteq\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
one can construct characters of
C
by setting

(4.8)

\tau(\exp(X))=\Psi(Y)X,

for suitable
Y\in L^{\wedge}
.
Using this set-up one can obtain an alternative definition of microlocalised vectors modelled on Definition
3.1
, where the lattice
L
plays the role of the localisation parameter
R
.

Remark 4.13
.

Note that if
v
is microlocalised at
(\tau,C)
then
v^{\prime}=\pi(g)v
is microlocalised at
(\tau^{g},gCg^{-1})
.

This simple remark allows for an important observation concerning the support of matrix coefficients. Indeed, we deduce that, if there is
h\in C\cap gCg^{-1}
with
\tau(h)\neq\tau^{g}(h)
, then
\langle\pi(g)v,v\rangle=0
. See Lemma
4.15
and its proof below for a related argument. In particular, we obtain that the support of the diagonal matrix coefficient
g\mapsto\langle\pi(g)v,v\rangle
associated to a vector
v
microlocalised at
(\tau,C)
is contained in the set

\{g\in G(F)\colon\tau|_{C\cap gCg^{-1}}=\tau^{g}|_{C\cap gCg^{-1}}\}.

The group elements
g
in this set are said to intertwine
\tau
. See
[
Hu18
, Definition 2.2]
for a general definition. In practice, we will work with tuples
(\tau,C)
, where we have good control on the set of intertwiners. This will directly translate to important properties of the corresponding microlocalised vectors. See Lemma
4.18
and Lemma
4.28
below for examples for the support of matrix coefficients.

4.3.1
Limits to localisation

As in Section
3.2.1
, we investigate how large the subgroup
C
may be taken in terms of the level of
\pi
.
To do this, we assume that
C
is an open group, which is always the case in the rest of our discussion, and therefore
K(n)\subset C
for some
n\geq 0
.
We show below that the existence of a vector localised at
C
implies that
\pi
has level at most
4n
.

Since the level is connected to subgroups fixing non-zero vectors, we observe that commutators
c_{1}c_{2}c_{1}^{-1}c_{2}^{-1}
, which we denote by
[c_{1},c_{2}]
,
c_{1},c_{2}\in C
act trivially on a microlocalised vector
v
.

Lemma 4.14
.

For
p>2
and
n>0
(and
n>1
if
p=2
), the commutator subgroup
[K(n),K(n)]
satisfies that

[K(n),K(n)]\cdot(Z\cap K(2n))=K(2n).

Proof.

The inclusion
[K(n),K(n)]\subset K(2n)
is easy to see.
If
1+\varpi^{n}X
is an element of
K(n)
with
X\in\operatorname{Mat}_{2}(\mathcal{O})
, then its inverse is of the form
(1-\varpi^{n}X+\varpi^{2n}X^{\prime})
for some
X^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Mat}_{2}(\mathcal{O})
, by the formula for the geometric series.
Modulo
\mathfrak{p}^{2n}
, the commutator of two such elements has the form

(1+\varpi^{n}X)(1+\varpi^{n}Y)(1-\varpi^{n}X)(1-\varpi^{n}Y)\equiv 1.

For the converse, we first observe that
[K(n),K(n)]
contains certain elementary matrices.
More precisely, looking at the commutators

\left[\begin{pmatrix}1&y\\
&1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}x&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right]\quad\text{and}\quad\left[\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
y&1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}x&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right]

shows that the subgroups

U^{+}(2n):=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathfrak{p}^{2n}\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right\}\quad\text{and}\quad U^{-}(2n):=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
\mathfrak{p}^{2n}&1\end{pmatrix}\right\}

are contained in
[K(n),K(n)]
.
We further compute that

(4.9)

\left[\begin{pmatrix}1&x\\
&1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
y&1\end{pmatrix}\right]=\begin{pmatrix}1+xy+x^{2}y^{2}&-x^{2}y\\
xy^{2}&1-xy\end{pmatrix}.

To proceed, we note the general computation

(4.10)

\begin{pmatrix}1&-b(1+d)^{-1}\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1+a&b\\
c&1+d\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
-c(1+d)^{-1}&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1+a^{\prime}&\\
&1+d\end{pmatrix},

where
a^{\prime}=a-bc(1+d)^{-1}
.
This implies, first, that the group
K(m)
is generated by the subgroups
U^{+}(m)
,
U^{-}(m)
, and
D\cap K(m)
, for any
m>0
.
Second, coupled with the computation (
4.9
) and noting that the matrix obtained there has determinant
1
, it also implies that the commutator
[K(n),K(n)]
additionally contains the elements of the form
\operatorname{diag}((1+z)^{-1},1+z)
for any
z\in\mathfrak{p}^{2n}
.
We deduce that
(Z\cap K(2n))[K(n),K(n)]
contains all elements of the form
\operatorname{diag}(1,(1+z)^{2})
with
z\in\mathfrak{p}^{2n}
.
Under our conditions, any element in
1+\mathfrak{p}^{2n}
has a square root and this shows that
(Z\cap K(2n))[K(n),K(n)]
contains
D\cap K(2n)
.
∎

Lemma
4.14
shows that the existence of a non-zero vector in
\pi
localised at a subgroup
C
containing
K(n)
implies the existence of a non-zero vector fixed by
K(2n)
.
Assuming that the central character is trivial, such a vector is fixed by
K_{H}(2n,2n)
.
As in the paragraph containing (
4.3
), this implies that
\pi
has level at most
2n+2n=4n
.

4.3.2
Orthogonality

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
\pi
be an irreducible unitary smooth representation of
G(F)
with trivial central character. Further, for
i=1,2
, let
v_{i}\in\pi
be localised at
(\tau_{i},C_{i})
. If
