\tau_{1}|_{C_{1}\cap C_{2}}\neq\tau_{2}|_{C_{1}\cap C_{2}}
, then

\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle_{\pi}=0.

This is a non-archimedean analogue of the orthogonality phenomenon explained in Section
3.2.2
.
Note that here we obtain actual orthogonality instead of an approximation.

Proof.

The proof is very simple. Take
c\in C_{1}\cap C_{2}
such that
\tau_{1}(c)\neq\tau_{2}(c).
We then compute

\tau_{1}(c)\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle_{\pi}=\langle\pi(c)v_{1},v_{2}\rangle=\langle v_{1},\pi(c^{-1})v_{2}\rangle=\tau_{2}(c)\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle_{\pi}.

This implies
\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle_{\pi}=0
as desired.
∎

Suppose that
\pi
is supercuspidal, suppose that
C
is compact modulo centre, and let
\tau\colon C\to S^{1}
be a character of
C
. Then the operator

\mathcal{P}_{(\tau,C)}w=\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}((C\cap Z)\backslash C)}\int_{(C\cap Z)\backslash C}\tau(c)^{-1}\cdot\pi(c)w\,d\mu(c)

defines a projector onto the space of
(\tau,C)
-microlocalised vectors. On the other hand, we can fix an orthonormal basis
\mathcal{B}
of this space and define the function

\varphi_{(\tau,C)}(g)=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{B}}\langle v,\pi(g)v\rangle.

Note that this function is compactly supported, because we are currently assuming that
\pi
is supercuspidal.
12
12

12

If
\pi
is not supercuspidal, then one encounters certain issues due to the lack of compactness of matrix coefficients. We will encounter these below.
Recall from
[
Car79
, Theorem 1.1 and 1.3]
that for matrix coefficients of supercuspidal representations one has a version of Schur orthogonality relations available. This allows us to write

\mathcal{P}_{(\tau,C)}w=d(\pi)\cdot\int_{Z\backslash G(F)}\varphi_{(\tau,C)}(g)\cdot\pi(g)w\,d\mu(g),

where
d(\pi)
is the formal degree of
\pi
.

In particular, if
\sharp\mathcal{B}=1
, i.e. the space of
(\tau,C)
-microlocalised vectors is one dimensional, then comparing these two expressions for the projector suggests a very simple formula for matrix coefficients of microlocalised vectors. Indeed, one might expect that

(4.11)

\langle v,\pi(g)v\rangle\overset{?}{=}\begin{cases}\frac{1}{d(\pi)\cdot\operatorname{vol}((C\cap Z)\backslash C)}\tau(c)^{-1}&\text{ if }g=z\cdot c\text{ for }z\in Z,c\in C,\\
0&\text{ else.}\end{cases}

This feature will be nicely illustrated in our examples below.

Remark 4.16
.

Let us stress that we have formulated (
4.11
) as a heuristic to motivate latter formulae for matrix coefficients of microlocalised vectors. Indeed, let
f_{1}(g)=d(\pi)\cdot\varphi_{\tau,C}(g)
and let
f_{2}(g)
be the right hand side of (
4.11
). Then we have shown that
\pi(f_{1})=\pi(f_{2})
as operators on
\pi
. This means when working within
\pi
we can use
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
interchangeably but it does
not
imply that
f_{1}=f_{2}
(pointwise) as functions in
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(Z\backslash G(F))
. However, for very well chosen tuples
(\tau,C)
, it is the case that
\pi^{(\tau,C)}\neq\{0\}
determines the isomorphism class of
\pi
. If this is the case, then our argument actually shows that
\sigma(f_{1})=\sigma(f_{2})
for all
\sigma
in the unitary dual of
Z\backslash G(F)
. This is now strong enough to deduce that
f_{1}(g)=f_{2}(g)
holds for all
g\in G(F)
. See
[
Dix77
, §18.8.1]
for example.

4.4
Examples of microlocalised vectors

To illustrate the abstract discussion from Section
4.3
above, we will now give examples for microlocalised vectors. This will be done in a pragmatic manner, keeping our application to the sup-norm problem in mind. Indeed, we will consider the two classes of representations appearing in Remark
4.6
. For each case of
\pi
that can occur we will construct localised vectors with particularly nice properties. Our plan is roughly as follows:

•

We start by constructing suitable tuples
(\tau,C)
. If
\pi
is supercuspidal, this will be a so-called
cuspidal type associated to
\pi
(see Definition
4.2
). For principal series we call this a
general
\chi
-type
(see Definition
4.4
).

•

Next we define the corresponding localised vectors. For supercuspidal representations these will be called
minimal vectors
(see Definition
4.3
). If
\pi
is principal series, we encounter
microlocal lift vectors
(see Definition
4.5
).

•

For these special localised vectors we can confirm that their matrix coefficients have the form predicted in (
4.11
). See Lemma
4.18
if
\pi
is supercuspidal and Lemma
4.28
if
\pi
is principal series.

•

To provide some intuition for these localised vectors, we also compute expressions for them in the Kirillov model. This allows us to relate them to the generalized new-vector
v^{\circ}
from Convention
4.1
. This is Corollary
4.20
for
\pi
supercuspidal and Corollary
4.32
for principal series.

Both examples that are considered here have already appeared in the literature. In the case when
\pi
is supercuspidal we will use this to our advantage and heavily rely on existing exposition. On the other hand, if
\pi
is a principal series, we will use the rather accessible induced model and prove many of the results using explicit computations. We hope that this makes the material more accessible and nicely complements the existing literature.

4.4.1
Minimal vectors

We start with minimal vectors. These appear in supercuspidal representations
\pi
and have been studied in
[
HNS19
,
Hu18
,
HSY20
]
, for example. Here we record the most important results following
[
HN20
]
. We make the following simplifying assumption.

Assumption 4.1
.

Throughout this subsubsection we assume that
\pi
is a supercuspidal representation of
G(F)
with trivial central character and exponent conductor
a(\pi)=4r
.

We first need to introduce some more notation. Let
E/F
be an unramified quadratic extension of
F
. The valuation ring of
E
is denoted by
\mathcal{O}_{E}
, its maximal ideal is
\mathfrak{p}_{E}
and the residue field is
\mathfrak{k}_{E}=\mathcal{O}_{E}/\mathfrak{p}_{E}
. We can choose
\varpi
as uniformiser for
E
. Note that
\#\mathfrak{k}_{E}=q_{E}=q^{2}
. The (normalised) valuation on
E
is denoted by
v_{E}
, and we obtain the corresponding absolute value
|\cdot|_{E}
as usual. We define an additive character on
E
by setting

\psi_{E}(x)=\psi(\operatorname{Tr}_{E/F}(x)).

Since
E/F
is unramified, this character has conductor
\mathcal{O}_{E}
.

If
E=F(\sqrt{D})
, then we define the embedding
E\to\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
by

a+b\sqrt{D}\mapsto\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\\
bD&a\end{matrix}\right).

We now fix a character
\theta\colon E^{\times}\to S^{1}
such that

1.

\theta
has even exponent conductor
a(\theta)=2r
; and

2.

\theta|_{F^{\times}}=1
.
13
13

13

This assumption implies that
\theta
is a minimal character. We refer to
[
HN20
, Section 2]
for general definitions and more information.

We define an element
b_{\theta}\in\mathfrak{p}_{E}^{-2r}
by requiring that

\theta(1+u)=\psi_{E}(b_{\theta}u)\text{ for all }u\in\mathfrak{p}_{E}^{r}.

The existence of such a
b_{\theta}
is easily verifies, since
u\mapsto\theta(1+u)
is a character of
\mathfrak{p}_{E}^{r}/\mathfrak{p}_{E}^{2r}
. See
[
HN20
, Lemma 2.1]
for a reference. Using this element we define the character
\theta_{b_{\theta}}\colon K(r)\to S^{1}
by setting

(4.12)

\theta_{b_{\theta}}(k)=\psi(\operatorname{tr}(b_{\theta}\cdot(k-1))).

Here we view
b_{\theta}
as an element in
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
using the embedding described above.

We associate the groups
14
14

14

Usually there are two other groups
H
and
H^{1}
, as in Definition 3.12 of
[
HN23
]
. However, since we are assuming that
E
is unramified and that
a(\theta)
is even we have
H=J
and
H^{1}=J^{1}
.

J=E^{\times}\cdot K(r)\text{ and }J^{1}=(1+\mathfrak{p}_{E})\cdot K(r).

One can now extend
\theta
to
\widetilde{\theta}\colon J^{1}\to S^{1}
by setting

\widetilde{\theta}(x\cdot k_{1})=\theta(x)\cdot\theta_{b_{\theta}}(k_{1}).

We call
(J^{1},\widetilde{\theta})
a simple character. It is instructive to check that this is well-defined. Indeed, suppose that
xk_{1}=x^{\prime}k_{1}^{\prime}\in J^{1}
. Then we have

x^{-1}x^{\prime}=k_{1}(k_{1}^{\prime})^{-1}\in(1+\mathfrak{p}_{E})\cap K(r).

Now we can compute

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}(x^{\prime}k_{1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\theta(x^{\prime})\theta_{b_{\theta}}(k_{1}^{\prime})=\theta(x)\theta(x^{-1}x^{\prime})\theta_{b_{\theta}}(k_{1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\theta(x)\psi_{E}(b_{\theta}(x^{-1}x^{\prime}-1))\theta_{b_{\theta}}(k_{1}^{\prime})=\theta(x)\theta_{b_{\theta}}(k_{1}(k_{1}^{\prime})^{-1})\theta_{b_{\theta}}(k_{1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\theta(x)\theta_{b_{\theta}}(k_{1})=\tilde{\theta}(xk_{1}).

Next one constructs an extension
(J,\Lambda)
of
(J^{1},\widetilde{\theta})
. This extension is uniquely determined by the properties:

1.

\Lambda
is a character,
\Lambda|_{J^{1}}=\widetilde{\theta}
and
\Lambda|_{Z(F)}=\theta|_{F^{\times}}
; and

2.

We have
\Lambda|_{E^{\times}}=\theta
.

See
[
HN20
, Lemma 3.18]
for more details as well as for the corresponding general notions.

Definition 4.2
.

We call the triple
(L(0),J,\Lambda)
a
cuspidal type
. Furthermore, it is said to be associated to
\pi
if
\pi=\textrm{c-Ind}_{J}^{G(F)}(\Lambda).

Remark 4.17
.

Note that we have started with a specific character
\theta
and used it to construct a supercuspidal representations
\pi
satisfying Assumption
4.1
. It is even true that all supercuspidal representations fulfilling these assumptions arise this way.

Definition 4.3
.

We call
v\in\pi
a
minimal vector
if there is a cuspidal type
(L(0),J,\Lambda)
associated to
\pi
such that
v
is an eigenvector of the simple character
(\widetilde{\theta},J^{1})
.
We denote such a vector by
v_{\mathrm{MV}}
.

In symbols, the defining property of a minimal vector reads

\pi(j)v_{\mathrm{MV}}=\widetilde{\theta}(j)\cdot v_{\mathrm{MV}}\text{ for all }j\in J^{1}.

Thus, according to Definition
4.1
,
v_{\mathrm{MV}}
is localised at
(\widetilde{\theta},J^{1})
. Note that in our situation a minimal vector actually satisfies
\pi(j)v_{\mathrm{MV}}=\Lambda(j)v_{\mathrm{MV}}
for all
j\in J
. Thus, it is even localised at
(\Lambda,J)
.
15
15

15

This is because we are assuming
c(\theta)
to be even, so that in the language of
[
HN20
, Definition 3.21]
Type 1 and Type 2 vectors agree.
Using the fact that
(L(0),J,\Lambda)
is a cuspidal type associated to
\pi
, one can easily verify that the dimension of
(\widetilde{\theta},J^{1})
localised vectors is
1
. Furthermore, it is a consequence of type theory, that the existence of
(\widetilde{\theta},J^{1})
-localised vectors fully determines the isomorphism class of
\pi
. See for example
[
BH06
, 15.7]
. We conclude that, in view of Remark
4.16
, we can expect the heuristic (
4.11
) to be true for the matrix coefficients of minimal vectors. This is one of the most important results concerning minimal vectors, and we formulate it properly.

Lemma 4.18

(Lemma 3.22,
[
HN20
]
and Proposition 3.2,
[
HNS19
]
)
.

Let
v_{\mathrm{MV}}\in\pi
be a minimal vector, then the matrix coefficient
\langle\pi(g)v_{\mathrm{MV}},v_{\mathrm{MV}}\rangle
is supported in
J
. Even more, we have

\langle\pi(g)v_{\mathrm{MV}},v_{\mathrm{MV}}\rangle=\begin{cases}\Lambda(j)^{-1}\cdot\|v_{\mathrm{MV}}\|^{2}&\text{ if }g=j\in J,\\
0&\text{ else.}\end{cases}

We record the following result from
[
HSY20
, Lemma 2.9]
, which is a version of
[
HN20
, Lemma A.7]
.

Lemma 4.19
.

Up to a constant multiple a minimal vector
v_{\mathrm{MV}}
is given in the Kirillov model by

g_{0}(y)=\mathbb{1}_{\varpi^{-2r}(1+\mathfrak{p}^{r})}(y).

We directly obtain the following, which is a version of
[
HSY20
, Corollary 2.10]
.

Corollary 4.20
.

Let
v_{\mathrm{MV}}
denote the minimal vector and let
v^{\circ}
be the generalised
K_{H}(2r,2r)
-invariant new-vector.
Then we have

\frac{v^{\circ}}{\|v^{\circ}\|}=\zeta_{q}(1)^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{-\frac{r}{2}}\sum_{x\in(\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}^{r})^{\times}}\frac{\pi(a(x))v_{\mathrm{MV}}}{\|v_{\mathrm{MV}}\|}.

Remark 4.21
.

The vector
v_{\mathrm{MV}}
generates the same
K_{0}
-type as the generalised new-vector
v^{\circ}
, see Convention
4.1
. This
K_{0}
-type, which we denote by
V_{\textrm{new}}
, has dimension
q^{\frac{a(\pi)}{2}}(1-\frac{1}{q})
.
This formula for the dimension is obtained for example by using
[
Ass24
, Lemma 2.2]
and recalling that
\pi
is automatically twist minimal in our case.

4.4.2
Microlocal lift vectors

Microlocal lift vectors play a major role in
[
Nel18
,
Mar23
]
.
In
[
Nel18
]
the properties of the microlocal lifts are pragmatically obtained via well-known properties of the new-vector in
1\boxplus\chi^{-2}
and transported via twisting. Here we aim to develop the theory using
microlocal calculations
. From this point of view many ideas can be found in
[
Mar23
,
HN23
]
, and we closely follow
[
Mar23
]
.

We will assume that
\pi=\chi\boxplus\chi^{-1}
for
\chi\colon F^{\times}\to S^{1}
with
a(\chi)=m=2r\geq 2
. Recall that in (
4.4
) we lifted
\chi
to a character
\chi_{B}\colon B\to S^{1}
on
B
. For convenience, we restate the definition:

\chi_{B}\left(\left(\begin{matrix}t_{1}&x\\
0&t_{2}\end{matrix}\right)\right)=\chi(t_{1}/t_{2}).

Further, the compact model for
\pi
was denoted by
I(\chi_{B})
. It consists of functions
f\colon K_{0}\to\mathbb{C}
satisfying

f(bk)=\chi_{B}(b)f(k)\text{ for }k\in K_{0}\text{ and }b\in B(\mathcal{O}).

Since
a(\chi)=2r
, the map
x\mapsto\chi(1+\varpi^{r}x)
is additive on
\mathcal{O}
. Thus, we can write

\chi(1+\varpi^{r}x)=\psi(b_{\chi}\cdot x\cdot\varpi^{-r})

where
b_{\chi}\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
is uniquely determined modulo
\mathfrak{p}^{r}
. We define

\tau({\chi})=\varpi^{-m}\left(\begin{matrix}b_{\chi}&0\\
0&-b_{\chi}\end{matrix}\right)\in L(-m).

More generally, given
\tau\in L(-m)
, we define
\theta_{\tau}\colon K(r)\to S^{1}
by

\theta_{\tau}(k)=\psi(\operatorname{tr}(\tau(k-1))).

This is analogous to (
4.12
).

We start with an easy but very useful observation.

Lemma 4.22
.

The following statements are true:

1.

Let
\tau\in L(-m)
be such that
\chi_{B}|_{B(\mathcal{O})\cap K(r)}=\theta_{\tau}|_{B(\mathcal{O})\cap K(r)}
. Then we have

\tau-\left(\begin{matrix}\varpi^{-m}b_{\chi}&\tau_{1,2}\\
0&-\varpi^{-m}b_{\chi}\end{matrix}\right)\in L(-r).

2.

Let
\chi_{D}
be the character on
D(\mathcal{O})
given by
\chi_{D}(\operatorname{diag}(t_{1},t_{2}))=\chi(t_{1}/t_{2}).
Then
\widetilde{\chi}(tk)=\chi_{D}(t)\cdot\theta_{\tau(\chi)}(k)
is the unique extension of
\chi_{D}
to
\widetilde{D}({r})=D(\mathcal{O})\cdot K(r).

Proof.

To prove the first statement, we take

b=\left(\begin{matrix}1+\varpi^{r}t_{1}&\varpi^{r}y\\
0&1+\varpi^{r}t_{2}\end{matrix}\right)\in B(\mathcal{O})\cap K(r).

We compute

(4.13)

\chi_{B}(b)=\psi((t_{1}-t_{2})b_{\chi}\varpi^{-r}).

On the other hand, unpacking the definition of
\theta_{\tau}
we get

(4.14)

\theta_{\tau}(b)=\psi((\tau_{1,1}t_{1}+\tau_{2,2}t_{2}+\tau_{2,1}y)\varpi^{r}).

Note that by assumption (
4.13
) is equal to (
4.14
) for all
t_{1},t_{2},y\in\mathcal{O}
. This directly gives the first result.

The second claim is essentially
[
Mar23
, Lemma 2.1]
. To start, let us note that
\widetilde{\chi}
is obviously well-defined. It remains to check uniqueness. Assume that
\xi
is some extension of
\chi_{D}
and consider
\xi^{\prime}=\widetilde{\chi}\cdot\xi^{-1}
. By assumption, we have
\xi^{\prime}|_{D(\mathcal{O})}\equiv 1
. On the other hand,
\xi^{\prime}
is a character, and so it is trivial on the commutator
[\widetilde{D}({r}),\widetilde{D}({r})]
.
Observe that

\left[\left(\begin{matrix}1&\mathfrak{p}^{r}\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right),\left(\begin{matrix}\mathcal{O}^{\times}&0\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)\right]=\left(\begin{matrix}1&\mathfrak{p}^{r}\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)\text{ and }\left[\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\mathfrak{p}^{r}&1\end{matrix}\right),\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&\mathcal{O}^{\times}\end{matrix}\right)\right]=\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\mathfrak{p}^{r}&1\end{matrix}\right).

In particular,
[\widetilde{D}({r}),\widetilde{D}({r})]
contains (among others) matrices of the form

\left(\begin{matrix}1&\mathfrak{p}^{r}\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)\text{ and }\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\mathfrak{p}^{r}&1\end{matrix}\right).

We conclude that
\xi^{\prime}
must be trivial.
∎

Definition 4.4

(Definition 2.1,
[
Mar23
]
)
.

We call the tuple
(\widetilde{D}(r),\widetilde{\chi})
the
standard
\chi
-type
. A general
\chi
-type is then a tuple
(J,\lambda)
obtained by conjugating the standard
\chi
-type by an element in
K_{0}
. (I.e.
J=\operatorname{Ad}_{k}\widetilde{D}(r)
and
\lambda=\operatorname{Ad}^{\ast}_{k}(\widetilde{\chi})
for
k\in K_{0}
.)

We can now construct a
(\widetilde{\chi},\widetilde{D}(r))
-localised vector.

Lemma 4.23
.

The representation
\pi
contains an up to scaling unique
(\widetilde{\chi},\widetilde{D}(r))
-localised element denoted by
v_{\mathrm{ML}}\in\pi
. The corresponding element
f_{\mathrm{ML}}\in I(\chi_{B})
is given by

f_{\textrm{ML}}(k)=\begin{cases}\chi_{B}(b)\theta_{\tau(\chi)}(k_{1})&\text{ if }k=bk_{1}\in B(\mathcal{O})K(r),\\
0&\text{ else.}\end{cases}

Proof.

For the proof it is sufficient to work in the compact model
I(\chi_{B})
. More precisely, we have to show that
f_{\textrm{ML}}
is well-defined and essentially unique. The first fact follows directly from Lemma
4.22
.

We turn to uniqueness. Suppose that
f\in I(\chi_{B})
is another non-trivial vector with the same localisation properties. Since we are assuming that
f
is non-trivial, we can fix
k=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in K_{0}
with
f(k)\neq 0
. For
b\in B(\mathcal{O})\cap K(r)
we compute that

\chi_{B}(b)f(k)=f(bk)=f(k)\theta_{\tau(\chi)}(k^{-1}bk)=f(k)\theta_{\operatorname{Ad}_{k}(\tau(\chi))}(b).

In particular, we have
\chi_{B}(b)=\theta_{\operatorname{Ad}_{k}(\tau(\chi))}(b)
for
b\in B(\mathcal{O})\cap K(r).
In view of the first part of Lemma
4.22
, we obtain that

\frac{\varpi^{-m}b_{\chi}}{ad-bc}\cdot\left(\begin{matrix}ad+bc&-2ab\\
2cd&-(ad+bc)\end{matrix}\right)=\operatorname{Ad}_{k}(\tau(\chi))\in\left(\begin{matrix}\varpi^{-m}b_{\chi}&-\frac{b_{\chi}\varpi^{-m}}{ad-bc}ab\\
0&-\varpi^{-m}b_{\chi}\end{matrix}\right)+L(-r).

Recall that we are working in odd residual characteristic, so that comparing entries gives the two conditions

bc\in\mathfrak{p}^{r}\text{ and }cd\in\mathfrak{p}^{r}.

This implies
k\in B(\mathcal{O})\cdot K(r)
. In particular, using the transformation behaviour of
f
shows that
f(1)\neq 0
and this lets us conclude that
f
is a scalar multiple of
f_{\mathrm{ML}}
.
∎

Definition 4.5

(Microlocal Lift Vector)
.

We call
v\in I(\chi)
a
microlocal lift vector
if there is a
\chi
-type
(J,\lambda)
such that
\pi(j)v=\lambda(j)v
for all
j\in J
. More precisely we say that
v
is a microlocal lift vector associated to
(J,\lambda)
. The microlocal lift vector
v
associated to the standard
\chi
-type (i.e.
v_{\mathrm{ML}}=f_{\textrm{ML}}
) is referred to as standard microlocal lift vector.

Remark 4.24
.

This is essentially
[
Mar23
, Definition 2.3]
. It should be noted that the standard microlocal lift vector features prominently in
[
Nel18
]
.

Lemma 4.25

(Lemma 2.4,
[
Mar23
]
)
.

For each
\chi
-type
(J,\lambda)
, the space of microlocal lift vectors in
I(\chi)
associated with
(J,\lambda)
is
1
-dimensional.

Proof.

After conjugation by an element from
K_{0}
, this follows directly from the uniqueness of the standard microlocal lift vector.
∎

Lemma 4.26

(Lemma 2.5,
[
Mar23
]
)
.

A nonzero microlocal lift vector is associated to at most one
\chi
-type.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
v
is the standard microlocal lift vector. Suppose that there is another
\chi
-type
(J,\lambda)
such that
\pi(j)v=\lambda(j)v
. By definition, we have
J=k\widetilde{D}(r)k^{-1}
for
k=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix}\in K_{0}
. Furthermore,

(4.15)

\widetilde{\chi}|_{K(r)}=\operatorname{Ad}_{k}^{\ast}(\widetilde{\chi})|_{K(r)}.

Writing out what this means leads to congruences, which in turn imply
k\in\widetilde{D}(r)
. Indeed, take
g=1+\varpi^{r}\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in K(r)
. Then we have

(4.16)

\psi(\varpi^{-r}b_{\chi}(a-d))=\widetilde{\chi}(g)=\operatorname{Ad}_{k}^{\ast}(\widetilde{\chi})(g)\\
=\psi\left(\frac{\varpi^{-r}b_{\chi}}{\alpha\delta-\beta\gamma}\cdot\left[(\alpha\delta+\beta\gamma)(a-d)+2\beta\delta\cdot c-2\alpha\gamma\cdot b\right]\right).

Recall that
g\in K(r)
can be anything, so that we can vary
(a-d),b,c\in\mathcal{O}
. We obtain the congruence conditions

\frac{\alpha\delta+\beta\gamma}{\alpha\delta-\beta\gamma}\in 1+\mathfrak{p}^{r},\,2\beta\delta\in\mathfrak{p}^{r}\text{ and }2\alpha\gamma\in\mathfrak{p}^{r}.

Since
\alpha\delta-\beta\gamma=\det(k)\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
(and
2\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
) the only solution to this system is
\beta,\gamma\in\mathfrak{p}^{r}
. In other words
k\in\widetilde{D}(r)
as desired.
∎

Lemma 4.27

(Lemma 2.6,
[
Mar23
]
)
.

The stabiliser in
K_{0}
of a
\chi
-type
(J,\lambda)
is equal to
J
.

Proof.

The computations relevant to the proof of this result are very similar to those in the proof of Lemma
4.26
and we will only sketch them. First, by conjugation, we reduce to the case
(J,\lambda)=(\widetilde{D}(r),\widetilde{\chi})
. Next, suppose that
k\in K_{0}
stabilises
(\widetilde{D}(r),\widetilde{\chi})
. Since
K(r)
is normal in
K
we find that
K(r)\subseteq\widetilde{D}(r)\cap k\widetilde{D}(r)k^{-1}
. Thus, we arrive at the identity (
4.15
) and can proceed as in the proof of Lemma
4.26
to show that
k\in\widetilde{D}(r)
as claimed.
∎

We now turn towards understanding the matrix coefficients associated to microlocal lift vectors. As in the case of minimal vectors considered above we expect them to behave as predicted in (
4.11
).
16
16

16

Note that this ignores some issues arising from the fact that matrix coefficients of principal series representations are not compactly supported modulo the centre.
Indeed, in lieu of the strong uniqueness properties exhibited by
\chi
-types, this expectation is morally supported by the discussion in Remark
4.16
. For our purposes we will content ourselves with the following result concerning the support of the matrix coefficients, which we can prove rather directly.

Lemma 4.28

(Lemma 2.7,
[
Mar23
]
)
.

We have
\langle\pi(g)v_{\mathrm{ML}},v_{\mathrm{ML}}\rangle=0
unless
g\in K(r)D(F)K(r).

Proof.

Let
\varphi(g)=\langle\pi(g)v_{\mathrm{ML}},v_{\mathrm{ML}}\rangle
. We can express this matrix coefficient in the induced model. To do so we extend
f_{\textrm{ML}}\in I(\chi_{B})
to a function on
G(F)
via the Iwasawa decomposition and recall that the inner product in the induced model is given by

\varphi(g)=\langle\pi(g)v_{\mathrm{ML}},v_{\mathrm{ML}}\rangle=\int_{K_{0}}f_{\textrm{ML}}(kg)\overline{f_{\textrm{ML}}(k)}dk.

We now compute that

\langle\pi(g)v_{\mathrm{ML}},v_{\mathrm{ML}}\rangle=[B(\mathcal{O})\colon B(\mathcal{O})\cap K(r)]\int_{K(r)}\theta_{\tau(\chi)}(k)^{-1}f_{\textrm{ML}}(kg)dk.

Note that
f_{\textrm{ML}}(kg)=0
unless
kg\in BK(r).
In particular, we must have
g\in K(r)BK(r)
. After using the centre and making an easy change of variables we can assume that

g=\left(\begin{matrix}t&x\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)\in B.

With this information gathered we return to the abstract setting. As in Lemma
4.15
we start by observing that

\theta_{\tau(\chi)}(k_{1})\varphi(g)=\varphi(k_{1}g)=\theta_{\tau(\chi)}(g^{-1}k_{1}g)\varphi(g)

for
k_{1}\in K(r)\cap gK(r)g^{-1}
. If we assume that
\varphi(g)\neq 0
, then we must have

(4.17)

\theta_{\tau(\chi)}(k_{1})=\theta_{\tau(\chi)}(g^{-1}k_{1}g)\text{ for }k_{1}\in K(r)\cap gK(r)g^{-1}.

We define the lattice
L_{g}=L(r)\cap gL(r)g^{-1}
and consider the corresponding dual lattice
L_{g}^{\ast}
. See (
4.2
) for the general definition of the dual lattice. We now claim that (
4.17
) holds if and only if

\tau(\chi)-g\tau(\chi)g^{-1}\in L_{g}^{\ast}=L(r)^{\ast}+gL(r)^{\ast}g^{-1}.

This is easy to see and stated explicitly in
[
How77a
, Lemma 2.1]
for example. We conclude that there are
X,X^{\prime}\in L(r)
such that

(4.18)

\left(\begin{matrix}b_{\chi}&0\\
0&-b_{\chi}\end{matrix}\right)+X=g\left(\left(\begin{matrix}b_{\chi}&0\\
0&-b_{\chi}\end{matrix}\right)+X^{\prime}\right)g^{-1}.

Using the specific shape of
g
allows us to write this as

\left(\begin{matrix}0&2xb_{\chi}\\
0&0\end{matrix}\right)=-X+gX^{\prime}g^{-1}.

Using the three conditions
[gX^{\prime}g^{-1}]_{ij}\in\mathfrak{p}^{r}
for
(i,j)\in\{(1,1),(2,1),(2,2)\}
allows us to deduce the congruence

2xb_{\chi}\in\mathfrak{p}^{r}+t\mathfrak{p}^{r}+x\mathfrak{p}^{r}

from the top right entry of (
4.18
). If
x\in\mathfrak{p}^{r}
, then
g=n(x)a(t)\in K(r)D(F)
and we are done. Otherwise, we must have
v(t)\leq v(x)-r
. But this implies that
g=a(t)n(x/t)
and
n(x/t)\in K(r)
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.29
.

The proof given in
[
Mar23
]
is more conceptual and crucially uses that
\tau(\chi)
is regular. Similar arguments are very common in the literature. See for example
[
How77a
, Lemma 3.3]
. In particular, the crucial technical lemma
[
Mar23
, Lemma 2.8]
is a slight refinement of
[
How77a
, Lemma 3.1]
. Here we took a more direct approach, because for
\operatorname{GL}(2)
this seems to be more transparent.

We turn our attention to the Kirillov model and let
g_{\textrm{ML}}\in\mathcal{K}(\pi,\psi)
be the element in the Kirillov model corresponding to
v_{\mathrm{ML}}
. In
[
Nel18
, Lemma 48]
it is shown that

(4.19)

\frac{g_{\textrm{ML}}(y)}{\|g_{\textrm{ML}}\|}=\eta\cdot\zeta_{q}(1)^{-\frac{1}{2}}q^{-\frac{r}{2}}\chi(y)|y|^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{1}_{\mathfrak{p}^{-r}}(y),

for some
\eta\in S^{1}
. An easy computation shows that this is orthogonal to
\xi_{\mathbf{1}}^{(m)}\in\mathcal{K}(\pi,\psi)
. As we have seen in Lemma
4.7
the latter function belongs to the generalised new-vector
v^{\circ}
. We conclude that the two vectors
v_{\mathrm{ML}}
and
v^{\circ}
are orthogonal.

However, for our global application we will need to construct a localised vector that is not orthogonal to the generalised new-vector (see more in Lemma
4.35
). This motivates us to study translates of
v_{\mathrm{ML}}
. It turns out that certain
K_{0}
-translates are sufficient. Put

\gamma_{a}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&-1\\
1&a\end{matrix}\right)\text{ and }\widetilde{\gamma}_{a}=\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
a&1\end{matrix}\right).

Note that

\widetilde{\gamma}_{a\varpi^{i}}=\operatorname{diag}(1,a)\widetilde{\gamma}_{\varpi^{i}}\operatorname{diag}(1,a^{-1})\text{ and }\gamma_{a\varpi^{i}}=\operatorname{diag}(1,a)\gamma_{\varpi^{i}}\operatorname{diag}(a^{-1},1).

Furthermore, for
a\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
we have

\left(\begin{matrix}a^{-1}&1\\
0&a\end{matrix}\right)\gamma_{a^{-1}}=\widetilde{\gamma}_{a}.

In particular, we only need to consider
\widetilde{\gamma}_{a}
for
a\in\mathfrak{p}.
More generally, we are motivated by the double coset decomposition

K_{0}=\bigsqcup_{a\in\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}^{r}}B(\mathcal{O})\left(\begin{matrix}0&-1\\
1&a\end{matrix}\right)K(r)\sqcup\bigsqcup_{a\in\mathfrak{p}/\mathfrak{p}^{r}}B(\mathcal{O})\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
a&1\end{matrix}\right)K(r).

This is a mild extension of
[
Ass24
, Lemma 3.4]
. Alternatively it can be checked by considering the right-action of
K_{0}
on the vector
(0,1)\in\mathcal{O}^{2}
and identifying the quotient
B(\mathcal{O})\backslash K_{0}
with the set of primitive vectors in
\mathcal{O}^{2}
quotiented by the action of
\mathcal{O}^{\times}
.

In principle one can use Lemma
4.5
and work completely in the induced model. However, it seems to be clearer to work in the Kirillov model. To translate between the two models we need the following lemma.

Lemma 4.30
.

For
a\in\mathcal{O}
let
v_{a}=\pi(\gamma_{a})v_{\mathrm{ML}}
and
\widetilde{v}_{a}=\pi(\widetilde{\gamma}_{a})v_{\mathrm{ML}}
. Denote the corresponding elements in the Kirillov model by
g_{a},\widetilde{g}_{a}\in\mathcal{K}(\pi,\psi)
. We have

\frac{g_{a}(y)}{\|g_{a}\|}=\zeta_{q}(1)^{-\frac{1}{2}}q^{-r/2}\chi(y)^{-1}|y|^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{1}_{2b_{\chi}a\varpi^{-m}+\mathfrak{p}^{-r}}(y).

Furthermore, if
a=a_{0}\cdot\varpi^{i}
for
0<i<r
and
a_{0}\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
, then

\frac{\widetilde{g}_{a}(y)}{\|\widetilde{g}_{a}\|}=\epsilon(\frac{1}{2},\chi^{2})\zeta_{q}(1)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\chi(y\varpi^{2i})q^{-\frac{r}{2}}\mathbb{1}_{(2a_{0}b_{\chi}+\mathfrak{p}^{r-i})\varpi^{i-m}}(y)

Finally, (
4.19
) holds with
\eta=\epsilon(\frac{1}{2},\chi^{2})
.

Proof.

Our goal is to compute
W_{v}(a(y))=\Lambda_{\pi}(\pi(a(y)v)
. To do so we need a workable expression of
\Lambda_{\pi}
. For principal series this can be written down explicitly. Suppose
v
is given by
f_{v}\in I(\chi)
. Then, using the Iwasawa decomposition, we can extend
f_{v}
to
G(F)
by setting

f_{v}(bk)=\chi(t_{1}/t_{2})|t_{1}/t_{2}|^{\frac{1}{2}}f(k)\text{ for }b=\left(\begin{matrix}t_{1}&y\\
0&t_{2}\end{matrix}\right).

Let
w=\left(\begin{matrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{matrix}\right)
be the long Weyl element. We can now define

\Lambda_{\pi}(f_{v})=\int_{F}^{\textrm{st}}f_{v}\left(w\left(\begin{matrix}1&x\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)\right)\psi(x)^{-1}dx.

Here
\int_{F}^{\textrm{st}}=\lim_{r\to\infty}\int_{\mathfrak{p}^{-r}}
is the stabilized integral. This allows us to write

W_{v}(a(y))=\chi(y)^{-1}|y|^{\frac{1}{2}}\underbrace{\int_{F}^{\textrm{st}}f_{v}\left(\left(\begin{matrix}0&1\\
-1&-x\end{matrix}\right)\right)\psi(yx)^{-1}dx}_{=I(v,y)}.

We expand the integral as

I(v,y)=I_{0}(v,y)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\chi(\varpi^{2n})I_{n}(v,y)

for

\displaystyle I_{0}(v,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{O}}f_{v}\left(\left(\begin{matrix}0&1\\
-1&-x\end{matrix}\right)\right)\psi(yx)^{-1}dx\text{ and }

\displaystyle I_{n}(v,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{O}^{\times}}\chi(x)^{2}f_{v}\left(\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\varpi^{n}x&1\end{matrix}\right)\right)\psi(yx^{-1}\varpi^{-n})^{-1}dx.

Here we have used the common identity

\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&-x\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-x^{-1}&1\\
0&-x\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
x^{-1}&1\end{pmatrix}.

At this point we will start to use the fact that we are dealing with specific vectors
v=v_{a}
. In this case
f_{v_{a}}=\pi(\gamma_{a})f_{\textrm{ML}}
is supported in
B(\mathcal{O})\gamma_{a}K(r)
. We start by computing
I_{0}(v_{a},y)
. To do so we observe that

f_{v_{a}}\left(\left(\begin{matrix}0&1\\
-1&-x\end{matrix}\right)\right)=f_{\textrm{ML}}\left(\left(\begin{matrix}0&1\\
-1&-x\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}0&-1\\
1&a\end{matrix}\right)\right)=f_{\textrm{ML}}\left(\left(\begin{matrix}1&a\\
-x&1-ax\end{matrix}\right)\right).

Note that on the support of
f_{\mathrm{ML}}
we have
x\in\mathfrak{p}^{r}
, so that in particular
1-ax\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
. We can write

f_{\textrm{ML}}\left(\left(\begin{matrix}1&a\\
-x&1-ax\end{matrix}\right)\right)=f_{\textrm{ML}}\left(\left(\begin{matrix}(1-ax)^{-1}&a\\
0&1-ax\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\frac{-x}{1-ax}&1\end{matrix}\right)\right)\\
=\chi(1-ax)^{-2}f_{\textrm{ML}}\left(\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\frac{-x}{1-ax}&1\end{matrix}\right)\right).

In particular, we find that

f_{\textrm{ML}}\left(\left(\begin{matrix}1&a\\
-x&1-ax\end{matrix}\right)\right)=\chi(1-ax)^{-2}\cdot\mathbb{1}_{\mathfrak{p}^{r}}(x).

We conclude that

\displaystyle I_{0}(v_{a},y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{p}^{r}}\chi(1-ax)^{-2}\psi(yx)^{-1}dx

\displaystyle=q^{-r}\int_{\mathcal{O}}\chi(1-ax\varpi^{r})^{-2}\psi(yx\varpi^{r})^{-1}dx

\displaystyle=q^{-r}\int_{\mathcal{O}}\psi(2b_{\chi}ax\varpi^{-r}-yx\varpi^{r})dx

\displaystyle=q^{-r}\mathbb{1}_{2b_{\chi}a\varpi^{-m}+\mathfrak{p}^{-r}}(y).

Finally, we observe that the integrals
I_{n}(v_{a},y)
must all vanish due to the support of
f_{v_{a}}
. Putting everything together yields

W_{v_{a}}(a(y))=q^{-r}\chi(y)^{-1}|y|^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{1}_{2b_{\chi}a\varpi^{-m}+\mathfrak{p}^{-r}}(y).

Using the definition of the inner product in the Kirillov model, one easily verifies that

\langle g_{a},g_{a}\rangle=\zeta_{q}(1)q^{-r}.

This gives the desired result.

We now turn towards
v=\widetilde{v}_{a}
. By an easy support argument, which uses
a\in\mathfrak{p}
, we see that
I_{0}(\widetilde{v}_{a},y)=0
. Thus, let us consider
I_{n}(\widetilde{v}_{a},y).
To do so we first compute

f_{\widetilde{v}_{a}}\left(\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\varpi^{n}x&1\end{matrix}\right)\right)=f_{\textrm{ML}}\left(\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
a+\varpi^{n}x&1\end{matrix}\right)\right)=\mathbb{1}_{\varpi^{-n}(-a+\mathfrak{p}^{r})}(x).

We put
i=v(a)
, write
a=a_{0}\varpi^{i}
and consider two cases. First suppose that
i<r
. Then
I_{n}(\widetilde{v}_{a},y)=0
unless
n=i
and we have

\displaystyle I_{i}(\widetilde{v}_{a},y)

\displaystyle=\int_{-a_{0}^{-1}+\mathfrak{p}^{r-i}}\chi(x)^{-2}\psi(yx\varpi^{-i})^{-1}dx

\displaystyle=\chi(a_{0})^{2}\int_{1+\mathfrak{p}^{r-i}}\chi(x)^{-2}\psi(a_{0}^{-1}yx\varpi^{-i})dx

This integral is sometimes referred to as incomplete Gauß sum and is well understood. See
[
Ass19a
, Remark 5.9]
for the correct size and
[
Ass19
, Remark 3.3.7]
for a precise evaluation. Indeed, we have

I_{i}(\widetilde{v}_{a},y)=\epsilon(\frac{1}{2},\chi^{2})\chi(ya_{0}^{-1}\varpi^{i})^{2}q^{-\frac{m}{2}}\mathbb{1}_{(2b_{\chi}+\mathfrak{p}^{r-i})\varpi^{i-m}}(a_{0}y).

We conclude that

W_{\widetilde{v}_{a}}(a(y))=\epsilon(\frac{1}{2},\chi^{2})\chi(y\varpi^{2i})q^{-\frac{m}{2}}\mathbb{1}_{(2a_{0}b_{\chi}+\mathfrak{p}^{r-i})\varpi^{i-m}}(y).

Checking the normalisation is now straight forward.

Finally, suppose
a\in\mathfrak{p}^{r}
. In this case we see that
I_{n}(\widetilde{v}_{a},y)=0
unless
n\geq r
. In the latter case we have

\displaystyle I_{n}(\widetilde{v}_{a},y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{O}^{\times}}\chi(x)^{-2}\psi(-yx\varpi^{-n})dx

\displaystyle=q^{-\frac{m}{2}}\epsilon(\frac{1}{2},\chi^{2})\chi^{2}(y\varpi^{-n})\mathbb{1}_{\varpi^{n-m}\mathcal{O}^{\times}}(y),

We conclude that

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{a}(y)

\displaystyle=\chi(y)^{-1}|y|^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{n\geq r}\chi(\varpi^{2n})I_{n}(\widetilde{v}_{a},y)

\displaystyle=\epsilon(\frac{1}{2},\chi^{2})\chi(y)|y|^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{-\frac{m}{2}}\sum_{n\geq r}\mathbb{1}_{\varpi^{n-m}\mathcal{O}^{\times}}(y)=\epsilon(\frac{1}{2},\chi^{2})\chi(y)|y|^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{-\frac{m}{2}}\mathbb{1}_{\mathfrak{p}^{-r}}(y).

The
L^{2}
-normalization is now easily computed.
∎

Remark 4.31
.

One can compute
g_{a}
following
[
Nel18
]
using the non-equivariant twisting isomorphism. The relevant formulae for the corresponding new-vector translates are well known in this case.

We can now compute the corresponding decomposition of the generalised new-vector
v^{\circ}
.
This then gives clear candidates for which localised vectors to use in our treatment of the sup-norm problem.
We recall the sketch in the archimedean case given in Section
2.1
and Section
2.2
, where we want a certain period of the chosen vector to be large.
We will apply an analogous idea at the place
p
.
In this case, the period is given by an integral over
K_{H}(m,m)
, which translates to the inner product with the new-vector
v^{\circ}
(see the next section).
Though this is more complicated in the archimedean world (due to approximations), in our
p
-adic setting we can directly compute inner products of localised vectors with the new-vector using the following orthogonal decomposition.

Corollary 4.32
.

Define the matrix

(4.20)

c_{t}=\left(\begin{matrix}b_{\chi}/t&-\frac{1}{2}\\
1&\frac{t}{2b_{\chi}}\end{matrix}\right).

Then we have

\frac{v^{\circ}}{\|v^{\circ}\|}=\sum_{t\in(\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}^{r})^{\times}}\frac{a_{t}}{\|v_{\mathrm{ML}}\|}\cdot\pi(c_{t})v_{\mathrm{ML}},

with

a_{t}=\zeta_{q}(1)^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{-r/2}\chi(t\varpi^{-m})\psi(-b_{\chi}\varpi^{-m}).

Proof.

We compute in the Kirillov model. To do so we let
w_{t}\in\mathcal{K}(\pi,\psi)
be the function associated to the vector
\frac{\pi(c_{t})v_{\mathrm{ML}}}{\|g_{\textrm{ML}}\|}.
Note that

c_{t}=n(b_{\chi}/t)\cdot\gamma_{\frac{t}{2b_{\chi}}}.

In particular, we have

\displaystyle w_{t}(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\|g_{\frac{t}{2b_{\chi}}}\|}\cdot[n(b_{\chi}/t).g_{\frac{t}{2b_{\chi}}}](y)=\psi(yb_{\chi}/t)\frac{g_{\frac{t}{2b_{\chi}}}(y)}{\|g_{\frac{t}{2b_{\chi}}}\|}

\displaystyle=\zeta_{q}(1)^{-\frac{1}{2}}q^{-r/2}\psi(yb_{\chi}/t)\chi(y)^{-1}|y|^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{1}_{t\varpi^{-m}+\mathfrak{p}^{-r}}(y)

We can modify
b_{\chi}\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
if necessary so that

\chi(1+z\varpi^{r})=\psi(b_{\chi}z\varpi^{-r})\text{ for }z\in\mathcal{O}.

We observe that

\displaystyle w_{t}(t(1+z\varpi^{r})\varpi^{-m})

\displaystyle=\zeta_{q}(1)^{-\frac{1}{2}}q^{r/2}\chi(t\varpi^{-m})^{-1}\chi(1+z\varpi^{r})^{-1}\psi(\varpi^{-m}(1+z\varpi^{r})b_{\chi})

\displaystyle=\zeta_{q}(1)^{-\frac{1}{2}}q^{r/2}\chi(t\varpi^{-m})^{-1}\psi(b_{\chi}\varpi^{-m}).

We can now compute the inner product between
g^{\circ}
and
w_{t}
:

\displaystyle\langle g^{\circ},w_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=\zeta_{q}(1)\int_{\varpi^{-m}\mathcal{O}^{\times}}\overline{w_{t}(y)}\frac{dy}{|y|}

\displaystyle=\zeta_{q}(1)\int_{1+\mathfrak{p}^{r}}\overline{w_{t}(t\varpi^{-m}y)}dy

\displaystyle=\zeta_{q}(1)q^{-r}\int_{\mathcal{O}}\overline{w_{t}(t(1+z\varpi^{r})\varpi^{-m})}dz

\displaystyle=\zeta_{q}(1)^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{-r/2}\chi(t\varpi^{-m})\psi(-b_{\chi}\varpi^{-m})=a_{t}.

This gives us the desired coefficients. Finally, we compute

\sum_{t\in(\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}^{r})^{\times}}|a_{t}|^{2}=1.

Since the supports of the
w_{t}
’s are disjoint, they are orthogonal, and we are done.
∎

We end our discussion of microlocal lift vectors by making two remarks that will turn out to be useful later on.

Remark 4.33
.

Lemma
4.28
shows that the matrix coefficient of
v_{\mathrm{ML}}
localises around the torus
D(F)
.
It is easy now to compute that the matrix coefficient of
\pi(c_{1})v_{\mathrm{ML}}
localises around

(4.21)

c_{1}D(F)c_{1}^{-1}=\left\{\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha+\beta&b_{\chi}(\alpha-\beta)\\
b_{\chi}^{-1}(\alpha-\beta)&\alpha+\beta\end{pmatrix}:\alpha,\beta\in F^{\times}\right\}

Remark 4.34
.

It was shown in
[
Sah17
, Lemma 2.17]
(see also
[
Mar16
]
) that the
K_{0}
-representation generated by
f_{\circ}
is irreducible. It turns out that the standard microlocal lift vector
v_{\mathrm{ML}}
generates the same representation. In the induced model we can write this representation down explicitly:

V_{\textrm{new}}=\{f\in I(\chi_{B})\colon f(kc)=f(k)\text{ for }k\in K_{0}\text{ and }c\in K(m)\}.

In particular, the dimension of this space is given by the cardinality of
B(\mathcal{O})\backslash K_{0}/K(m)
. Computing this cardinality reduces to computing
B(\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}^{m})\backslash G(\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}^{m})
. Since
\sharp G(\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}^{m})=q^{4m}(1-q^{-1})(1-q^{-2})
and
\sharp B(\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}^{m})=q^{3m}(1-q^{-1})^{2}
we conclude that

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V_{\textrm{new}}=\sharp B(\mathcal{O})\backslash K_{0}/K(m)=\sharp B(\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}^{m})\backslash G(\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}^{m})=q^{m}(1+1/q).

This is in line with the proof of Proposition 2.13 for non-supercuspidal representations in
[
Sah17
]
.

4.5
An example of a local period

We will now probe how the microlocalised vectors from our examples interact with vectors fixed under the (generalised) Hecke congruence subgroups
K_{H}(m,m)
, i.e. generalised new-vectors as in Convention
4.1
. To capture this, we define
17
17

17

The notational clash with the archimedean section is intentional, and it is resolved in the next global section.

(4.22)

Q(v)=\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{H}(m,m))}\int_{K_{H}(m,m)}\langle\pi(k)v,v\rangle_{\pi}\,dk.

Given an irreducible unitary smooth representation
\pi
, we write
\mathcal{P}_{m,m}
for the orthogonal projection on
\pi^{K_{H}(m,m)}
.

First, we observe that

(4.23)

Q(v)=\langle\mathcal{P}_{m,m}(v),v\rangle=\|\mathcal{P}_{m,m}(v)\|^{2}.

If we further assume that
\dim\pi^{K_{H}(m,m)}=1
, then we can evaluate
Q(v)
as follows. Let
v^{\circ}\in\pi^{K_{H}(m,m)}
be the unit vector, so that the orthogonal projection
\mathcal{P}_{m,m}\colon\pi\to\pi
is given by

\mathcal{P}_{m,m}(v)=\langle v,v^{\circ}\rangle\cdot v^{\circ}.

We can now write

Q(v)=\langle\mathcal{P}_{m,m}(v),v\rangle=|\langle v,v^{\circ}\rangle|^{2}.

In particular, if
v^{\circ}=a\cdot v+v^{\bot}
with
\langle v,v^{\bot}\rangle=0
, then

Q(v)=|a|^{2}.

One of the main conclusions of our computations in the previous sections is the following evaluation of
Q
at microlocalised vectors appearing in the decomposition of the new-vector.
These are the vectors for which
Q
is large, at least among the class of microlocalised vectors.

Lemma 4.35
.

If
\pi
is a smooth unitary irreducible representation of
G(F)
with trivial central character and exponent conductor
a(\pi)=4r
, then we have

Q(v)=\zeta_{q}(1)q^{-r},

where
v=v_{\mathrm{MV}}/\|v_{\mathrm{MV}}\|
is the standard minimal vector if
\pi
is supercuspidal and
v=\pi(c_{t})v_{\mathrm{ML}}/\|v_{\mathrm{ML}}\|
with
t\in(\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}^{r})^{\times}
if
\pi
is principal series.

Proof.

This follows directly from the discussion above using Corollary
4.20
if
\pi
is supercuspidal and Corollary
4.32
if
\pi
is principal series.
∎

Heuristically one can explain the size of
Q(v)
as in the archimedean case. Indeed, in analogy to the reasoning from Section
2.4
, one finds that

Q(v)\approx\frac{\operatorname{vol}(J\cap K_{H}(m,m))}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{H}(m,m))}\approx q^{-r}

in the supercuspidal case, for example. Here
J
is the group carrying the support of the matrix coefficient (see Lemma
4.18
).

4.6
A non-archimedean volume estimate

In this section we prove non-archimedean volume estimates similar to those in Theorem
3.7
.
The test function we consider is a truncated matrix coefficient of a microlocalised vector with large period
Q
, as in Lemma
4.35
.
By Lemma
4.18
and Lemma
4.28
, these functions localise around a torus
T
.
Thus, such a test function takes the desired form (
2.7
) mentioned in the introduction.

In the case of supercuspidals, we take the minimal vector
v_{\mathrm{MV}}
, for which
T
is the torus
E^{\times}
, where
E/F
is an unramified extension.
In the case of principal series, we consider the vector
\pi(c_{1})v_{\mathrm{ML}}
, with
c_{1}
defined in Corollary
4.32
, for which
T
is a conjugate of the diagonal torus given in (
4.21
).

More precisely, let
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
\pi
be an irreducible representation of conductor
a(\pi)=4n
and trivial central character.
Write
H(2n)=K_{H}(2n,2n)
for simplicity.
We fix the
L^{2}
-normalized vector
v\in\pi
to be
v=\frac{\pi(c_{1})v_{\mathrm{ML}}}{\|v_{\mathrm{ML}}\|}
if
\pi
is principal series and
v=\frac{v_{\mathrm{MV}}}{\|v_{\mathrm{MV}}\|}
if
\pi
is supercuspidal.
Our goal is to understand the integral

(4.24)

I_{\gamma}(f\ast f)=\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}(H(2n))^{2}}\int_{H(2n)}\int_{H(2n)}[f\ast f](x^{-1}\gamma y)dxdy,

for

f(g)=\dim(V_{\textrm{new}})\cdot\langle v,\pi(g)v\rangle\cdot\mathbb{1}_{K_{0}}(g).

Note that, by Lemma
4.8
, this function satisfies
f\ast f=f
.

For
\gamma\in H(2n)
, this integral
I_{\gamma}(f)
is easily evaluated. Indeed, after a simple change of variables we have

(4.25)

I_{\gamma}(f)=\operatorname{vol}(H(2n))^{-1}\cdot\int_{H(2n)}f(x)dx\\
=\dim(V_{\textrm{new}})\langle v,\mathcal{P}_{2n,2n}(v)\rangle=\dim(V_{\textrm{new}})\cdot Q(v).

Recalling Lemma
4.35
, Remark
4.21
, and Remark
4.34
, we record the estimate

(4.26)

I_{\gamma}(f\ast f)\ll q^{n}\text{ for }\gamma\in H(2n),

which is essentially sharp.

For general
\gamma
, we will ignore any oscillation of the matrix coefficients
f
and simply bound

I_{\gamma}(f\ast f)\ll I_{\gamma}(|f|).

According to the support properties of the matrix coefficients
f
we have

|f|\ll\operatorname{vol}(\mathcal{U})^{-1}\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{U}},

where
\mathcal{U}=T(\mathcal{O})\cdot K(n)
and
T(\mathcal{O})=T\cap G(\mathcal{O})
.

We follow Section
2.8
, where all steps are directly applicable and no approximations are involved.
Thus, we perform the
x
-integral for each
y
such that
\gamma y\in H(2n)\mathcal{U}
and obtain that

(4.27)

\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}(H(2n))^{2}}\int_{H(2n)}\int_{H(2n)}|f|(x^{-1}\gamma y)\,dx\,dy\\
\ll\frac{\operatorname{vol}\{y\in H(2n)\mid\gamma y\in H(2n)\mathcal{U}\}}{\operatorname{vol}(H(2n))}\cdot\frac{\operatorname{vol}(\mathcal{U}\cap H(2n))}{\operatorname{vol}(H(2n))\operatorname{vol}(\mathcal{U})}.

In this section we will prove a bound for the volume

V_{\gamma}(\mathcal{U})=\operatorname{vol}\{y\in H(2n)\mid\gamma y\in H(2n)\mathcal{U}\}.

Remark 4.36
.

Note that if
\gamma\in H(2n)K(n)
, then we have

V_{\gamma}(\mathcal{U})=\operatorname{vol}(H(2n)).

Indeed, here we can reduce to the case that
\gamma=1
, since
K(n)
is normal and contained in
\mathcal{U}
, so
\gamma y\in H(2n)\mathcal{U}
for all
y\in H(2n)
. We obtain the bound
18
18

18

We use that
\operatorname{vol}(H(2n))\asymp q^{-4n}
,
\operatorname{vol}(\mathcal{U})\asymp q^{-2n}
and
\operatorname{vol}(\mathcal{U}\cap H(2n))\asymp q^{-5n}
.

I_{\gamma}(f\ast f)\ll\frac{\operatorname{vol}(\mathcal{U}\cap H(2n))}{\operatorname{vol}(H(2n))\operatorname{vol}(\mathcal{U})}\ll q^{n},\text{ for }\gamma\in H(2n)K(n).

For
\gamma\in H(2n)
we recover (
4.26
), but it is valid in a larger region.

4.6.1
Distances

The goal is to bound the volumes above in terms of a notion of distance between
\gamma
and
H(2n)
.

Definition 4.6
.

For a subgroup
H\leq K_{0}
and
g\in K_{0}
, we set
d_{H}(g)=0
if
g\in H
. Otherwise, we define
d_{H}(g)=q^{-l}
for
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that
g\in H\cdot K(l)\setminus H\cdot K(l+1)
.

As in Section
2.8
, we may reduce
\gamma
to a certain shape, although we do not have a Cartan decomposition.
We always assume
\gamma\in K_{0}
and write
\gamma=(\gamma_{ij})
. We start with the case where
\gamma_{22}\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
. Since
H(2n)
contains
D(\mathcal{O})
, we can reduce to the case that
\gamma_{22}=1
. Observe also that
d_{H(2n)}(\gamma)=q^{-l}
implies that at least
\gamma_{12}
or
\gamma_{21}
has valuation precisely
l
.

Remark 4.37
.

If we assume that
d_{H(2n)}(\gamma)<1
, so that
\gamma\in K(1)\cdot H(2n)
, then we always have
\gamma_{11},\gamma_{22}\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
. This is due to the invertibility of the determinant.

Recall that the cases where
\gamma\in H(2n)K(n)
are handled in Remark
4.36
. Thus, we assume from now on that
\gamma\in H(2n)K(l)\setminus H(2n)K(l+1)
for
l<n
.

Remark 4.38
.

The reduction
\gamma_{22}=1
only works if
\gamma_{22}\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
. If this is not the case, then we must have
d_{H(2n)}(\gamma)=1
. Furthermore, we also deduce that

Note that if
\gamma_{22}\in\mathfrak{p}
and
\gamma\in K_{0}
, then we must have
d_{H(2n)}(\gamma)=1
. In particular,
\gamma_{12},\gamma_{21}\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
. We write
\gamma=w\cdot\gamma^{\prime}
and, since
w
normalises
H(2n)
, we observe that

V_{\gamma}(\mathcal{U})=\{y\in H(2n)\mid\gamma^{\prime}y\in H(2n)w\mathcal{U}\}.

At this point, we observe that
H(2n)w\mathcal{U}=H(2n)\mathcal{U}
.
19
19

19

For example if
\mathcal{U}
is the thickening of the standard torus conjugated by
c_{1}
, then we simply compute

wc_{1}=\left(\begin{matrix}1&1/2b_{\chi}\\
-b_{\chi}&\frac{1}{2}\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}-b_{\chi}^{-1}&0\\
0&b_{\chi}\end{matrix}\right)c_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right).

We conclude that

V_{\gamma}(\mathcal{U})=V_{\gamma^{\prime}}(\mathcal{U})

in this case.

In particular, for
\gamma=w
, we have

V_{w}(\mathcal{U})=V_{1}(\mathcal{U})=\operatorname{vol}(H(2n)).

This is larger than what we expect, given that
d(\gamma)=1
. This phenomenon is related to the failure of transversality for rank two as described in
[
HN23
, Remark 5.2]
.

4.6.2
Further reductions

Since
K(n)\subset\mathcal{U}
, we can reduce the computation of
V_{\gamma}(\mathcal{U})
modulo
\mathfrak{p}^{n}
, as is done in
[
HN23
, Sec. 9.4]
.
More precisely, we consider cosets
H(2n)/H(2n)\cap K(n)
, which can be represented by diagonal matrices with elements in
\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}^{n}
.
Indeed, this follows by a computation similar to (
4.10
).
Of course, we will always pick up the relevant volume in the central direction, which is contained in
D
.
We then have

(4.28)

V_{\gamma}(\mathcal{U})=\operatorname{vol}(H(2n)\cap K(n))\cdot\sharp(\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}^{n})^{\times}\\
\cdot\sharp\{\alpha\in(\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}^{n})^{\times}\mid\gamma\operatorname{diag}(\alpha,1)\in D(\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}^{n})T\}.

Assume now that

\gamma\operatorname{diag}(\alpha,1)=d\cdot t,

with
d\in D(\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}^{n})
and
t\in T
.
Again, since
T
contains
Z(\mathcal{O})
, we may assume that
d=\operatorname{diag}(\beta,1)
.

Recall that in both cases, supercuspidal and principal series, the torus element
t
can be written as

t=\begin{pmatrix}a&u_{1}b\\
u_{2}b&a\end{pmatrix},

where
a,b\in\mathcal{O}
and
u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
.
More precisely, for the minimal vector
u_{1}=1
and
u_{2}=D
, whereas for the shifted microlocal lift vector
u_{1}=\beta_{\chi}
and
u_{2}=\beta_{\chi}^{-1}
.

4.6.3
Polynomials with large coefficients and bounds

To bound the last factor in the (
4.28
) using the distance function, we reduce to counting solutions to polynomials with
p
-adically large coefficients.

Note now that our assumptions (i.e.
\gamma_{22}=1
) imply that
a=1
by looking at the lower-right entry in the matrix equation.
Explicitly, we have the condition

\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\gamma_{11}&\gamma_{12}\\
\alpha\gamma_{21}&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\beta&\beta u_{1}b\\
u_{2}b&1\end{pmatrix},

modulo
\mathfrak{p}^{n}
.
Observe that, since
\beta,u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
, we must have
\nu(\gamma_{12})=\nu(\gamma_{21})=\nu(b)
.
In particular, if
d_{H(2n)}(\gamma)=q^{-l}
, then
\nu(\gamma_{12})=\nu(\gamma_{21})=l
.
Let us also assume that
\gamma_{11}\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
. (This happens for example if
d_{H(2n)}(\gamma)<1
.)

By computing the ratio of elements on the first row, we obtain the conditions

\frac{\beta u_{1}b}{\beta}=u_{1}b=\frac{\gamma_{12}}{\alpha\gamma_{11}}\quad\text{and}\quad u_{2}b=\alpha\gamma_{21}.

Putting these together,
\alpha
must satisfy

\gamma_{21}\alpha^{2}-c\equiv 0\,(\text{mod }\mathfrak{p}^{n}),

for some
c\in\mathcal{O}
.
Assuming that
\alpha
is close to the identity, we can produce a non-trivial bound from this condition as in
[
Mar23
, Lemma 6.4]
.

Lemma 4.39
.

The number of
\alpha=1+z\in(\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}^{n})^{\times}
with
z\in\mathfrak{p}
satisfying
c_{2}\alpha^{2}-c_{0}\equiv 0
with
\nu(c_{2})=l\leq n
and
c_{0}\in\mathcal{O}
is bounded by
q^{(n+l)/2}
.

Proof.

The polynomial equation can be rewritten as
c_{2}z^{2}+c_{1}z+c_{0}=0
with
c_{1}\in\mathcal{O}
and same conditions.
Assuming the existence of any solution, we can shift
z
by that solution and reduce to
the case where
c_{0}=0
.
Furthermore, if
\nu(c_{1})\leq l
, then
\nu(c_{2}z^{2}+c_{1}z)=\nu(c_{1}z)\geq n
implies that
\nu(z)\geq n-l
.
Otherwise, we have that

\nu(c_{2}z(z+c_{1}/c_{2}))\geq n

and, putting
\nu(c_{2})=l
on the other side, this implies that
\nu(z)\geq(n-l)/2
or
\nu(z+c_{1}/c_{2})\geq(n-l)/2
.
The number of such
z
is bounded in all cases by
q^{n-(n-l)/2}=q^{(n+l)/2}
.
∎

Recalling the definition of
d_{H(2n)}(\gamma)
, the previous lemma implies that

(4.29)

\sharp\{\alpha\in(\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}^{n})^{\times}\mid\gamma\operatorname{diag}(\alpha,1)\in D(\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}^{n})T\}\leq q\cdot\frac{q^{n/2}}{d_{H(2n)}(\gamma)^{1/2}}

for
d_{H(2n)}(\gamma)\geq q^{-n}
and
\gamma_{11},\gamma_{22}\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
. The factor of
q
accounts for the assumption that
\alpha
is close to the identity, i.e. reducing to
\alpha\equiv 1
modulo
\mathfrak{p}
.

4.6.4
The main volume bound

The results of this subsection imply the following important result.

Theorem 4.40
.

For
\gamma\in K_{0}
we have

V_{\gamma}(\mathcal{U})\ll\operatorname{vol}(H(2n))\cdot q\max_{\gamma^{\prime}\in\{\gamma,w\gamma\}}\min\left(\frac{q^{-n/2}}{d_{H(2n)}(\gamma^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{2}}},1\right).

Proof.

First, we note that if
d_{H(2n)}(\gamma)<q^{-n}
, then we have

V_{\gamma}(\mathcal{U})=\operatorname{vol}(H(2n))

by Remark
4.36
. Thus, we assume
d_{H(2n)}(\gamma)\geq q^{-n}
. Using (
4.28
) with
\operatorname{vol}(H(2n)\cap K(n))\ll\operatorname{vol}(H(2n))q^{-2n}
and
\sharp(\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}^{n})\ll q^{n}
yields

V_{\gamma}(\mathcal{U})\ll\operatorname{vol}(H(2n))q^{-n}\cdot\sharp\{\alpha\in(\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}^{n})^{\times}\mid\gamma\operatorname{diag}(\alpha,1)\in D(\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}^{n})T\}.

If
\gamma_{11},\gamma_{22}\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
, then we can apply (
4.29
) and obtain

V_{\gamma}(\mathcal{U})\ll q^{1-n}\cdot\operatorname{vol}(H(2n))\frac{q^{n/2}}{d_{H(2n)}(\gamma)^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

(At
d_{H(n)}(\gamma)=q^{-n}
this agrees with the trivial bound up to a factor of
q
and it is strictly worse from then on.)

Finally, if
\gamma_{11}\in\mathfrak{p}
or
\gamma_{22}\in\mathfrak{p}
, then we have
\gamma_{12},\gamma_{21}\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
. Thus, we can apply the argument above to
\gamma^{\prime}=w\gamma
. As in Remark
4.38
we obtain

V_{\gamma}(\mathcal{U})=V_{\gamma^{\prime}}(\mathcal{U})\ll q\cdot\operatorname{vol}(H(2n))\min\left(\frac{q^{-n/2}}{d_{H(2n)}(\gamma^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{2}}},1\right).

This completes the proof.
∎

We obtain the following important result concerning the size of our integral:

Corollary 4.41
.

We have

I_{\gamma}(|f|)\ll\frac{q^{1-n}}{\operatorname{vol}(\mathcal{U})}\max_{\gamma^{\prime}\in\{\gamma,w\gamma\}}\min\left(\frac{q^{-n/2}}{d_{H(2n)}(\gamma^{\prime})^{1/2}},1\right).

Proof.

This follows directly from the previous theorem and (
4.27
).
∎

4.6.5
On the relation to matrix coefficients

In this subsection, we will discuss how much information we have lost when trivially estimating
I_{\gamma}(f)\ll I_{\gamma}(|f|)
. To do so, we will make a connection to estimates for matrix coefficients of new-vectors.

Recall that
\mathcal{P}_{2n,2n}
is the projection on the (normalised) generalised new-vector in
\pi
as above. We compute

\displaystyle I_{\gamma}(f\ast f)=\dim(V_{\textrm{new}})\langle\mathcal{P}_{2n,2n}(v),\pi(\gamma)\mathcal{P}_{2n,2n}(v)\rangle,

for
\gamma\in K_{0}
. We further have

\langle\mathcal{P}_{2n,2n}(v),\pi(\gamma)\mathcal{P}_{2n,2n}(v)\rangle=Q(v)\langle v^{\circ},\pi(\gamma)v^{\circ}\rangle,

where
v^{\circ}
is as in Convention
4.1
. After recalling that
\dim(V_{\textrm{new}})\asymp\operatorname{vol}(\mathcal{U})^{-1}
and
Q(v)=\zeta_{q}(1)q^{-n}
we obtain

(4.30)

\langle v^{\circ},\pi(\gamma)v^{\circ}\rangle\ll q\max_{\gamma^{\prime}\in\{\gamma,w\gamma\}}\min\left(\frac{q^{-n/2}}{d_{H(2n)}(\gamma^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{2}}},1\right).

Note that, by Cauchy–Schwarz, the trivial bound for the matrix coefficient is
1
.
Thus, up to the factor
q
our bound is always as least as good as the trivial bound.

Our method is quite soft and reduces essentially to a counting problem.
More elaborate methods, more precisely the method of stationary phase applied to suitable integral representations of matrix coefficients, were applied in
[
HS20
]
.
In
[
HS20
, Proposition 5.2]
the following bounds are established:

20
20

20

Since we are assuming
a(\pi)=4n
we have
n_{1}=2n
and because we are working directly with the normalized generalised new-vector (i.e. the unique vector fixed by
K_{H}(2n,2n)
) our vector
v^{\circ}
corresponds to
v_{\pi}^{\prime}/\|v_{\pi}\|
in
[
HS20
]
.
Also note that our subgroup
H(2n)
is denoted by
K^{\ast}(2n)
in loc.cit.
With these translations made we have
\Phi^{\prime}_{\pi}(g)=\langle v^{\circ},\pi(g)v^{\circ}\rangle
.

(4.31)

\langle v^{\circ},\pi(\gamma)v^{\circ}\rangle\ll q^{\frac{j-2n}{2}+O(1)},\text{ for }\gamma\in H(j)\setminus H(j+1)\text{ with }1\leq j\leq 2n.

After recalling the definition of
d_{H(2n)}(\gamma)
, we observe that

d_{H(2n)}(\gamma)=q^{-l}\text{ if and only if }\gamma\in H(l)\setminus H(l+1),

for
0<l<2n
. Up to a term
q^{O(1)}
, these bounds are sharp and they are superior to our volume bounds. A full comparison is displayed in Table
1
.

l

l=\infty

n\leq l\leq 2n

0<l<n

l=0

A_{\textrm{Vol}}(\gamma)

1

1

\frac{l-n}{2}

\frac{l^{\prime}-n}{2}

A_{\textrm{Hu-Sa}}(\gamma)

1

\frac{l}{2}-n

\frac{l}{2}-n

-

Table 1:
Bounds for matrix coefficients:
Here we have written
d_{H(2n)}(\gamma)=q^{-l}
and
l^{\prime}=\max\left(-\log(d_{H(2n)}(w\gamma))/\log(q),1\right)
.
Then we can write the bound for the matrix coefficient as
\langle v^{\circ},\pi(\gamma)v^{\circ}\rangle\ll q^{-A_{\ast}(\gamma)+O(1)},

where
A_{\textrm{Vol}}(\gamma)
is the exponent derived from (
4.30
) and
A_{\textrm{Hu-Sa}}(\gamma)
is the exponent coming from (
4.31
).

5
Global framework

So far we have abstractly discussed aspects of
microlocalisation
and the
quantitative orbit method
. We have done so in the realm of the local representation theory of
\operatorname{PGL}(2)
over
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. We will now switch gears and turn towards the global setting. Our goal for the rest of this text is to showcase how the tools developed earlier can be applied to concrete problems in the analytic theory of automorphic forms. More precisely, the application we have in mind is the
sup-norm problem
for Hecke–Maaß newforms and we will ultimately prove in Section
8
below the new bound stated in Theorem
1.1
. This requires some preparations.

Recall that we are ultimately interested in bounding classical Hecke–Maaß newforms
f\colon\Gamma_{H}(N)\backslash\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{C}
, for
N=p^{4n}
. In order to effectively piece together the local theory developed above, we work in the adelic framework. In this section, we introduce the necessary terminology. Most importantly, we will define an adelic automorphic form
\varphi^{\prime}
, see (
5.1
). This form is related to the classical form
f
, and in particular bounding the sup-norm of
\varphi^{\prime}
is equivalent to bounding the sup-norm of
f
.

5.1
Adelic groups and measures

We let
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{A}^{\times}
denote the adeles and ideles, respectively, of
\mathbb{Q}
.
The adeles come equipped with the modulus

|\cdot|_{\mathbb{A}}\colon\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},\quad(x_{\infty},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots)\mapsto|x_{\infty}|_{\infty}\cdot\prod_{p}|x_{p}|_{p}.

We embed
\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathbb{Q}^{\times}
diagonally into
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{A}^{\times}
, respectively.
The absolute values on
\mathbb{Q}_{\infty}=\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
are then normalised so that
|q|_{\mathbb{A}}=1
for all
q\in\mathbb{Q}
.
This matches the normalisation in Section
4
where we take
F=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and
\varpi=p
.

If
\psi_{p}
is the standard additive character of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and
\psi_{\infty}(x)=\exp(2\pi ix)
, then we set

\psi_{\mathbb{A}}(x)=\psi_{\infty}(x_{\infty})\cdot\prod_{p}\psi_{p}(x_{p}).

Again we note that
\psi_{\mathbb{A}}(q)=1
for
q\in\mathbb{Q}
, so that we can view
\psi_{\mathbb{A}}
as a character on
\mathbb{Q}\backslash\mathbb{A}
.

Finally, recall the notation

\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}=\prod_{p}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}=\prod_{p}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}.

We now turn towards global aspects of the general linear group. At the archimedean place, we define

G_{\infty}=\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R}),\quad G_{\infty}^{\prime}=\operatorname{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{R}),\quad G_{\infty}^{+}=\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R}).

Similarly, we put

G_{p}=\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}).

All other subgroups from previous sections, such as
N,D,B
and
Z
, are analogously equipped with subscripts to indicate the field over which consider them.

We set
K_{\infty}=\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
K_{\infty}^{\prime}=\textrm{PSO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
K_{p}=\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
.
Put these together to we obtain a compact subgroup

K=K_{\infty}\times\prod_{p}K_{p}.

Recall that we equipped the local groups
K_{\infty}
and
K_{p}
with appropriately normalised Haar measures, so that we can define the product measure
\mu_{K}
on
K
.
In our normalisation we have
\operatorname{vol}(K)=2\pi
. Let

N=p^{4n}\text{ and }N_{0}=p^{2n},\text{ for }n\in\mathbb{N}\text{ and }p\text{ odd prime.}

We will encounter the subgroup

K^{\ast}(N_{0})=K_{\infty}\times K_{H,p}(2n,2n)\times\prod_{l\neq p}K_{l},

where
K_{H,p}(m,m^{\prime})
denotes the subgroup of
K_{p}
defined in (
4.3
) with
F=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. It is easy to see that

\displaystyle\operatorname{vol}(K^{\ast}(N_{0}))

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi}{[K\colon K^{\ast}(N_{0})]}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi}{N(1+p^{-1})}=2\pi N^{-1+o(1)}.

By
K_{\textrm{fin}}
and
K^{\ast}(N_{0})_{\textrm{fin}}
we denote the finite parts of these groups.

The adele group
G(\mathbb{A})
is now defined as the restricted product (with respect to
\{K_{p}\}_{p}
)

G(\mathbb{A})=G_{\infty}\times{\prod_{p}}^{\prime}G_{p}.

The adelic points of all other groups mentioned are defined analogously.
In particular, we have

N(\mathbb{A})\cong\mathbb{A},\quad D(\mathbb{A})\cong(\mathbb{A}^{\times})^{2},\quad Z(\mathbb{A})\cong\mathbb{A}^{\times}.

We identify
Z(\mathbb{A})\backslash D(\mathbb{A})
with

\{a(y)=\operatorname{diag}(y,1)\colon y\in\mathbb{A}^{\times}\}\cong\mathbb{A}^{\times}.

These groups can thus be endowed with the measures coming from
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{A}^{\times}
and
(\mathbb{A}^{\times})^{2}
, respectively.
By construction, these agree with the measures that are inherited from the restricted product structure. Similarly, the local Haar measures define product measures on
G(\mathbb{A})
and
G(\mathbb{A})
, normalised so that

\int_{G(\mathbb{A})}f(g)\,dg=\int_{Z(\mathbb{A})}\int_{\mathbb{A}}\int_{\mathbb{A}^{\times}}\int_{K}f(zn(x)a(y)k)\,dk\,\frac{dy}{|y|_{\mathbb{A}}^{2}}\,dx\,dz.

5.1.1
Adelic quotients

We recall the approximation theorems

\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{R}\times\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}+\mathbb{Q}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{A}^{\times}=\mathbb{Q}^{\times}\cdot\mathbb{R}^{\times}\cdot\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}.

Note that this allows us to compute that

\operatorname{vol}(\mathbb{Q}\backslash\mathbb{A})=\operatorname{vol}([0,1]\times\widehat{\mathbb{Z}})=1.

The strong approximation theorem (see
[
Bum97
, Theorem 3.3.1]
) for
\operatorname{GL}(2)
implies that

G(\mathbb{A})=G(\mathbb{Q})\cdot G_{\infty}^{+}\cdot K^{\ast}(N_{0})_{\textrm{fin}},

using the fact that the determinant from
K^{\ast}(N_{0})_{\textrm{fin}}
to
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
is surjective.
We can check that

G(\mathbb{Q})\cap G_{\infty}^{+}\cdot K^{\ast}(N_{0})=\Gamma_{H}(N_{0},N_{0}),

where

\Gamma_{H}(N_{1},N_{0})=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbb{Z}&N_{0}\mathbb{Z}\\
N_{1}\mathbb{Z}&\mathbb{Z}\end{matrix}\right]\cap\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}).

We therefore find that the quotient

X_{N_{0}}=Z(\mathbb{A})G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A})/K^{\ast}(N_{0})

is isomorphic to
\Gamma_{H}(N_{0},N_{0})\backslash\mathbb{H}
. Finally, we write

[G]=Z(\mathbb{A})G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}).

5.2
Automorphic forms and representations

A cuspidal Hecke–Maaß newform
\varphi
on
\Gamma_{H}(N)\backslash\mathbb{H}
can be translated to be
\Gamma_{H}(N_{0},N_{0})
invariant and then be interpreted as a function on the adelic quotient
X_{N_{0}}
. This is due to the isomorphism with
\Gamma_{H}(N_{0},N_{0})\backslash\mathbb{H}
.
As in Chapter 5, Part C, of
[
Gel75
]
, or Section 3 of
[
Cas73
]
, or Section 3.6 of
[
Bum97
]
, it generates an irreducible automorphic representation, say,
\pi\subset L^{2}([G])
.

The representation
\pi
is isomorphic to
\pi_{\infty}\otimes\bigotimes_{l}\pi_{l}
and
\pi_{\infty}\cong\mathcal{P}(s)
, where
\frac{1}{4}-s^{2}
is the Laplace eigenvalue of
\varphi
. In the following, we will often make the simplifying assumption that
\pi_{\infty}
is tempered (i.e.
\pi
is tempered at infinity and
s\in i\mathbb{R}
). We write
s=iT
and assume
T
to be large. The local representations
\pi_{l}
with
l\neq p
are class 1 and uniquely determined by the Hecke eigenvalue
\lambda_{\varphi}(l)
of
\varphi
at
l
. Pinning down the ramified local representation
\pi_{p}
is harder. We recall from Remark
4.6
that due to our restriction on central character and level we find the following options:

•

There is a character
\chi\colon\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\to S^{1}
of (exponent) conductor
a(\chi)=2n
and
\pi_{p}=\chi\boxplus\chi^{-1}
; or

•

The representation
\pi_{p}
is supercuspidal and associated to an unramified quadratic extension
E/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and a character
\theta\colon E^{\times}\to S^{1}
of (exponent) conductor
a(\theta)=2n
.

5.2.1
Uniqueness theorems

Note that
\varphi
is
K_{0}(N)
-invariant. On the tensor product side, this latter vector therefore lies in the tensor product of

W_{l}=\{w\in\pi_{l}\mid\pi_{l}(k)w=w\,\text{for all}\,k\in K_{H,l}(v_{l}(N),0)\}

over all places
l
, tensored with
\pi_{\infty}^{K_{\infty}}
.

A crucial input in our approach to the sup-norm problem are the following uniqueness theorems.
21
21

21

This is not quite standard terminology. Depending on the situation, one speaks more generally of Gelfand pairs or multiplicity-one statements.

If
v
is the archimedean place or an unramified non-archimedean place, that is, a prime
l\neq p
, then
W_{v}
is (at most) one-dimensional by the uniqueness of spherical vectors. We refer here to Theorem 2.4.2 and Theorem 4.6.2 in
[
Bum97
]
.

At the ramified place
p
, we have the theory of newforms or new-vectors, as described in Section
4.2.2
. If
a(\pi_{p})=4n
the exponent conductor of
\pi_{p}
, then

\{w\in\pi_{p}\mid\pi_{p}(k)w=w\,\text{for all}\,k\in K_{H,p}({a(\pi_{p})},0)\}

is one-dimensional. This is due to newform theory, in the sense of Atkin and Lehner, as explained in Section 3 of
[
Cas73
]
. We conclude that
W_{p}
is also one-dimensional.

All in all, we see that the space of
K_{0}(N)
-invariant vectors in the representation
\pi
is spanned by
\varphi
.
Furthermore, since
N=N_{0}^{2}
, then the space of
K^{\ast}(N_{0})
-invariant vectors is spanned by

(5.1)

\varphi^{\prime}=\pi(a(N_{0}))\varphi.

This follows directly because
a(N_{0})^{-1}K^{\ast}(N_{0})_{\textrm{fin}}a(N_{0})=K_{0}(N)
.
As observed in
[
Mar16
,
Sah17
]
, it is advantageous to work with the form
\varphi^{\prime}
instead of
\varphi
, more generally for some well-chosen factorisation of
N
, when bounding the sup-norm in the level aspect. We follow this approach below.

6
The global period I

We keep the notation of the previous section, but to shorten notation we set
H=K^{\ast}(N_{0})
and
Z_{H}=Z\cap H
. Note that
Z_{H}
is compact and we have normalised measures so that
\operatorname{vol}(Z_{H})=2
. Define also
\Gamma=\operatorname{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Furthermore, we let
\pi
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q})
with trivial central character and level
N=p^{4n}
. We assume that
\pi
is tempered and spherical at infinity. Its spectral parameter is denoted by
T
and is assumed to be large. Let
\varphi^{\prime}
be the normalised shifted newform defined in (
5.1
).

In this section we make the first steps towards estimating the global sup-norm of
\varphi^{\prime}
(and thus also of our classical Hecke–Maaß newform
f
) using the local ideas developed earlier. The first step, carried out in Section
6.1
below, is to replace the rather singular period
\varphi^{\prime}(g)
, given by evaluation at a single point, by a more flexible one, following the sketch in Section
2.1
.

Indeed, using uniqueness of the
K^{\ast}(N_{0})
-invariant vector in
\pi
, we are able to write

(6.1)

\mathcal{Q}_{g}(\Psi)\cdot|\varphi^{\prime}(g)|^{2}=\left|\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}(Z_{H}\backslash H)}\int_{Z_{H}\backslash gHg^{-1}}\Psi(h)dh\right|^{2}.

Here,
\mathcal{Q}_{g}(\Psi)
is a weight that can be expressed as an integral of matrix coefficients — see (
6.1
) below for the precise definition. The key idea is now to choose
\Psi\in\pi
to be a microlocalised vector. If the microlocal support of
\Psi
is chosen carefully, one has good hope of obtaining satisfying lower bounds for
\mathcal{Q}_{g}(\Psi)
and simultaneously good control on the
gHg^{-1}
-periods of
\Psi
. In practice, the right hand side of (
6.1
) is bounded by embedding
\Psi
in a suitable spectral family and applying a relative trace formula. This is recorded in Proposition
6.1
below, which is our main global work horse.

We then use the local theory developed in Section
3
at the archimedean place and in Section
4
at the ramified place to choose suitable test functions and to gather important estimates. As a first result we establish a
hybrid baseline (or local) bound
: see Theorem
6.3
below.
Thus, in this section we rigorously execute all the steps sketched out in Section
2.6
, including an analogous treatment of the level aspect.

6.1
Integrals of global matrix coefficients

We now start as in Section
2.1
and roughly follow Section 3.5 in
[
Nel23b
]
.

First, the value
\varphi^{\prime}(1)
can be related to an integral of matrix coefficients.
For this we define the (densely defined) linear functional
l:L^{2}([G])\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
by

l(\Psi)=\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}(H)}\int_{H}\Psi(h)\,dh=\frac{2}{\operatorname{vol}(H)}\int_{Z_{H}\backslash H}\Psi(h)\,dh,

for smooth elements
\Psi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}([G])
.
It is straight-forward to check that

l(\pi(h)\Psi)=l(\Psi)\text{ for }h\in H.

Since
K_{\infty}\subseteq G_{\infty}
has measure
0
, we can not define
l
on the full Hilbert space
L^{2}([G])
directly. However, for our purposes it is sufficient to extend
l|_{\pi}\colon\pi\cap\mathcal{C}^{\infty}([G])\to\mathbb{C}
to the Hilbert (sub)space
\pi\subseteq L^{2}([G])
. To do so, we observe that
l
can be written as the evaluation at the identity of a vector, that is

(6.2)

l|_{\pi}(\Psi)=\left(\int_{H}\pi(k)\Psi dk\right)(1).

At this point we observe that the operator

\Psi\mapsto\int_{H}\pi(k)\Psi dk

is a projection on subspace of
H
-invariant elements in
\pi
. Since the latter space is spanned by the
L^{2}
-normalized element
\varphi^{\prime}
, see Section
5.2.1
, we find that

\int_{H}\pi(k)\Psi dk=\langle\Psi,\varphi^{\prime}\rangle\cdot\varphi^{\prime}.

Finally, note
\varphi^{\prime}
is smooth so that we can evaluate it at the identity. In view of (
6.2
) we obtain

(6.3)

l(\Psi)=\varphi^{\prime}(1)\cdot\langle\Psi,\varphi^{\prime}\rangle.

Next, decomposing into
H
-types and using orthogonality, we compute that

(6.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(\Psi)=\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}(H)}\int_{H}\langle\pi(h)\Psi,\Psi\rangle\,dh=\lvert{\langle\Psi,\varphi^{\prime}\rangle}\rvert^{2}.

One can compare this to the notation in Section 3.5 of
[
Nel23b
]
, where we could consider
\mathcal{Q}(\Psi\otimes 1)
, taking the trivial representation of
H
.
22
22

22

One could also take, for instance, other
\operatorname{SO}(2)
-types, generalising the present discussion to weight-
k
Maaß forms.

We combine (
6.3
) and (
6.4
) to obtain

|l(\Psi)|^{2}=|\varphi^{\prime}(1)|^{2}\cdot\mathcal{Q}(\Psi).

This is our replacement for
[
Nel23b
, (3.1)]
.

The advantage of the integral expression of
\mathcal{Q}
is that it factorises over all places and is not dependent on the global realisation of the vector
\Psi
.
Interpreting
\pi
now as a tensor product and assuming
\Psi
is a factorisable vector, we can write

\mathcal{Q}(\Psi)=Q_{\infty}(\Psi_{\infty})\cdot\prod_{p}Q_{p}(\Psi_{p}),

where

Q_{v}(\Psi_{v})=\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}(H_{v})}\int_{H_{v}}\langle\pi(h_{v})\Psi_{v},\Psi_{v}\rangle\,dh_{v}.

These local integrals of matrix coefficients are defined the same way in Section
3.3.4
, at the real place, and in Section
4.5
, at finite places.

To obtain more general values of
\varphi
, let
g\in G
and define
H^{g}=gHg^{-1}
. As above, the vector
\pi(g)\varphi
spans the space of
H^{g}
-invariant vectors in
\pi
.
Defining

l_{g}(\Psi)=\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}(\bar{H}^{g})}\int_{\bar{H}^{g}}\Psi(h)\,dh

and

\mathcal{Q}_{g}(\Psi)=\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}(\bar{H}^{g})}\int_{\bar{H}^{g}}\langle\pi(h)\Psi,\Psi\rangle\,dh,

the same computations as above now show that

(6.5)

|l_{g}(\Psi)|^{2}=|(\pi(g)\varphi^{\prime})(1)|^{2}\cdot\mathcal{Q}_{g}(\Psi)=|\varphi^{\prime}(g)|^{2}\cdot\mathcal{Q}_{g}(\Psi).

6.2
The pretrace inequality

Let
\mathcal{B}(\pi)
be any orthonormal basis for the representation
\pi
, viewed as a subspace of
L^{2}([G])
. For any test function
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G)
, we have a pretrace inequality

(6.6)

\sum_{\Psi\in\mathcal{B}(\pi)}\left|\int_{H}\pi(f)\Psi\right|^{2}\leq\int_{x,y\in H}\sum_{\gamma\in G^{\prime}(\mathbb{Q})}(f\ast f^{\ast})(x^{-1}\gamma y)\,dx\,dy

as given in
[
Nel23b
, Lemma 5.5]
or
[
Mar23
, Lemma 3.1]
. A nice way to derive this inequality is by an adaption of the argument presented in
[
Blo+20
, Section 3]
. We apply this inequality with a different subgroup and test function for each
g\in G
.

Recall that we put
H^{g}=gHg^{-1}
and define

f_{g}(x)=f(g^{-1}xg)

for any function
f
on
G
.
By the definition of convolution operators and a change of variables, we see that

\pi(f_{g})=\pi(g)\pi(f)\pi(g^{-1}).

Another simple application of the definition and a change of variables shows that

(f_{g}\ast f_{g}^{\ast})(x)=(f\ast f^{\ast})(g^{-1}xg).

We apply now the inequality (
6.6
) to
H^{g}
and
f_{g}
to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\Psi\in\mathcal{B}(\pi)}|l_{g}(\pi(f_{g})\cdot\Psi)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}(\bar{H}^{g})^{2}}\int_{x,y\in\bar{H}^{g}}\sum_{\gamma\in G^{\prime}(\mathbb{Q})}(f\ast f^{\ast})(g^{-1}x^{-1}\gamma yg)\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}(\bar{H})^{2}}\int_{x,y\in\bar{H}}\sum_{\gamma\in G^{\prime}(\mathbb{Q})}(f\ast f^{\ast})(x^{-1}g^{-1}\gamma gy)\,dx\,dy.

To treat the left-hand side above, we change the basis
\mathcal{B}(\pi)
by applying the unitary operator
\pi(g)
.
Thus, by independence of the choice of basis and the relation (
6.5
),

\displaystyle\sum_{\Psi\in\mathcal{B}(\pi)}|l_{g}(\pi(f_{g})\cdot\Psi)|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\Psi\in\mathcal{B}(\pi)}|l_{g}(\pi(f_{g})\pi(g)\cdot\Psi)|^{2}

\displaystyle=|\varphi^{\prime}(g)|^{2}\cdot\sum_{\Psi\in\mathcal{B}(\pi)}\mathcal{Q}_{g}(\pi(f_{g})\pi(g)\Psi).

Unpacking the definition of
\mathcal{Q}_{g}
and using the factorisation of
\pi(f_{g})
quickly shows that

\mathcal{Q}_{g}(\pi(f^{g})\pi(g)\Psi)=\mathcal{Q}(\pi(f)\Psi),

where we recall that
\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{Q}_{1}
.
All in all, we deduce the main pretrace inequality

(6.7)

|\varphi^{\prime}(g)|^{2}\cdot\sum_{\Psi\in\mathcal{B}(\pi)}\mathcal{Q}(\pi(f)\Psi)\leq\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}(H)^{2}}\int_{x,y\in H}\sum_{\gamma\in G^{\prime}(\mathbb{Q})}(f\ast f^{\ast})(x^{-1}g^{-1}\gamma gy)\,dx\,dy.

6.3
The test function

We now fix our choice of test function
f
.
This is done place by place, and we write

f=f_{\infty}\otimes f_{p}\otimes\bigotimes_{l\neq p}f_{l}.

We choose
f_{\infty}
as in Theorem
3.7
. Recall that
f_{\infty}
is supported in a small neighbourhood of the identity, which we denote by
\mathfrak{U}
.
At the unramified places
l\neq p
, we simply put
f_{l}=\mathbb{1}_{ZK_{l}}

(these places will be used later when we discuss amplification).
Finally, at the ramified place
p
, we have to consider two scenarios:

•

Suppose
\pi_{p}=\chi\boxplus\chi^{-1}
for
\chi\colon\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\to S^{1}
with
a(\chi)=2n
. Then we put

f_{p}(g)=\dim(V_{\textrm{new},p})\cdot\frac{\langle v_{p},\pi(g)v_{p}\rangle}{\|v_{p}\|^{2}}\cdot\mathbb{1}_{ZK_{p}}(g),

where
v_{p}=\pi_{p}(c_{1})v_{\mathrm{ML}}
with
c_{1}
as in (
4.20
) and
v_{ML}
being the standard microlocal lift vector.

•

Suppose
\pi_{p}
is supercuspidal with
a(\pi)=4n
. Then we define

f_{p}(g)=\dim(V_{\textrm{new},p})\cdot\frac{\langle v_{p},\pi(g)v_{p}\rangle}{\|v_{p}\|^{2}}\cdot\mathbb{1}_{ZK_{p}}(g)

where
v_{p}=v_{MV}
is the standard minimal vector. Note that here the matrix coefficient is automatically compactly supported, so that no cut-off is necessary.

With this choice we can compute that

\sum_{\phi\in\mathcal{B}(\pi)}\mathcal{Q}(\pi(f)\phi)=\left(\sum_{v\in\mathcal{B}(\pi_{\infty})}\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}(\pi(f_{\infty})v)\right)\cdot\mathcal{Q}_{p}(v_{p})

This follows from Lemma
4.10
and a convenient choice of (factorisable) basis
\mathcal{B}(\pi)
. Now we can use our local results to evaluate

\mathcal{Q}_{p}(v_{p})=\zeta_{p}(1)p^{-n}.

Here we used Lemma
4.35
. Finally, recall the definition of the integral
I_{\gamma}(f_{p}\ast f_{p})
given in (
4.24
).

Next, we observe that the support of
f
is in
Z(\mathbb{A})\cdot(\mathfrak{U}\times K_{\operatorname{f}})
. Very conveniently, we have

Z(\mathbb{A})\backslash[Z(\mathbb{A})\cdot(\mathfrak{U}\times K_{\operatorname{f}})]\cap G^{\prime}(\mathbb{Q})=\Gamma\cap\mathfrak{U}.

This is a consequence of strong approximation.x

We summarise this discussion in the following proposition:

Proposition 6.1
.

Suppose that
g_{\operatorname{f}}\in K_{\operatorname{f}}
, Then, with the choice of test function
f
exhibited above, we have

|\varphi^{\prime}(g)|^{2}\cdot\frac{\zeta_{p}(1)}{p^{n}}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{B}(\pi_{\infty})}\mathcal{Q}(\pi(f_{\infty})v)\\
\ll\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma\cap g\mathfrak{U}g^{-1}}I_{g^{-1}\gamma g}(f_{p}\times f_{p})\cdot\int_{x,y\in K_{\infty}^{\prime}}(f\ast f^{\ast})(x^{-1}g^{-1}\gamma gy)\,dx\,dy.

Remark 6.2
.

It will later be useful to shrink the support of the test function
f_{p}
a little bit. Indeed, we define

f_{p}^{\prime}=f_{p}\cdot\mathbb{1}_{ZK_{H}(1)}.

Note that we still have

f_{p}^{\prime}\ast f_{p}^{\prime}=\operatorname{vol}(K_{H}(1))\cdot f_{p}^{\prime}.

On the other hand we can choose the orthonormal basis
\mathcal{B}(\pi_{p})
of
\pi_{p}
such that it contains a finite subset
\mathcal{B}_{p}
so that

(6.8)

\pi(f_{p})w=\operatorname{vol}(K_{H}(1))w.

See
[
Sah17
, Corollary 2.16]
for a similar argument. In particular
v_{p}\in\mathcal{B}_{p}
.

6.4
The local bound

At this point we can already discuss how to establish the local bound, i.e. the baseline bound.

Theorem 6.3

(Local bound)
.

Let
\varphi^{\prime}
be as in (
5.1
) and fix a compact set
\Omega
. Then, for
\epsilon>0
, we have

\|\varphi^{\prime}|_{\Omega}\|_{\infty}^{2}\ll_{\Omega,p,\epsilon}(N_{0}T)^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}.

Proof.

By strong approximation we can assume that
\Omega\subset G_{\infty}^{+}
. Our starting point is Proposition
6.1
. Note that if
\gamma=1
, then

\int_{x,y\in K_{\infty}^{\prime}}(f\ast f^{\ast})(x^{-1}g^{-1}\gamma gy)\,dx\,dy=\operatorname{vol}(K_{\infty}^{\prime})\cdot\int_{K_{\infty}^{\prime}}(f\ast f^{\ast})(y)dy\ll T^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon},

where we have used the second estimate in Theorem
3.7
. Making use of the remaining two estimates of this theorem yields

|\varphi^{\prime}(g)|^{2}\cdot\frac{\zeta_{p}(1)}{p^{n}T^{\frac{1}{2}}}\ll_{\epsilon}T^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}I_{1}(f_{p}\times f_{p})+T^{\epsilon}\sum_{1\neq\gamma\in\Gamma\cap\mathfrak{U}}I_{g^{-1}\gamma g}(f_{p}\times f_{p})\cdot d(g^{-1}\gamma g)^{-1}.

Using (
4.27
) together with the volume bounds from Theorem
4.40
yields the estimate

|\varphi^{\prime}(g)|^{2}\ll_{p,\epsilon}T^{1+\epsilon}p^{2n}+T^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}p^{2n}\sum_{1\neq\gamma\in\Gamma\cap\mathfrak{U}}d(g^{-1}\gamma g)^{-1}.

Finally, we trivially estimate

(6.9)

\sum_{1\neq\gamma\in\Gamma\cap\mathfrak{U}}d(g^{-1}\gamma g)^{-1}\ll_{\mathfrak{U},\Omega}1

and recall that
N_{0}=p^{2n}
to obtain the bound

|\varphi^{\prime}(g)|^{2}\ll_{p,\epsilon}T^{1+\epsilon}p^{2n}.

Taking the square root gives the desired result.
∎

Remark 6.4
.

This local bound can be achieved in a variety of ways. For example, using the generating set for
X_{N_{0}}
from
[
Sah17
]
, one gets a global (i.e. without restriction to
\Omega
) version of Theorem
6.3
by estimating the Fourier expansion. See also Proposition
A.1
below. To the best of our knowledge, a local bound as above first appeared in the work of Marshall
[
Mar16
]
.

Note that our proof only uses the most basic volume estimates. In particular, at the ramified place
p
we have not used anything but the trivial bound. Doing so we have lost a lot of valuable information on the geometric side. In the next sections we will upgrade the simple pretrace inequality used here to an amplified pretrace inequality. This will allow us to exploit the full strength of our volume bounds. Beside this, we will choose a suitable generating domain for the quotient
