[G]
which allows us to address the dependence on the compact set
\Omega
.

7
Amplification and counting

This section is an intermezzo in which we prepare some further tools that are necessary before we can return to the study of the global period in Section
8
. Our goals here are twofold.

First, in Section
7.1
we construct a refined test function at the unramified places, which will complement the choice made at the places
\infty
and
p
in Section
6.3
. This unramified test function is essentially a cleverly chosen combination of Hecke operators and often referred to as an
amplifier
.

Second, recall that in order to establish the baseline bound in the proof of Theorem
6.3
, we have estimated the geometric side using the essentially trivial count (
6.9
). Such an naive approach is not sufficient if one wants to improve on the baseline bound. We will provide sufficiently uniform counting results in Section
7.3
below. See Corollary
7.10
for a packaging of these that will be used later on.

As in the previous section, we are working with a cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi=\bigotimes_{v}\pi_{v}
. Let
N
denote the level of
\pi
and let
\varphi^{\prime}
be as in (
5.1
).

7.1
Hecke operators and the amplifier

For each
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
(m,N)=1
we can define a test function
\kappa_{m}
such that
R(\kappa_{m})
is a Hecke operator. In particular, we have

R(\kappa_{m})\varphi^{\prime}=\lambda_{\pi}(m)\varphi^{\prime}.

Here
\lambda_{\pi}(m)
are Hecke eigenvalues of
\pi
(resp. of
\varphi
or
\varphi^{\prime}
), which we normalise such that
|\lambda_{\pi}(m)|\leq\tau(m)
under the Ramanujan conjecture.

We define the functions
\kappa_{m}
following
[
Sah17
, Section 3.5]
. First we factor them as

\kappa_{m}=\prod_{(l,N)=1}\kappa_{l,v_{l}(m)}\text{ for }\kappa_{l,v_{l}(m)}\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(Z\backslash G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})).

The local test functions are now defined by

\kappa_{l,r}(g)=\begin{cases}l^{-\frac{r}{2}}&\text{ if }g\in ZK_{l}a(l^{r})K_{l},\\
0&\text{ else.}\end{cases}

Note that these are bi-
K_{l}
-invariant and
\kappa_{l,0}
is simply the characteristic function on
ZK_{l}
. For later use we record the Hecke relations

(7.1)

\kappa_{r}\ast\kappa_{s}=\sum_{t\mid(r,s)}\kappa_{\frac{rs}{t^{2}}}\text{ for }(rs,N)=1.

See for example
[
Bum97
, Proposition 4.6.4]
for details. The Hecke eigenvalues inherit this recursion property. In particular, they satisfy
\lambda(l)^{2}=\lambda(l^{2})+1
for all primes
l\nmid N
. This allows us to deduce the important inequality

(7.2)

|\lambda(l)|+|\lambda(l^{2})|>\frac{1}{2}.

Define the weights

x_{m}=\frac{\overline{\lambda_{\pi}}(m)}{|\lambda_{\pi}(m)|}.

For a parameter
X
, fix the finite set of primes
\mathcal{P}_{X}\subseteq\{l\in[X,2X]\colon(l,N)=1\}
and define the test function

(7.3)

f_{\textrm{ur}}^{X}=\left(\sum_{l\in\mathcal{P}_{X}}x_{l}\kappa_{l}\right)\ast\left(\sum_{l\in\mathcal{P}_{X}}x_{l}\kappa_{l}\right)+\left(\sum_{l\in\mathcal{P}_{X}}x_{l^{2}}\kappa_{l^{2}}\right)\ast\left(\sum_{l\in\mathcal{P}_{X}}x_{l^{2}}\kappa_{l^{2}}\right).

This version of the amplifier is taken from
[
Blo+20
, (9.14)]
.
23
23

23

This amplifier is state of the art. For our purposes more rudimentary amplifiers would be sufficient, but this one is still very convenient to work with.
This function is constructed as a sum of self-convolutions, so that
R(f_{\textrm{ur}}^{X})
is a positive operator.
It will be useful to write

(7.4)

f_{\textrm{ur}}^{X}=f_{\textrm{ur},1}^{X}\ast f_{\textrm{ur},1}^{X}+f_{\textrm{ur},2}^{X}\ast f_{\textrm{ur},2}^{X}

for the obvious functions
f_{\textrm{ur},i}^{X}
.

We find that

R(f_{\textrm{ur}}^{X})\varphi^{\prime}=C_{X}(\pi)\cdot\varphi^{\prime}.

with

\displaystyle C_{X}(\pi)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{l\in\mathcal{P}_{X}}|\lambda_{\pi}(l)|\right)^{2}+\left(\sum_{l\in\mathcal{P}_{X}}|\lambda_{\pi}(l^{2})|\right)^{2}

(7.5)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{l\in\mathcal{P}_{X}}|\lambda(l)|+|\lambda(l^{2})|\right)^{2}>\frac{1}{8}(\sharp\mathcal{P}_{X})^{2}.

In the last step we used (
7.2
). See also
[
Blo+20
, (9.17)]
for the same chain of inequalities.
On the other hand, we can write

(7.6)

f_{\textrm{ur}}^{X}=\sum_{m}a_{m}\cdot\kappa_{m}

using the Hecke relations (
7.1
). More precisely we only need to compute

\displaystyle f_{\textrm{ur},1}^{X}\ast f_{\textrm{ur},1}^{X}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{X}}x_{l_{1}}x_{l_{2}}\cdot\kappa_{l_{1}}\ast\kappa_{l_{2}}=\sum_{l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{X}}x_{l_{1}}x_{l_{2}}\cdot(\kappa_{l_{1}l_{2}}+\delta_{l_{1}=l_{2}}\kappa_{1})

and

\displaystyle f_{\textrm{ur},2}^{X}\ast f_{\textrm{ur},2}^{X}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{X}}x_{l_{1}^{2}}x_{l_{2}^{2}}\cdot\kappa_{l_{1}^{2}}\ast\kappa_{l_{2}^{2}}=\sum_{l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{X}}x_{l_{1}^{2}}x_{l_{2}^{2}}\cdot(\kappa_{l_{1}^{2}l_{2}^{2}}+\delta_{l_{1}=l_{2}}(\kappa_{l_{1}^{2}}+\kappa_{1})).

Adding these two expressions yields that the coefficients
a_{m}
in (
7.6
) are given by

(7.7)

a_{m}=\begin{cases}x_{l_{1}^{2}}x_{l_{2}^{2}}&\text{ if }m=l_{1}^{2}l_{2}^{2}\text{ for }l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{X},\\
x_{l_{1}}x_{l_{2}}\cdot(1+\delta_{m=\square})&\text{ if }m=l_{1}l_{2}\text{ for }l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{X},\\
2\cdot\sharp\mathcal{P}_{X}&\text{ if }m=1,\\
0&\text{ else.}\end{cases}

For our purposes, it is sufficient to keep in mind that
a_{1}\asymp\sharp\mathcal{P}_{X}
,
|a_{m}|\asymp 1
if
m=l_{1}^{i}l_{2}^{i}
for
l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{X}
and
i=1,2
.

Note that any one of the functions
\kappa_{m}
are bi-
K_{l}
invariant for all finite places
l\nmid N
. In particular, for any
\Psi\in\pi
, the elements
R(\kappa_{m})\Psi
are spherical at all finite places
(l,N)=1
. Thus, when working with test functions that are combinations of the
\kappa_{m}
, it is sufficient to work with the corresponding subspace of
\pi
consisting of elements that are unramified away from
N
. We will denote this subspace by
\pi_{N}
. (Note that we can identify
\pi_{N}
with
\pi_{\infty}\otimes\bigotimes_{p\mid N}\pi_{p}
). Let
\mathcal{B}^{N}(\pi)
be a basis of
\pi_{N}
. We observe that for all
\Psi\in\mathcal{B}^{N}(\pi)
we have

R(\kappa_{m})\Psi=\lambda_{\pi}(m)\Psi.

This justifies the notation
\lambda_{\pi}(m)
. We will always assume that
\mathcal{B}^{N}(\pi)\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\pi)
.

Remark 7.1
.

We have only discussed the
amplifying
effect that
f_{\textrm{ur}}^{X}
has on
\varphi^{\prime}
. This is sufficient because we can drop the contribution of all other terms on the spectral side by positivity. It is however instructive to consider other functions. Indeed, suppose
\pi^{\prime}
is some other cuspidal automorphic representation containing a non-trivial
H
-invariant element
\psi^{\prime}
. Then we obtain

R(f_{\textrm{ur}}^{X})\psi^{\prime}=C_{X}(\pi^{\prime})\cdot\psi^{\prime}

for

C_{X}(\pi^{\prime})=\left(\sum_{l\in\mathcal{P}_{X}}\operatorname{sgn}(\lambda_{\pi}(l))\cdot\lambda_{\pi^{\prime}}(l)\right)^{2}+\left(\sum_{l\in\mathcal{P}_{X}}\operatorname{sgn}(\lambda_{\pi}(l^{2}))\cdot\lambda_{\pi^{\prime}}(l^{2})\right)^{2}.

Since
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
are distinct their Hecke eigenvalues are expected to be uncorrelated and we expect that

C_{X}(\pi^{\prime})=o((\sharp\mathcal{P}_{X})^{2}).

While showing this in practice is beyond our capabilities, it shows that at least morally
R(f_{\textrm{ur}}^{X})
dampens the contribution of representations besides
\pi
in the relative trace formula.

7.2
Analysing the support condition

Throughout this section we let
N=p^{4n}
and let
g_{\infty}\in G(\mathbb{R})
and
g_{p}\in K_{p}
. We will need to control the size of the sets

(7.8)

\mathfrak{M}(g_{\infty},g_{p};m,\delta,l)=\{\gamma\in G^{\prime}(\mathbb{Q})\colon\kappa_{m}(\gamma)\neq 0,\,d_{H(2n)}(g_{p}^{-1}\gamma g_{p})\leq p^{-l}\\
\text{ and }d(g_{\infty}^{-1}\gamma g_{\infty})\leq\delta\}.

We want to find representatives for this set which allow us to run more classical counting arguments.

Recall the action of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
on the upper half-plane
\mathbb{H}\subset\mathbb{C}
(see e.g.
[
Iwa02
, Chap. 1]
).
The latter inherits the invariant distance function by setting
d(z,i)=d(g_{z})
for
z\in\mathbb{H}
and a matrix
g_{z}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
such that
g_{z}.i=z
, where
i
is the imaginary unit.
By invariance, we set
d(z,w)=d(g_{z}^{-1}w,i)
.
We then define the point-pair invariant

u(z,w)=\frac{\lvert{z-w}\rvert^{2}}{\operatorname{Im}z\cdot\operatorname{Im}w}.

As in (1.3) in
[
Iwa02
, Chap. 1]
, we have

\cosh d(z,w)=1+\frac{1}{2}\cdot u(z,w).

This implies the bound

(7.9)

d(z,w)^{2}\asymp u(z,w)

for small
d(z,w)
.

The following lemma is essentially
[
Sah17
, Lemma 3.19]
and translates between the adelic and classical setting.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
l\geq 0
and let
\delta>0
be sufficiently small.
Let
g_{p}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
,
z=g_{\infty}.i
and, for a positive integer
L
, define

\displaystyle M(z,g_{p};\delta^{\prime},L,m)=\sharp\{\gamma\in\operatorname{Mat}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\colon

\displaystyle u(\gamma.z,z)\leq\delta^{\prime},\,L\mid[g_{p}^{-1}\gamma g_{p}]_{12},

\displaystyle L\mid[g_{p}^{-1}\gamma g_{p}]_{21}\text{ and }\det(\gamma)=m\}.

Then we have

\sharp\mathfrak{M}(g_{\infty},g_{p};m,\delta,l)\leq M(z,g_{p};\delta^{\prime},p^{l},m),

where
\delta^{2}\asymp\delta^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Suppose
\gamma\in\mathfrak{M}(g_{\infty},g_{p};m,\delta,l)
. We first observe that

\gamma\in ZK_{p}.

This is because
l\geq 0
and
g_{p}\in K_{p}
, so that the condition
d_{H(2n)}(g_{p}^{-1}\gamma g_{p})
in particular implies that
\gamma\in ZK_{p}
. At places
l\neq p
we use the support of
\gamma_{m}
to obtain that

\gamma\in ZK_{l}a(l^{v_{l}(m)})K_{l}.

Thus we find
\xi\in Z(\mathbb{Q})
such that
\gamma=\xi\gamma^{\prime}
with
\gamma^{\prime}\in\textrm{Mat}_{2\times 2}(\mathbb{Z})
and
\det(\gamma^{\prime})=m
.
Next, the distance condition follows now from (
7.9
).

Finally, we have to take the condition
d_{H(2n)}(g_{p}^{-1}\gamma g_{p})=p^{-l}
into account. We relax this condition to

g_{p}^{-1}\gamma^{\prime}g_{p}\in K_{H,p}(l,l).

Under the assumption
g_{p}\in\textrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, this reduces to

g_{p}^{-1}\gamma^{\prime}g_{p}=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{matrix}\right)\in\textrm{Mat}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})

with
p^{l}\mid(c,b)
.
∎

Remark 7.3
.

In the volume bounds Corollary
4.41
we cannot distinguish between
d_{H(2n)}(g_{p}^{-1}\gamma g_{p})=p^{l}
and
d_{H(2n)}(wg_{p}^{-1}\gamma g_{p})=p^{l}
, where
w
is the long Weyl element. Thus, a priori one would also encounter the following sets of matrices:

(7.10)

\mathfrak{M}^{\textrm{op}}(g_{\infty},g_{p};m,\delta,l)=\{\gamma\in G^{\prime}(\mathbb{Q})\colon\kappa_{m}(\gamma)\neq 0,\,d_{H(2n)}(wg_{p}^{-1}\gamma g_{p})\leq p^{-l}\\
\text{ and }d(g_{\infty}^{-1}\gamma g_{\infty})\leq\delta\}.

Essentially re-producing the arguments given above one finds that

\sharp\mathfrak{M}^{\textrm{op}}(g_{\infty},g_{p};m,\delta,l)\leq M^{\textrm{op}}(z,g_{p};\delta^{\prime},L,m),

where
\delta^{2}\asymp\delta^{\prime}
sufficiently small and

(7.11)

M^{\textrm{op}}(z,g_{p};\delta^{\prime},L,m)=\sharp\{\gamma\in\textrm{Mat}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\colon u(\gamma.z,z)\leq\delta^{\prime},\\
L\mid[g_{p}^{-1}\gamma g_{p}]_{ii}\text{ for }i=1,2\text{ and }\det(\gamma)=m\}.

These matrices seem hard to count.
To circumvent this issue, we later on employ the support shrinking technique, as in Remark
6.2
, to avoid the sets
\mathfrak{M}^{\textrm{op}}(g_{\infty},g_{p};m,\delta,l)
altogether.

7.2.1
Useful inequalities and congruences

To aid in counting the matrices
\gamma
, we observe the following bounds on matrix entries.
These can also be found in
[
Blo+20
, Sec. 10.1]
, but we give here slightly more conceptual arguments.

Firstly, assume that
\gamma\in\operatorname{Mat}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
with
\det\gamma=m>0
and set
\tilde{\gamma}=m^{-1/2}\gamma\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
Then the hyperbolic distance
d(g_{\infty}^{-1}\gamma g_{\infty})=\delta
can be given using the Cartan decomposition (or geodesic polar coordinates, see
[
Iwa02
, Sec. 1.3]
).
More precisely, there are
k^{\prime},k^{\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{SO}(2)
such that
g_{\infty}^{-1}\tilde{\gamma}g_{\infty}
is equal to
k^{\prime}\operatorname{diag}(e^{-\delta/2},e^{\delta/2})k^{\prime\prime}
.
Expanding the exponential into a series, if
\delta<1
, we find there is
k\in\operatorname{SO}(2)
such that

(7.12)

g_{\infty}^{-1}\gamma g_{\infty}=\sqrt{m}(k+O(\delta)).

Write now

\gamma=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}.

Applying the conjugation invariant trace to equation
7.12
, the former being conjugation invariant, we obtain that

(7.13)

a+d\ll\sqrt{m},

using that orthogonal matrices are bounded.
Next, using the Iwasawa decomposition, we can write

g_{\infty}=\begin{pmatrix}y&x\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

Performing the conjugation shows that the lower-left entry of
g_{\infty}^{-1}\gamma g_{\infty}
is simply
cy
.
Thus, the condition implies that

(7.14)

cy\ll\sqrt{m}.

Finally, the condition
u(\gamma z,z)<\delta^{\prime}
gives the bound

(7.15)

\lvert{-cz^{2}+(a-d)z+b}\rvert^{2}\ll m\delta^{\prime}y^{2},

where
z=g_{\infty}.i\in\mathbb{H}
, as in
[
Tem15
, (1.10)]
, for example.

On the non-archimedean side of the medal, these inequalities are replaced by congruences coming from the condition

p^{l}\mid[g_{p}^{-1}\gamma g_{p}]_{ij}\text{ for }\{i,j\}=\{1,2\},

which are satisfied for all
\gamma
that contribute to the count
M(z,g_{p};\delta^{\prime};p^{l},m)
. Let
24
24

24

Observe that the lower right entry of
g_{p}
is denoted by
w
, which is the symbol usually reserved for the long Weyl element. We hope this double usage of the letter
w
does not cause any confusion.

g_{p}=\left(\begin{matrix}t&u\\
v&w\end{matrix}\right)\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}).

Thus we have
tw-uv=1
. We note that, since
wK_{H,p}(l,l)w^{-1}=K_{H,p}(l,l)
we can always replace
g_{p}
by
g_{p}w
.
In particular, we can assume that
(v,p)=1
.
Put
s=v_{p}(w)
.
25
25

25

We have
g_{p}^{-1}\gamma g_{p}\in K_{H,p}(l,l)
if and only if
(g_{p}w)^{-1}\gamma(g_{p}w)\in K_{H,p}(l,l)
.
However,

g_{p}w=\left(\begin{matrix}-u&t\\
-w&v\end{matrix}\right).

Since
tw-uv=1
we can not have
v_{p}(w)>0
and
v_{p}(v)>0
simultaneously.
,
26
26

26

We remark in passing that this reduction step is reminiscent of the Atkin-Lehner operators applied in the treatment of the square-free level case, e.g.
[
HT13
]
. Indeed, the normalising Weyl element
w
corresponds to the Fricke involution in our balanced group.

Recall that we want to count
M(z,g_{p};\delta^{\prime},p^{l},m)
with
\delta^{\prime}<1
and
l>0
. (The case
l=0
is straight forward and follows directly from the counting results of
[
Tem15
]
.) We write a matrix
\gamma
contributing to this count as

\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{matrix}\right)\in\operatorname{Mat}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}).

We compute

(7.16)

g_{p}^{-1}\gamma g_{p}=\left(\begin{matrix}\ast&(a-d)uw+bw^{2}-cu^{2}\\
-(a-d)vt-bv^{2}+ct^{2}&\ast\end{matrix}\right).

Setting

A=a-d,

we obtain the congruence conditions

\displaystyle Auw+bw^{2}-cu^{2}

\displaystyle\equiv 0\text{ mod }p^{l}

\displaystyle-Avt-bv^{2}+ct^{2}

\displaystyle\equiv 0\text{ mod }p^{l}.

We put

T_{g}=\left(\begin{matrix}u&w\\
-t&-v\end{matrix}\right)\text{ and }v_{g}=\left(\begin{matrix}u^{2}\\
-t^{2}\end{matrix}\right).

We can write the congruence conditions as

\left(\begin{matrix}w&0\\
0&v\end{matrix}\right)T_{g}\left(\begin{matrix}A\\
b\end{matrix}\right)\equiv c\cdot v_{g}\text{ mod }p^{l}.

We note that
\det(T_{g})=1
and

T_{g}^{-1}=\left(\begin{matrix}-v&-w\\
t&u\end{matrix}\right).

We can further reduce this by writing
w=p^{s}w_{0}
and
v=p^{r}v_{0}
with
\gcd(p,v_{0}w_{0})=1
. Let
\overline{w_{0}}
(resp.
\overline{v}_{0}
) be a representative of the modular inverse of
w_{0}
(resp.
v_{0}
) modulo
p^{n}
. (Here we anticipate that later we will only need
l\leq n
.) Then we further reduce to

(7.17)

\left(\begin{matrix}p^{s}&0\\
0&p^{r}\end{matrix}\right)T_{g}\left(\begin{matrix}A\\
b\end{matrix}\right)=c\left(\begin{matrix}\overline{w_{0}}&0\\
0&\overline{v_{0}}\end{matrix}\right)v_{g}+p^{l}\mathbb{Z}^{2}=\left(\begin{matrix}c\cdot u^{2}\overline{w_{0}}\\
-c\cdot t^{2}\overline{v_{0}}\end{matrix}\right)+p^{l}\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

As mentioned above we can later reduce to the case where
r=0
, so that
v=v_{0}
is co-prime to
p
.

7.3
Counting results

It is now key to have uniform estimates for
M(z,g_{p};\delta^{\prime},L,m)
.
The easiest results can be obtained when only focusing on
\delta^{\prime}
.
Here we can drop the constraints coming from
g_{p}
and
L
altogether.
In particular, it will be sufficient to work with
z
in the standard fundamental domain for
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, which we denote by
\mathcal{F}
.
We import the following counting result from
[
IS95
, Appendix 1]
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Suppose
z\in\mathcal{F}
,
g_{p}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and assume that
\delta^{\prime}\leq 1
. Then we have

M(z,g_{p};\delta^{\prime},L,m)\ll m^{\epsilon}(1+m(\delta^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{4}}+\sqrt{m\delta^{\prime}}y).

The same bound holds for
M^{\textrm{op}}(z,g_{p};\delta^{\prime},L,m)
.

Note that the implicit constant is independent of
L
and
g_{p}
, since we are simply forgetting the corresponding conditions.
However, we would expect and, indeed, require some saving in
L
.
To see this saving, we present a slight adaptation of the Harcos-Templier
[
HT13
]
counting argument.
It is based on the following simple, yet powerful lattice point estimate.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
B_{w}(R)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a ball of radius
R
centred at
w\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Next, let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a lattice of full rank, with first successive minimum
\lambda_{1}
and covolume
V
.
Then we have

(7.18)

\sharp\Lambda\cap B_{w}(R)\ll 1+\frac{R}{\lambda_{1}}+\frac{R^{2}}{V}.

Furthermore, if
\Lambda^{\ast}
is the set of primitive lattice points, i.e. not of the form
k\cdot v
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
and
v\in\Lambda
, then

(7.19)

\sharp\Lambda^{\ast}\cap B_{0}(R)\ll 1+\frac{R^{2}}{V}.

Proof.

This is well-known and, for convenience, we refer to
[
BHM16
, Lemma 1]
, which covers the first assertion.
To prove the second assertion we easily modify the argument as follows.

Assume there exists
0\neq v\in\Lambda^{\ast}\cap B_{0}(R)
.
Then the
\mathbb{R}
-span of
\Lambda^{\ast}\cap B_{0}(R)
is one- or two-dimensional.
In the first case, there are only 2 primitive points.
In the latter, we follow the argument of
[
BHM16
]
using translates of a fundamental parallelipiped, whose volume is
V
.
∎

Given
z=x+iy\in\mathbb{H}
, our analysis revolves around the lattice

\Lambda_{z}=z\cdot\mathbb{Z}+\mathbb{Z}\subseteq\mathbb{C}

and its sublattices given by congruence conditions.
Note that the covolume of
\Lambda_{z}
is given by

\det(\Lambda_{z})=y.

The first successive minimum, i.e. the shortest non-zero length, of
\Lambda_{z}
is denoted by
\lambda_{z}
.
Reduction theory is classically done so that, if
z
lies in the standard fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}
for
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, then the shortest vector in
\Lambda_{z}
is
1\in\mathbb{C}
and thus
\lambda_{z}=1
.
More generally, it is true that
\lambda_{z}\geq\min(y,1)
.

We can now turn towards the counting results.
Adapting the Harcos-Templier method, more precisely inspired by Lemma 2 in Section 2.3 of
[
HT13
]
, we obtain the following.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
z=x+iy\in\mathbb{H}
with
y\gg 1
,
g_{p}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
,
0<\delta^{\prime}<1
and
0\leq l\leq n
then we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m=l_{1}l_{2}\\
l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{X}\end{subarray}}M(z,g_{p};\delta^{\prime},p^{l},m)\ll X^{2}+\frac{X^{3}\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}}{p^{l}}y+\frac{X^{4}\delta^{\prime}}{p^{l}}.

Proof.

We assume
l>0
, since otherwise the result follows directly from the counting results in
[
Tem15
]
. We use the notation for
g_{p}
and
\gamma
introduced in Section
7.2.1
. In particular, we reduce to the case where
(v,p)=1
and
w=p^{s}w_{0}
.

We start by estimating the subset of matrices where
c\neq 0
.
For clarity, we consider several cases.
First, if
s\geq l
, then the congruence coming from (
7.17
) degenerates to

c\cdot\underbrace{u^{2}\overline{w_{0}}}_{\in(\mathbb{Z}/p^{l}\mathbb{Z})^{\times}}\equiv 0\text{ mod }p^{l}.

In particular, we must have
p^{l}\mid c
. Forgetting the remaining congruences,
27
27

27

This will cost us a power of
p^{l}
in the end, but this is irrelevant in our application.
we can simply count pairs
(A,b)
using (
7.15
), which we write as

(7.20)

|-cz^{2}+Az+b|^{2}\ll X^{2}\delta^{\prime}y^{2}.

In view of (
7.18
) we get

\sharp\{(a-d,b)\}\ll 1+\frac{Xy\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}}{\lambda_{z}}+X^{2}\delta^{\prime}y.

Note that, since
y\gg 1
we have
\lambda_{z}^{-1}\ll 1
and that by (
7.14
) the bound
c\ll X/y
holds. This allows us to simply sum over all such
c
and obtain

\sharp\{(c,b,a-d)\}\ll\frac{X}{p^{l}}+\frac{X^{2}\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}}{p^{l}}+\frac{X^{3}\delta^{\prime}}{p^{l}}.

Here we also used that
p^{l}\mid c
and
c\neq 0
, as well as
y^{-1}\ll 1
in the first term.
Finally, recall that (
7.13
) states that
a+d\ll X
. This allows for an easy count of possible values for
a+d\in\mathbb{Z}
. Altogether we have

\sharp\{(a-d,a+d,b,c)\colon c\neq 0\}\ll\frac{X^{2}}{p^{l}}+\frac{X^{3}\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}}{p^{l}}+\frac{X^{4}\delta^{\prime}}{p^{l}}

in the case
s\geq l
.
Each quadruple obviously fixes
\gamma
.

Next, we consider the case
0\leq s<l
. Here the argument is similar. We first look at (
7.17
) but consider it modulo
p^{s}
. We infer that
p^{s}\mid c
, which we keep in mind (this is an empty condition if
s=0
, but this is allowed).

For what follows it will be useful to identify
\mathbb{C}
with
\mathbb{R}^{2}
via

\mathbb{C}\ni x+iy\mapsto\left(\begin{matrix}y\\
x\end{matrix}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

In particular,
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
interpreted as column vectors corresponds to the Gaußian integers in
\mathbb{C}
. Returning to the argument we fix
c
and combine (
7.20
) with (
7.17
).
The latter says that
(A,b)^{T}
shifted by a fixed vector lies in the lattice

\Lambda_{z,g_{p}}=p^{l}\cdot\left(\begin{matrix}y&0\\
x&1\end{matrix}\right)T_{g}^{-1}a(p^{-s})\cdot\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

Shifting the centre of the ball in (
7.20
), we see that

\sharp\{(a-d,b)\}\ll\sharp\Lambda_{z,g_{p}}\cap B_{\mathfrak{v}_{c}}(X\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}y),

where
B_{\mathfrak{v}_{c}}(R)
is the ball or radius
R
around

\mathfrak{v}_{c}=c\left(\begin{matrix}2xy\\
x^{2}-y^{2}\end{matrix}\right)-c\left(\begin{matrix}y&0\\
x&1\end{matrix}\right)T_{g}^{-1}a(p^{-s})\cdot v_{g},

which we identify with a point in
\mathbb{C}
, as usual.
Let
\lambda_{z,g_{p}}
denote the minimum of
\Lambda_{z,g_{p}}
, and note that
\det(\Lambda_{z,g_{p}})=yp^{2l-s}
. Applying (
7.18
) yields

\sharp\{(a-d,b)\}\ll 1+\frac{X\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}y}{\lambda_{z,g_{p}}}+\frac{X^{2}\delta^{\prime}y}{p^{2l-s}}.

At this point we observe that, since
p^{l}a(p^{-s})\mathbb{Z}^{2}\subseteq p^{l-s}\cdot\mathbb{Z}^{2}
for
s<l
and
T_{g}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, we have
\Lambda_{z,g_{p}}\subseteq p^{l-s}\Lambda_{z}
. In particular, we have
\lambda_{z,g_{p}}\geq p^{l-s}\lambda_{z}
. Inserting this and
\lambda_{z}\gg 1
above and summing over the possibilities for
p^{s}\mid c
with
0\neq c\ll X/y
yields

\sharp\{(c,a-d,b)\colon c\neq 0\}\ll X+\frac{X^{2}\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}}{p^{l}}+\frac{X^{3}\delta^{\prime}}{p^{2l}}.

Finally, we account for
a+d
trivially using
a+d\ll X
as above. The result is

\sharp\{(c,a-d,a+d,b)\colon c\neq 0\}\ll X^{2}+\frac{X^{3}\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}}{p^{l}}+\frac{X^{4}\delta^{\prime}}{p^{2l}}.

Next, we consider the case of
c=0
.
The congruence condition from (
7.17
) on the second component implies that

tA+vb\equiv 0\text{ mod }p^{l}\mathbb{Z}.

Thus, we can write

b=-\overline{v_{0}}t\cdot A+p^{l}b^{\prime},

for some
b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
, where we recall that we can assume
v=v_{0}
and
\gcd(v_{0},p)=1
.
At this point we modify the standard lattice point counting argument and first sort the lattice points by their greatest common divisor. This enables us to input a primitivity condition and improve on the trivial bound from (
7.18
). Therefore, let
b_{0}=\gcd(A,b^{\prime})
and write
b^{\prime}=b_{0}b^{\prime\prime}
and
A=b_{0}A^{\prime}
.
Keep in mind that we have achieved
(A^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})=1
.

Fixing a value of
b_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
, condition (
7.15
) gives the problem of counting primitive lattice points
(A^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})
such that

|A^{\prime}(z-\overline{v_{0}}t)+p^{l}b^{\prime\prime}|^{2}\ll\frac{X^{2}\delta^{\prime}y^{2}}{b_{0}^{2}}.

By (
7.19
), the number of such points is bounded by

1+\frac{X^{2}\delta^{\prime}}{p^{l}b_{0}^{2}}y.

Here, the lattice
\mathbb{Z}(z-\overline{v_{0}}t)+p^{l}\mathbb{Z}
is a sublattice of index
p^{l}
inside
\mathbb{Z}(z-\overline{v_{0}}t)+\mathbb{Z}=\Lambda_{z}
. In particuar we find that the covolume of
\mathbb{Z}(z-\overline{v_{0}}t)+p^{l}\mathbb{Z}
is
p^{l}\cdot\det(\Lambda_{z})=p^{l}y
.

Next, we bound the number of possibilities for
b_{0}
. For this, we observe that
ad\ll X^{2}
, by applying the determinant, and that
a+d\ll X
, by applying the trace. Since
(a-d)^{2}=(a+d)^{2}-4ad
, we obtain that
a-d\ll X
. This implies that
b_{0}\ll X
, because
b_{0}\mid(a-d)
, showing that there are
\ll X
possibilities for
b_{0}
. We now sum the bound for the number of primitive points above over
b_{0}\ll X
and find that the number of pairs
(A,b)
is

\ll X+\frac{X^{2}\delta^{\prime}}{p^{l}}y.

Since
ad=m=l_{1}l_{2}
, we conclude that each such pair gives rise to at most
X
matrices contributing to the count. We get
28
28

28

The standard argument gives

\sharp\{(a-d,ad,b)\}\ll X+\frac{\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}X^{2}}{\lambda_{z,l}}y+\frac{X^{3}\delta^{\prime}}{p^{l}}y

instead, where
\lambda_{z,l}
is the minimum of the lattice
\mathbb{Z}\cdot z+p^{l}\mathbb{Z}
.

\sharp\{(a-d,ad,b)\}\ll X^{2}+\frac{X^{3}\delta^{\prime}}{p^{l}}y.

Combining the two cases completes the counting for
M
and the result as stated follows by simplifying some of the terms.
∎

For counting matrices with square determinant
m=l_{1}^{2}l_{2}^{2}
, we employ a standard strategy to detect the sparseness in the average over the determinants.
We write

M(z,1;\delta^{\prime},p^{l},m)=M_{\ast}(z,1;\delta^{\prime},p^{l},m)+M_{\mathrm{par}}(z,1;\delta^{\prime},p^{l},m),

where the subscript
\mathrm{par}
stands for the sub-count of parabolic matrices with non-vanishing bottom-left entry.
Recall that parabolic matrices satisfy the equation
\operatorname{tr}^{2}=4\det
.
Now, for the first term, we proceed with a similar argument.

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
z=x+iy\in\mathbb{H}
with
y\gg 1
,
g_{p}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
,
0<\delta^{\prime}<1
and
0\leq l
.
Then, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m=l_{1}^{2}l_{2}^{2}\\
l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{X}\end{subarray}}M_{\ast}(z,g_{p};\delta^{\prime},p^{l},m)\ll_{\epsilon}X^{2+\epsilon}\cdot\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}X^{2}}{p^{l}}y+\frac{\delta^{\prime}X^{4}}{p^{l}}\right).

Proof.

Note that every matrix contributing to the count has square determinant
\det(\gamma)=m\asymp X^{4}
. We first assume
c\neq 0
and count admissible entries
a-d,b,c
as above. This gives

\sharp\{(a-d,b,c)\}\ll X^{2}+\frac{\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}X^{4}}{p^{l}}+\frac{X^{6}\delta^{\prime}}{p^{l}}.

Having fixed one such triple, instead of counting possibilities for
a+d
trivially using an upper bound for the trace, we recall that

(a-d)^{2}+4bc=(a+d)^{2}-4m=(a+d-2l_{1}l_{2})(a+d+2l_{1}l_{2}).

Since we are excluding parabolic matrices, we obtain that the left-hand side is non-zero. Arguing as in the proof of
[
HT13
, Lemma 2]
we find at most
X^{\epsilon}
possibilities for tuples
(a+d,m)
. This gives

\sharp\{(a-d,a+d,b,c)\colon c\neq 0,\,(a+d)^{2}-4m\neq 0\}\ll X^{\epsilon}\left(X^{2}+\frac{\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}X^{4}}{p^{l}}+\frac{X^{6}\delta^{\prime}}{p^{l}}\right).

Finally, we account for
c=0
by following the argument in
[
HT13
, Lemma 4]
. Indeed, as in the previous lemma we use lattice point counting to estimate

\sharp\{a-d,b\}\ll X^{2}+\frac{X^{4}\delta^{\prime}}{p^{l}}y.

Then we use
ad=l_{1}^{2}l_{2}^{2}
for primes
l_{1}l_{2}
to find for each (fixed)
a-d
there are
\ll X^{\epsilon}
possibilities for pairs
(a,d)
. Combining the contributions completes the proof.
∎

For bounding the parabolic contribution, we present a new strategy that produces a result similar in shape to
[
Tem15
, Lemma 4.4]
, where the proof is left out, but with a saving in
p^{l}
.

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
z=x+iy\in\mathbb{H}
with
y\gg 1
,
0<\delta^{\prime}<1
and
0\leq l\leq n
then we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m=(l_{1}l_{2})^{2}\\
l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{X}\end{subarray}}M_{\mathrm{par}}(z,g_{p};\delta^{\prime},p^{l},m)\ll X^{2+\epsilon}+\frac{X^{4+\epsilon}y\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}}{p^{l}}.

Proof.

Let
\gamma
be parabolic with
\det\gamma=m=a^{2}
, where
a=l_{1}l_{2}
.
Then there exists
\xi\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
such that

\xi\gamma\xi^{-1}=\gamma^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
0&a\end{pmatrix}.

Thus, it suffices to count the possibilities for
\xi\in U(\mathbb{Z})\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, where
U(\mathbb{Z})
are upper triangular matrices in
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, and
\gamma^{\prime}
of the form above.
We note that the classes
U(\mathbb{Z})\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
are parametrised by the second row of their representative.

To start, the number of
\gamma=\operatorname{diag}(a,a)
is simply bounded by the number of terms in the sum, that is
X^{2}
.
From now on, we assume that
\gamma
is not a central matrix.
This implies that
\gamma^{\prime}
as above must satisfy that
b\neq 0
.

At the place
p
, write

\xi g_{p}=\begin{pmatrix}t&u\\
v&w\end{pmatrix}.

Then we can rewrite the condition as

(\xi g_{p})^{-1}\gamma^{\prime}(\xi g_{p})\in K_{H,p}(l,l).

Using the computation (
7.16
), we deduce from the special shape of
\gamma^{\prime}
that

p^{l}\mid v^{2}b\quad\text{and}\quad p^{l}\mid w^{2}b.

Since
\gcd(w,v)=1
, it follows that
p^{l}\mid b
.

Moving to the archimedean place, put
z^{\prime}=\xi z=x^{\prime}+iy^{\prime}
.
Then the condition can be rewritten as
u(\gamma^{\prime}z^{\prime},z^{\prime})\leq\delta^{\prime}
, by invariance of the distance function.
In terms of matrix entries as in (
7.15
), this implies that
b\ll ay^{\prime}\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}
.

Since
p^{l}\mid b
and
b\neq 0
by assumption, this gives a bound of
X^{2}y^{\prime}\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}p^{-l}
on the number of
b
for every fixed
\xi
.
There is no such
\xi
unless
1/y^{\prime}\ll a\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}
.

Now we sum up all possibilities, sorting by
\xi
, to get that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lvert{cz+d}\rvert^{2}\leq yX^{2}\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}},\\
(c,d)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{X^{2+\epsilon}y\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}}{p^{l}\lvert{cz+d}\rvert^{2}}\ll p^{-l}X^{2}y\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}.

Here we have used the formula for
y^{\prime}
and that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lvert{cz+d}\rvert^{2}\leq Y,\\
(c,d)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\lvert{cz+d}\rvert^{2}}\leq Y^{\epsilon}\sum_{(c,d)=1}\frac{1}{\lvert{cz+d}\rvert^{2+\epsilon}}\ll_{\epsilon}Y^{\epsilon},

for any
Y
. In the last step we have used absolute convergence of the sum, see
[
Iwa02
, Section 3.2]
, as well as
y\gg 1
. We now finish the count by considering all possibilities for the determinant.
∎

Finally, we also need the following result.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
z=x+iy\in\mathbb{H}
with
y\gg 1
,
g_{p}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
,
0<\delta^{\prime}<1
and
0\leq l
then we have

M(z,g_{p};\delta^{\prime},p^{l},1)\ll 1+\frac{\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}}{p^{l}}y.

Proof.

Counting
M(z,g_{p},\delta^{\prime},p^{l},1)
is straight forward following the argument from
[
Blo+20
, Lemma 11]
for example. We omit the details.
∎

We now present our counting results in a compact form, which is convenient for the application below.

Corollary 7.10
.

Let
z=x+iy\in\mathbb{H}
with
y\gg 1
,
g_{p}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
,
0<\delta^{\prime}<1
and
0\leq l
. Then we have

M(z,g_{p},\delta^{\prime},p^{l},1)\ll 1+\frac{\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}}{p^{l}}y.

Furthermore, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m=l_{1}^{i}l_{2}^{i}\\
l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{X}\\
i=1,2\end{subarray}}m^{-\frac{1}{2}}M(z,g_{p};\delta^{\prime},p^{l},m)\ll X^{1+\epsilon}+X^{2+\epsilon}\frac{\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}}{p^{l}}y+\frac{X^{4+\epsilon}\delta^{\prime}}{p^{l}}.

Remark 7.11
.

Our counting is based on a quite elaborate lattice point counting scheme and has its origin in a series of papers by Templier and Harcos-Templier. Historically, the development of the counting method (and also of the amplifier) was driven by optimizing the sup-norm bounds in the (square-free) level aspect. Indeed, while there have been many improvements in the level aspect over the years, the spectral bound from
[
IS95
]
has not been surpassed at the time of writing the present article.

This being said, efficient counting turns out to be crucial for us.
We will illustrate this in Section
8.2
below, where Corollary
8.3
shows that our soft analysis and the old counting techniques of Iwaniec and Sarnak are not enough to recover the best known exponent in the spectral aspect.
See the discussion after Theorem
3.7
for the comparison of the analytic results.

For later reference, we record the following consequence of Lemma
7.4
. For
z\in\mathcal{F}
,
g_{p}\in\textrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and
\delta^{\prime}\leq 1
we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m=l_{1}^{i}l_{2}^{i}\\
l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{X}\\
i=1,2\end{subarray}}m^{-\frac{1}{2}}M(z,g_{p};\delta^{\prime},p^{l},m)\ll X^{\epsilon}(X+X^{4}(\delta^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{4}}+X^{2}\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}y).

Observe that here we encounter a term featuring
X^{4}(\delta^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{4}}
, while in the refined counting result this is improved to
X^{4}\delta^{\prime}p^{-2l}
.
It is this improvement which will be responsible for recovering the Iwaniec–Sarnak spectral exponent even with slightly weaker bounds on the archimedean test functions.

8
The global period II

In this section, we combine all the pieces and finally prove our main Theorem
1.1
(see also Theorem
8.7
below). The strategy behind the proof was sketched in Section
2.7
and we carry out the details here. We first upgrade the relative trace fromula from Proposition
6.1
by including the amplifier discussed in Section
7.1
. This results in Proposition
8.1
, which is the starting point of our analysis. For pedagogical purposes we will present three sub-baseline sup-norm bounds gradually increasing the technical difficulty of the argument:

•

In Theorem
8.2
, we present the most naive argument, which is closest to the sketch given in Section
2.7
. Here we estimate the archimedean test function using the geometric ideas developed in Section
3
and handle the finite ramified place trivially. We also directly import the original counting result from Iwaniec and Sarnak
[
IS95
]
. As a result we obtain a bound that is sub-baseline in the spectral aspect and matches the baseline bound in the level aspect.

•

The next result is Proposition
8.4
. Here, we refine our argument by improving the analysis at the ramified finite place. We do so by bounding the orbital integrals (i.e. newform matrix coefficients) on the geometric side using the soft geometric tools developed in Sections
4.6.4
and
4.6.5
. This refinement also requires the use of the new counting results obtained in Section
7.3
. As a result we obtain a hybrid sub-baseline bound for the sup-norm, which matches the Iwaniec–Sarnak exponent in the spectral aspect.

•

Finally, the best bounds are obtained in Theorem
8.7
below. Here, we replace the soft treatment of the
p
-adic test-function by sharp bounds for newform matrix coefficients obtained by Hu and Saha. This allows for an improvement of the exponent in the level aspect. Indeed, the achieved hybrid sup-norm bound finally has the Iwaniec–Sarnak exponents in all aspects.

Throughout this section, we let
\pi
be a cuspidal automorphic representation for
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q})
with trivial central character and level
N=p^{4n}
. We write
N_{0}=p^{2n}
. We assume that
\pi
is spherical at infinity and denote its spectral parameter by
T
. For convenience, we assume that
T
is positive and sufficiently large. In particular,
\pi
is assumed to be tempered at infinity.

8.1
An amplified relative pretrace inequality

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
f_{\infty}\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(Z\backslash G(\mathbb{R}))
and
f_{p}\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(Z\backslash G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}))
be two test functions, which we combine to

f=f_{\infty}\otimes f_{p}\otimes\bigotimes_{l\neq p}\mathbb{1}_{ZK_{l}}.

Then, for
g\in G(\mathbb{R})\times G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
we have

(8.1)

|\varphi^{\prime}(g)|^{2}\cdot\sum_{\Psi\in\mathcal{B}^{N}(\pi)}\mathcal{Q}(\pi(f)\Psi)\\
\leq\frac{8}{(\sharp\mathcal{P}_{X})^{2}}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}a_{m}\int_{x,y\in H}\sum_{\gamma\in G^{\prime}(\mathbb{Q})}(f\ast f^{\ast})(x^{-1}g^{-1}\gamma gy)\cdot\kappa_{m}(\gamma)\,dx\,dy.

with coefficients
a_{m}
as in (
7.6
).

Proof.

We first define the test-function

\tilde{f}_{i}=f_{\infty}\otimes f_{p}\otimes f_{\textrm{ur},i}^{X}\text{ for }i=1,2.

If
\Psi\in\mathcal{B}^{N}(\pi)
, then

(8.2)

\pi(\tilde{f}_{i})\Psi=\mathcal{S}_{\pi,i}(X)\cdot\pi(f)\Psi

for

\mathcal{S}_{\pi,i}(X)=\sum_{l\in\mathcal{P}_{X}}|\lambda_{\pi}(l^{i})|.

In particular
C_{X}(\pi)=\mathcal{S}_{\pi,1}(X)^{2}+\mathcal{S}_{\pi,2}(X)^{2}
. Of course,
\pi(\tilde{f}_{i})\Psi=0
if
\Psi\in\mathcal{B}(\pi)\setminus\mathcal{B}^{N}(\pi)
. This allows us to write

C_{X}(\pi)\cdot\sum_{\Psi\in\mathcal{B}^{N}(\pi)}\mathcal{Q}(\pi(f)\Psi)=\sum_{i=1,2}\sum_{\Psi\in\mathcal{B}(\pi)}\mathcal{Q}(\pi(\tilde{f}_{i})\Psi).

Applying (
6.6
) to each piece and expanding
f_{\textrm{ur}}^{X}=f_{\textrm{ur},1}^{X}\ast f_{\textrm{ur},1}^{X}+f_{\textrm{ur},2}^{X}\ast f_{\textrm{ur},2}^{X}
using (
7.6
) yields

(8.3)

C_{X}(\pi)\cdot|\varphi^{\prime}(1)|^{2}\cdot\sum_{\Psi\in\mathcal{B}^{N}(\pi)}\mathcal{Q}(\pi(f)\Psi)\\
\leq\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}a_{m}\int_{x,y\in H}\sum_{\gamma\in G^{\prime}(\mathbb{Q})}(f\ast f^{\ast})(x^{-1}\gamma y)\,dx\,dy\cdot\kappa_{m}(\gamma).

After dividing by
C_{X}(\pi)
and applying the lower bound (
7.5
) the proof of the initial case is complete. To deal with general
g
one follows the arguments leading to (
6.7
).
∎

8.2
The naive archimedean bound

Let us start by using the formula from Proposition
3.2
to prove a sub-baseline bound in the spectral aspect.

Theorem 8.2
.

Let
\pi
be a cuspidal automorphic representation for
\textrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q})
with trivial central character, level
N=p^{4n}
and spectral parameter
T
. We assume that
T
is sufficiently large, so that in particular
\pi
is tempered and spherical at infinity. Let
g=g_{\infty}g_{p}
with
g_{\infty}.i=x+iy\in\mathcal{F}
and
g_{p}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. For the unique
L^{2}
-normalized
K_{H}(2n,2n)
-invariant form
\varphi^{\prime}
in
\pi
defined in (
5.1
). We have

|\varphi^{\prime}(g)|^{2}\ll N^{\frac{1}{2}}T^{\frac{11}{12}+\epsilon}+N^{\frac{1}{2}}T^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}y.

Proof.

Our starting point is Proposition
8.1
with the test functions
f_{\infty}
and
f_{p}
as in Section
6.3
. The parameter
X
will be chosen as a power of
T
, so that
\sharp\mathcal{P}_{X}\gg XT^{-\epsilon}
. Furthermore, recall from Theorem
3.7
and Lemma
4.35
that

(8.4)

\sum_{\Psi\in\mathcal{B}^{N}(\pi)}\mathcal{Q}(\pi(f)\Psi)\gg p^{-n}T^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

On the other hand we estimate the
H
-integrals as

(8.5)

\int_{x,y\in H}(f\ast f^{\ast})(x^{-1}g^{-1}\gamma gy)\,dx\,dy\ll T^{\epsilon}p^{n}\cdot\min(d(g_{\infty}^{-1}\gamma g_{\infty})^{-1},T^{\frac{1}{2}}).

For the archimedean part we have used the estimate from Theorem
3.7
. On the other hand, we have used only the trivial bound for the non-archimedean part, see Corollary
4.41
.

Altogether, using also the support properties of the test functions, we obtain

|\varphi^{\prime}(g)|^{2}\ll\frac{T^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}N^{\frac{1}{2}}}{X^{2}}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}a_{m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in G^{\prime}(\mathbb{Q}),\\
d(g_{\infty}^{-1}\gamma g_{\infty})\ll 1,\\
d_{H}(g_{p}^{-1}\gamma g_{p})\leq 1\end{subarray}}\min(d(g_{\infty}^{-1}\gamma g_{\infty})^{-1},T^{\frac{1}{2}})\cdot|\kappa_{m}(\gamma)|.

We sort the
\gamma
-sum by
d(g_{\infty}^{-1}\gamma g_{\infty})\in[\delta/2,\delta]
and sum
\delta
over a dyadic intervals. This way we get

(8.6)

|\varphi^{\prime}(g)|^{2}\ll\frac{T^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}N^{\frac{1}{2}}}{X^{2}}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{a_{m}}{m^{\frac{1}{2}}}\bigg[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T^{-\frac{1}{2}}\leq\delta\ll 1,\\
\text{dyadic}\end{subarray}}\delta^{-1}\cdot\sharp\mathfrak{M}(g_{\infty},g_{p};m,\delta,0)\\
+T^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\sharp\mathfrak{M}(g_{\infty},g_{p};m,T^{-\frac{1}{2}},0)\bigg].

We first discuss the contribution from
d(g_{\infty}^{-1}\gamma g_{\infty})<T^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. Indeed, by Lemma
7.2
and Lemma
7.4
we obtain

\sharp\mathfrak{M}(g_{\infty},g_{p};m,T^{-\frac{1}{2}},0)\leq M(z,g_{p},T^{-1},1,m)\ll_{\epsilon}m^{\epsilon}(1+mT^{-\frac{1}{4}}+m^{\frac{1}{2}}T^{-\frac{1}{2}}y).

Inserting this estimate above leads to a contribution

\displaystyle\frac{T^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}N^{\frac{1}{2}}}{X^{2}}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{a_{m}}{m^{\frac{1}{2}}}T^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\sharp\mathfrak{M}(g_{\infty},g_{p};m,T^{-\frac{1}{2}},0)

\displaystyle\qquad\ll\frac{T^{1+\epsilon}N^{\frac{1}{2}}}{X^{2}}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}a_{m}(m^{\epsilon-\frac{1}{2}}+m^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}T^{-\frac{1}{4}}+m^{\epsilon}T^{-\frac{1}{2}}y)

\displaystyle\qquad\ll\frac{T^{1+\epsilon}N^{\frac{1}{2}}}{X^{2}}(X+X^{4}T^{-\frac{1}{4}}+X^{2}T^{-\frac{1}{2}}y).

In the last step we have estimated the
m
-sum by recalling that the sequence
a_{m}
is supported on
m=l_{1}^{i}l_{2}^{i}
for
i=1,2
and
l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(X)
. We have also used the bound
a_{m}\ll 1+\delta_{m=1}\cdot X
. In summary, we see that the
\gamma
’s with
d(g_{\infty}^{-1}\gamma g_{\infty})<T^{-\frac{1}{2}}
contribute at most

\frac{T^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}N^{\frac{1}{2}}}{X^{2}}\left(XT^{\frac{1}{2}}+X^{4}T^{\frac{1}{4}}+X^{2}y\right)

to our bound for
|\varphi^{\prime}(g)|^{2}
. It turns out that this will match the contribution of
\delta\geq T^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, which we will discuss next.

As before, we start by using Lemma
7.2
. This allows us to write

|\varphi^{\prime}(g)|^{2}\ll\frac{T^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}N^{\frac{1}{2}}}{X^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T^{-1}\leq\delta^{\prime}\ll 1,\\
\text{dyadic}\end{subarray}}(\delta^{\prime})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{|a_{m}|}{m^{\frac{1}{2}}}M(z,g_{p};\delta^{\prime},1,m).

We now recall that
a_{1}\ll X
and that
M(z,g_{p};\delta^{\prime},1,m)\ll 1+\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}y
by Lemma
7.4
. On the other hand, we have
a_{m}\ll 1
if
m=l_{1}^{i}l_{2}^{i}
for
i=1,2
and
l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{X}
. Thus, using the bound from Remark
7.11
for the remaining
m
-sum, we obtain

|\varphi^{\prime}(g)|^{2}\ll\frac{T^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}N^{\frac{1}{2}}}{X^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T^{-1}\leq\delta^{\prime}\ll 1,\\
\text{dyadic}\end{subarray}}\left(X(\delta^{\prime})^{-\frac{1}{2}}+X^{4}(\delta^{\prime})^{-\frac{1}{4}}+X^{2}y\right).

The length of the dyadic
\delta^{\prime}
-sum is
\ll T^{\epsilon}
. Thus executing it trivially yields

|\varphi^{\prime}(g)|^{2}\ll\frac{T^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}N^{\frac{1}{2}}}{X^{2}}\left(XT^{\frac{1}{2}}+X^{4}T^{\frac{1}{4}}+X^{2}y\right).

Finally, choosing
X=T^{\frac{1}{12}}
gives the desired result.
∎

Corollary 8.3
.

We keep the notation from Theorem
8.2
and let
\Omega\subset\mathcal{F}
be a fixed compact set. Put
\Omega^{\prime}=\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\Omega
. Then we have

\|\varphi^{\prime}|_{\Omega^{\prime}}\|_{\infty}\ll_{\Omega,\epsilon}N^{\frac{1}{4}}T^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{24}+\epsilon}.

Note that we match here the local bound (i.e. Theorem
6.3
) in the level aspect. However, we have improved the exponent in the spectral aspect by
1/24
. Note that this is worse than the improvement of
1/12
that is established in
[
IS95
]
. This is an important observation, because we have used the same counting (and even an improved amplifier). The only difference is that the volume bound we use to handle the geometric side is slightly weaker than the Iwaniec–Sarnak analogue. See Theorem
3.5
and the surrounding discussion.

It turns out that we can still match the saving obtained by Iwaniec and Sarnak in the spectral aspect by using the improved counting results developed above. This will be implemented in the next section. Doing so we will also perform a non-trivial analysis at the place
p
and obtain a hybrid sub-local bound, which is our final result.

8.3
The general bound

We will use
g_{p}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and allow
g_{\infty}
to vary such that
z=x+iy=g_{\infty}.i
satisfies
y\gg 1
. We will start estimating the amplified (relative) trace inequality from Proposition
8.1
essentially as in the previous subsection. However, we will replace
f_{p}
by
f_{p}^{\prime}=f_{p}\cdot\mathbb{1}_{ZK_{H}(1)}
as defined in Remark
6.2
. Note that the lower bound from (
8.4
) still holds, up to possibly changing the implicit constant, which is now allowed to depend on
p
. The point of restricting the support of the
p
-adic test function is to slightly simplify the counting problem. This trick was already alluded to in Remark
7.3
above. Indeed, suppose
f_{p}^{\prime}(x^{-1}g_{p}^{-1}\gamma g_{p}y)\neq 0
for
x,y\in H(2n)
, then
g_{p}^{-1}\gamma g_{p}\in K_{H}(1)
. However, this implies that
p\mid[g_{p}^{-1}\gamma g_{p}]_{21}
and we infer that

d_{H(2n)}(wg_{p}^{-1}\gamma g_{p})=0.

As a result we can drop the maximum over
\gamma^{\prime}
in our volume bound Corollary
4.41
. This allows us to replace the estimate (
8.5
), which was trivial in the level aspect, by the finer estimate

(8.7)

\int_{x,y\in H}(f\ast f^{\ast})(x^{-1}g^{-1}\gamma gy)\,dx\,dy\ll_{p}T^{\epsilon}\cdot\min(d(\gamma)^{-1},T^{\frac{1}{2}})\\
\cdot\min\left(p^{n/2}d_{H(2n)}(g_{p}^{-1}\gamma g_{p})^{-\frac{1}{2}},p^{n}\right).

We now drop any additional support property coming from
f_{p}^{\prime}
and continue the argument as above. We sort the
\gamma
-sum according to
d(\gamma)\in[\delta/2,\delta]
and
d_{H(2n)}(\gamma)=p^{-l}
. In view of Lemma
7.2
we obtain

|\varphi^{\prime}(g)|^{2}\ll_{p,\epsilon}\frac{T^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}p^{n}}{X^{2}}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{|a_{m}|}{m^{\frac{1}{2}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T^{-1}\leq\delta^{\prime}\leq 1\\
\text{dyadic}\end{subarray}}\sum_{l=0}^{n}M(g_{\infty},g_{p};\delta^{\prime},p^{l},m)\cdot\frac{p^{\frac{n+l}{2}}}{(\delta^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

Recall the bounds
a_{1}\ll X
and
a_{m}\ll 1
for
m>1
as well as the support of the weights
a_{m}
. Inserting the counting results from Corollary
7.10
yields

|\varphi^{\prime}(g)|^{2}\ll_{p,\epsilon}\frac{T^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}p^{n}}{X^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T^{-1}\leq\delta^{\prime}\leq 1\\
\text{dyadic}\end{subarray}}\sum_{l=0}^{n}\frac{p^{\frac{n+l}{2}}}{(\delta^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\cdot\left(X+X^{2}\frac{\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}}{p^{l}}y+\frac{X^{4}\delta^{\prime}}{p^{l}}\right).

Here we have ignored the contribution of
\delta^{\prime}<T^{-1}
, which can be handled similarly and will give a comparable contribution. Executing the
\delta^{\prime}
and
l
sums trivially yields

|\varphi^{\prime}(g)|^{2}\ll_{p,\epsilon}\frac{T^{1+\epsilon}p^{(2+\epsilon)n}}{X}+T^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}p^{(\frac{3}{2}+\epsilon)n}(X^{2}+y).

At this point we choose
X=(Tp^{n})^{\frac{1}{6}}
. After recalling that
N=p^{4n}
we obtain

|\varphi^{\prime}(g)|^{2}\ll_{p,\epsilon}T^{\frac{5}{6}+\epsilon}N^{\frac{11}{24}+\epsilon}+T^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}N^{\frac{3}{8}+\epsilon}y.

We record this as a proposition.

Proposition 8.4
.

Let
g_{\infty}\in G(\mathbb{R})
and
g_{p}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and assume that
g_{\infty}.i=x+iy\in\mathbb{H}
satisfies
y\gg 1
. Then we have

|\varphi^{\prime}(g_{\infty})|\ll_{p,\epsilon}T^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{12}}N^{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{48}}+T^{\frac{1}{4}}N^{\frac{3}{16}}\sqrt{y}.

Note that this immediately provides a numerical improvement over the result from Corollary
8.3
. For our main result we want to make this estimate global.

Theorem 8.5
.

Let
\pi
be a cuspidal automorphic representation for
\textrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q})
with trivial central character, level
N=p^{4n}
and spectral parameter
T
. We assume that
T
is sufficiently large, so that in particular
\pi
is tempered and spherical at infinity. For the unique
L^{2}
-normalized newform
\varphi
in
\pi
we have

\|\varphi\|_{\infty}\ll_{p,\epsilon}(TN)^{\epsilon}\cdot\left(T^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{12}+\epsilon}N^{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{48}+\epsilon}\right).

Proof.

Recall the shifted newform
\varphi^{\prime}
from (
5.1
) and note that
\|\varphi\|_{\infty}=\|\varphi^{\prime}\|_{\infty}
. By strong approximation it is sufficient to estimate
\varphi^{\prime}(g)
where
g=g_{\infty}g_{p}
where
g_{\infty}.i=z=x+iy\in\mathbb{H}
lies in the standard fundamental domain for
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and
g_{p}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. We first observe that, for
\sqrt{y}>T^{\frac{1}{6}}N^{\frac{1}{24}}
, the Fourier bound from Proposition
A.1
yields

|\varphi^{\prime}(g_{\infty})|\ll_{p,\epsilon}T^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{6}+\epsilon}N^{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{24}+\epsilon}.

For
\sqrt{y}\leq T^{\frac{1}{6}}N^{\frac{1}{24}}
we use the pretrace bound and obtain

|\varphi^{\prime}(g_{\infty})|\ll_{p,\epsilon}T^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{12}+\epsilon}N^{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{48}+\epsilon}.

This is the desired bound.
∎

Remark 8.6
.

We have stated this global sup-norm bound for the classical newform
\varphi
(i.e. the unique
L^{2}
-normalized
K_{H}(N)
-invariant vector.) This is simply a cosmetic choice because, as already observed in the proof, we have
\|\varphi\|_{\infty}=\|\varphi^{\prime}\|_{\infty}
. However, in the bounds for the restricted sup-norm, for example Theorem
8.2
, it is essential that we work with
\varphi^{\prime}
. This is because we fix the compact set
\Omega
independent of
N
and
T
. But then
\|\varphi|_{a(N_{0})\Omega}\|_{\infty}=\|\varphi^{\prime}|_{\Omega}\|_{\infty}
and the volume of
\Omega
and
a(N_{0})\Omega
differ significantly. Classically speaking, if we bound
\varphi^{\prime}
at the point
i\in\mathbb{H}
, this corresponds to bounding
\varphi
at
\frac{i}{N_{0}}
.

Note that arguably the most important analytic ingredients for our proof are the volume bounds given in Theorem
3.7
(resp. Corollary
4.41
) in the archimedean (resp.
p
-adic aspect). In contrast to earlier works, these bounds are somehow of geometric nature and do not rely on direct analysis of the Selberg–Harish-Chandra transform (or other elaborate constructions of test functions using Paley–Wiener theory) as in
[
IS95
]
or
[
Tem15
]
.
In the
p
-adic case, we completely avoid any stationary phase analysis of new-vector matrix coefficients as for example in
[
HS20
]
. While our bound matches the long-standing Iwaniec–Sarnak barrier in the spectral aspect we have a rather weak exponent in the level. This can be improved using matrix coefficients bounds. One obtains the following very harmonic result:

Theorem 8.7
.

Let
\pi
be a cuspidal automorphic representation for
\textrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q})
with trivial central character, level
N=p^{4n}
and spectral parameter
T
. We assume that
T
is sufficiently large, so that in particular
\pi
is tempered and spherical at infinity. For the unique
L^{2}
-normalized newform
\varphi
in
\pi
we have

\|\varphi\|_{\infty}\ll_{p,\epsilon}(TN_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{12}+\epsilon}.

Proof.

Again we have to estimate
\varphi^{\prime}(g)
(as in (
5.1
)) for
g=g_{\infty}g_{p}
with
g_{p}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and
g_{\infty}.i=x+iy
with
y\gg 1
. Note that for
\sqrt{y}>T^{\frac{1}{6}}N^{\frac{1}{12}}
we can simply use Proposition
A.1
. This will be complemented using the amplification method.

We want to run the same argument as above. However, we can use (
4.31
) to improve (
8.7
) and obtain

(8.8)

\int_{x,y\in H}(f\ast f^{\ast})(x^{-1}g^{-1}\gamma gy)\,dx\,dy\ll_{p,\epsilon}T^{\epsilon}\cdot\min(d(\gamma)^{-1},T^{\frac{1}{2}})\\
\cdot\min\left(d_{H(2n)}(\gamma)^{-\frac{1}{2}},p^{n}\right).

With this at hand, we obtain

|\varphi^{\prime}(g)|^{2}\ll_{p,\epsilon}\frac{T^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}p^{n}}{X^{2}}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{|a_{m}|}{m^{\frac{1}{2}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T^{-1}\leq\delta^{\prime}\leq 1\\
\text{dyadic}\end{subarray}}\sum_{l=0}^{2n}M(g_{\infty},g_{p};\delta^{\prime},p^{l},m)\cdot\frac{p^{\frac{l}{2}}}{(\delta^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

From here we follow the same argument as above. Namely, we recall support and size of the amplification weights
a_{m}
, insert our counting results and execute the
\delta^{\prime}
- and
l
-sums trivially. We arrive at

|\varphi^{\prime}(g)|^{2}\ll_{p,\epsilon}\frac{T^{1+\epsilon}p^{(2+\epsilon)n}}{X}+T^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}p^{(1+\epsilon)n}(X^{2}+y).

Here we choose
X=(TN_{0})^{\frac{1}{6}}
and obtain

|\varphi^{\prime}(g)|^{2}\ll_{p,\epsilon}(TN_{0})^{\frac{5}{6}+\epsilon}+(TN_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}y.

In the range
\sqrt{y}\leq T^{\frac{1}{6}}N^{\frac{1}{12}}
this bound is sufficient.
∎

Appendix A
Bounds via the Fourier expansion

In this appendix, we prove the following proposition.

Proposition A.1
.

Let
\pi
be a cuspidal automorphic representation with conductor
N=p^{4n}
, trivial central character and spectral parameter
T
. We assume that
T
is positive and sufficiently large.
29
29

29

This assumption can be dropped if one replaces
T
be
1+|T|
in the upper bounds.
Let
\varphi
be the
L^{2}
-normalized newform and write
\varphi^{\prime}=\pi(a(N_{0}))\varphi
as in (
5.1
), for
N_{0}=N^{\frac{1}{2}}
. Let
g_{\infty}\in G(\mathbb{R})
with
g_{\infty}.i=x+iy\in\mathcal{F}
and let
g_{p}\in K_{p}
be arbitrary. Then we have

\varphi^{\prime}(g_{\infty}g_{p})\ll_{p,\epsilon}(TN)^{\epsilon}\cdot\left((N_{0}T)^{\frac{1}{6}}+\frac{(N_{0}T)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{y^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right).

This will be proven using the Fourier/Whittaker expansion of
\varphi
. We will need some preparations. We keep the notation from Proposition
A.1
throughout this section and refer to Section
5.2
for details concerning
\pi
and
\varphi
.

Let
g_{\infty}\in G(\mathbb{R})
such that

g_{\infty}.i=x+iy\in\mathcal{F}

and write
g_{p}^{\prime}=g_{p}\cdot a(N_{0})
. Our goal is to estimate

\varphi^{\prime}(g_{\infty}g_{p})=\varphi(g_{\infty}g_{p}^{\prime}).

Recall that the global Whittaker function

(A.1)

W_{\varphi}(g)=\int_{\mathbb{Q}\backslash\mathbb{A}}\varphi(n(x)g)\psi(x)dx

of
\varphi
associated to the standard additive character
\psi
of
\mathbb{Q}\backslash\mathbb{A}
factors as

W_{\varphi}=c_{\pi}\prod_{v}W_{v}.

Here we normalise
W_{l}(1)=1
for all finite places
l
(including
l=p
) and

|W_{\infty}(a(q)g_{\infty})|=|Tqy|^{\frac{1}{2}}\dot{e}^{\frac{\pi t}{2}}|K_{iT}(2\pi|qy|)|.

Note that by Rankin-Selberg theory we have

c_{\pi}\ll(TN)^{\epsilon}T^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

(Note that we have re-scaled by
T^{-\frac{1}{2}}
in order to appropriately normalise the
K
-Bessel function.) Furthermore,

\prod_{l\neq p}W_{l}(a(q))=\lambda_{\pi}(m)\cdot m^{-\frac{1}{2}}\text{ for }q=m\cdot p^{r}

with
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\neq 0}
and
r\in\mathbb{Z}
. (If
q
is not of this shape, then the unramified part vanishes.)

We need to recall some facts concerning the ramified Whittaker new-vector
W_{p}
.
Since we are assuming that
\pi
has level
N=p^{4n}
and trivial central character, the function
W_{p}
is right invariant by the (local) Hecke congruence subgroup
K_{H,p}(4n)
defined in (
4.1
).
Write
K_{H,p}^{\prime}(4n)\subseteq K_{H,p}(4n)
for the subgroup consisting of elements
k
whose bottom right entry
k_{2,2}
satisfies
k_{2,2}-1\in p^{4n}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. By the decomposition given in
[
Sah17
, (3)]
every
g\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
can be written as

g\in ZN(\mathbb{Q}_{p})g_{t(g),l(g),v(g)}K_{H,p}^{\prime}(4n),

for
t(g)\in\mathbb{Z}
,
0\leq l(g)\leq 4n
,
v(g)\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
and

g_{t,l,v}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&p^{t}\\
-1&-\frac{v}{p^{k}}\end{matrix}\right).

Obviously, we have

|W_{p}(g)|=|W_{p}(g_{t(g),l(g),v(g)})|.

Lemma A.2
.

Let

g_{p}=\left(\begin{matrix}t&u\\
v&w\end{matrix}\right)\in K_{p}

and write
g_{p}^{\prime}=g_{p}a(p^{2n})
. Then we have

l(g_{p}^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\min(2n+v_{p}(v),4n)&\text{ if }v_{p}(w)=0\\
\max(0,2n-v_{p}(w))&\text{ if }v_{p}(w)>0,\end{cases}

and
t(g_{p}^{\prime})=-2n-2\cdot\min(2n,v_{p}(v)).

Proof.

By the Iwasawa decomposition we find
y\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}
and
k=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in K_{p}
such that
g_{p}^{\prime}\in ZN(\mathbb{Q}_{p})a(y)k
. It is the content of
[
Sah17
, Remark 2.1]
that
l(g_{p}^{\prime})=\min(v_{p}(c),4n)
and
t(g_{p}^{\prime})=v_{p}(y)-2l(g_{p}^{\prime})
. Therefore, it remains to compute the Iwasawa decomposition of
g_{p}^{\prime}
. We first look at the case
w\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. Then we can write

\left(\begin{matrix}p^{2n}&\frac{u}{w}\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}t-\frac{uv}{w}&0\\
p^{2n}v&w\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}p^{2n}t&u\\
p^{2n}v&w\end{matrix}\right)=g_{p}\cdot a(p^{2n}).

Thus, we have

l(g_{p}^{\prime})=\min(2n+v_{p}(v),4n)\text{ and }t(g_{p}^{\prime})=-2n-\min(4n,2v_{p}(v)).

Next, consider
w=p^{s}w_{0}
for
w_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
and
s>0
. In this case, we must have
v\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. Assume we have an Iwasawa decomposition of the form

\left(\begin{matrix}p^{z}&0\\
0&p^{z}\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}p^{y}&x\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{matrix}\right)=g_{p}^{\prime}.

Then the bottom row gives

p^{z}c=p^{2n}v\text{ and }p^{z}d=w.

Considering the valuations, we obtain

z+v_{p}(c)=2n\text{ and }z+v_{p}(d)=s.

But since
ad-bc\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, at least one of the two valuations is
0
. Thus, we have two cases. First, if
z=2n
, then
v_{p}(c)=0
and
v_{p}(d)=s-2n\geq 0
. In this case, computing the determinant we find
y=-2n
. Thus,

l(g_{p}^{\prime})=0\text{ and }t(g_{p}^{\prime})=-2n.

On the other hand we can have
z=s
, so that
v_{p}(d)=0
. Then we have
v_{p}(c)=2n-s
so that we need to assume
2n\geq s
. The determinant forces
y=2n-2s
. Thus,

l(g_{p}^{\prime})=2n-s\text{ and }t(g_{p}^{\prime})=-2n.

Combining these concludes the proof.
∎

To simplify notation we write
l=l(g_{p}^{\prime})
and
t=t(g_{p}^{\prime})
. We will assume
g_{p}
to be as in Lemma
A.2
and will frequently consider the corresponding cases.

It is easy to check that

t(a(q)g_{t,l,v})=t+v_{p}(q)\text{ and }l(a(q)g_{t,l,v})=l.

We define

s_{l}=\begin{cases}4n&\text{ if }l\leq 2n,\\
2l&\text{ if }l>2n.\end{cases}

Further write
S_{l}=p^{s_{l}}
. All the parameter combinations that appear in the following have been summarised in Table
2
below. Going through each case separately reveals that
-2n+t=-s_{l}
.

v_{p}(v)=0
,

v_{p}(v)=0
,

v_{p}(v)\leq 2n
,

v_{p}(v)>2n
,

v_{p}(w)\geq 2n

v_{p}(w)<2n

v_{p}(w)=0

v_{p}(w)=0

l(g_{p}^{\prime})=l

0

2n-v_{p}(w)

2n+v_{p}(v)

4n

t(g_{p}^{\prime})=t

-2n

-2n

-2n-2v_{p}(v)

-6n

s_{l}

4n

4n

4n+2v_{p}(v)

8n

Table 2:
Summary of parameter combinations.

We return to the study of the local Whittaker function
W_{p}
. By
[
Sah17
, Proposition 2.10]
, we have
W_{p}(g_{t^{\prime},l,v})=0
unless
t^{\prime}\geq-s_{l}
. In order to control the size of
W_{p}
we introduce the quantity
D_{l}=D_{l}(\pi_{p})\geq 0
satisfying that, for all
\epsilon>0
,
v\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
and all
r\geq 0
, the bound

(A.2)

W_{p}(g_{-s_{l}+r,l,v})\ll p^{(\epsilon-\frac{1}{2})r}\cdot D_{l}

holds with an implied constant only depending on
\epsilon
. The existence, i.e. finiteness, of
D_{l}
is not obvious, but it follows for example from the results in
[
Ass19a
]
. The relevant cases will be discussed in more detail below.

Finally, recall the Whittaker expansion

\varphi(g)=\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Q}_{\neq 0}}W_{\varphi}(a(q)g).

We put
g=g_{\infty}g_{p}^{\prime}
. In view of the vanishing criteria recalled above, we note that the sum actually runs over
q\in\frac{1}{N_{0}}\mathbb{Z}_{\neq 0}
.
30
30

30

This is also contained in
[
Sah17
, Proposition 2.11]
. Note that, in the notation of
[
Sah17
]
, we have
t-q(g_{p}^{\prime})=-s_{l}
, and one can check in the case under consideration here that
q(g_{p}^{\prime})=2n
for all configurations of
l=l(g_{p}^{\prime})
. Globally, we refer to
[
Sah17
, Lemma 3.11]
and note that
Q^{g_{p}^{\prime}}=N_{0}
.
Inserting all our estimates we obtain

\varphi^{\prime}(g_{\infty}g_{p})\ll(TN)^{\epsilon}D_{l}\frac{y^{\frac{1}{2}}}{N_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}T^{\frac{1}{2}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\neq 0}\\
(m,p)=1\end{subarray}}|\lambda_{\pi}(m)|\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}p^{\epsilon r}\cdot T^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{\frac{\pi T}{2}}|K_{iT}(2\pi\frac{|m|p^{r}y}{N_{0}})|.

We start with a slight variation of the standard argument from
[
Sah17
, Section 3.4]
anticipated in
[
Sah17
, Remark 3.9]
. Define

R=N_{0}\frac{T+T^{\frac{1}{3}+\epsilon}}{2\pi y}\asymp\frac{N_{0}T}{y}.

We obtain

(A.3)

\varphi^{\prime}(g_{\infty}g_{p})\ll\\
(TN)^{\epsilon}D_{l}\frac{y^{\frac{1}{2}}}{N_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}T^{\frac{1}{2}}}\sum_{1\leq m\leq R}(m,p^{\infty})^{\epsilon}|\lambda_{\pi}(\frac{m}{(m,p^{\infty})})|\cdot T^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{\frac{\pi T}{2}}|K_{iT}(2\pi\frac{|m|y}{N_{0}})|+\textrm{Tail}.

The tail is easily bounded using the exponential decay of the
K
-Bessel function and we will ignore its contribution for now. The remaining part of the sum is handled using Cauchy–Schwarz to separate the Hecke eigenvalues from the Bessel function.

First, we estimate

\sum_{1\leq m\leq R}Te^{\pi T}|K_{iT}(2\pi\frac{my}{N_{0}})|^{2}\ll(NT)^{\epsilon}(T^{\frac{1}{3}}+R).

One can for example follow the proof of
[
Sah17
, Lemma 3.13]
to see this. On the other hand, we have

(A.4)

\sum_{1\leq m\leq R}(m,p^{\infty})^{2\epsilon}|\lambda_{\pi}(\frac{m}{(m,p^{\infty})})|^{2}\ll(NT)^{\epsilon}R.

This is
[
Sah17
, Lemma 3.15]
. Combining everything yields

(A.5)

\varphi^{\prime}(g_{\infty}g_{p})\ll(TN)^{\epsilon}D_{l}\frac{y^{\frac{1}{2}}R^{\frac{1}{2}}}{N_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}T^{\frac{1}{2}}}(T^{\frac{1}{6}}+R^{\frac{1}{2}})\ll(TN)^{\epsilon}\cdot D_{l}\cdot(T^{\frac{1}{6}}+\frac{(N_{0}T)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{y^{\frac{1}{2}}}).

We now recall from Section
5.2
that the possible local representations
\pi_{p}
come in two different families. They are either supercuspidal or principal series. We consider these cases individually.

Lemma A.3
.

We keep the notation above and assume that
\pi_{p}
is supercuspidal. Then we have

\varphi^{\prime}(g_{\infty}g_{p})\ll_{\epsilon}(TN)^{\epsilon}\cdot(T^{\frac{1}{6}}+\frac{(N_{0}T)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{y^{\frac{1}{2}}}).

Proof.

This will follow from (
A.5
) after we establish that
D_{l}\ll 1
. This can be extracted from the proof of
[
Ass19a
, Lemma 5.1]
and we refer to
[
Ass19
, Lemma 3.4.1]
for more details. See also
[
Ass19
, Section 3.4.4]
for a summary. Let us give some more details.

First, for
l\neq 2n
, we always have
D_{l}\ll 1
. This is established in Case I and Case III of the proof of
[
Ass19a
, Lemma 5.1]
. We need to take a closer look at
l=2n
, covered in Case II of
[
Ass19a
, Lemma 5.1]
. We recall that since
\pi
is supercuspidal and we are assuming that it has trivial central character, it follows that
\pi
is twist minimal. However, by analysing the critical points appearing in the stationary phase analysis for
W_{p}(g_{-4n,2n,v})
, one finds that these are non-degenerate for twist minimal supercuspidal representations
\pi_{p}
. See
[
Ass19
, Remark 3.4.3]
for details. Thus, we also obtain
D_{2n}\ll 1
and the proof is complete.
∎

Lemma A.4
.

We keep the notation above and assume that
\pi_{p}
is principal series. Then we have

\varphi^{\prime}(g_{\infty}g_{p})\ll_{\epsilon}(TN)^{\epsilon}\cdot((TN_{0})^{\frac{1}{6}}+\frac{(N_{0}T)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{y^{\frac{1}{2}}}).

Proof.

We first note that for
l\neq 2n
we have
D_{l}\ll 1
, so that the desired bound follows from (
A.5
). This property of
D_{l}
can be extracted from the proof of
[
Ass19a
, Lemma 5.12]
. See also
[
Ass19
, Lemma 3.4.16 and Section 3.4.4]
.

We have to be more careful with the case
l=2n
. Here we first observe that one has

(A.6)

W_{p}(g_{-s_{l}+r,l,v})\ll 1

as long as
r>0
. This follows by combining
[
Ass19a
, Lemma 3.6]
with the estimates from
[
Ass19a
, Lemma 5.11]
. A compact summary is also contained in
[
Ass19
, Section 3.4.4]
. Thus, we estimate the Whittaker expansion as

\varphi^{\prime}(g_{\infty}g_{p})\ll(TN)^{\epsilon}(\mathcal{S}_{0}+\mathcal{S}_{\geq 1})+\textrm{Tail},

where

\mathcal{S}_{0}=\frac{y^{\frac{1}{2}}}{N_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}T^{\frac{1}{2}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq m\leq R\\
(m,p)=1\end{subarray}}|\lambda_{\pi}(m)|\cdot|W_{p}(a(m)g_{-4n,2n,v(g_{p}^{\prime})})|\cdot T^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{\frac{\pi T}{2}}|K_{iT}(2\pi\frac{|m|y}{N_{0}})|

and

\mathcal{S}_{\geq 1}=\frac{y^{\frac{1}{2}}}{N_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}T^{\frac{1}{2}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq m\leq R\\
p\mid m\end{subarray}}(m,p^{\infty})^{\frac{1}{2}}|\lambda_{\pi}(\frac{m}{(m,p^{\infty})})|\cdot T^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{\frac{\pi T}{2}}|K_{iT}(2\pi\frac{|m|y}{N_{0}})|.

The contribution of
S_{\geq 1}
is easy to handle. Indeed, we follow exactly the argument above. The only difference is that we replace (
A.4
) by the estimate

(A.7)

\sum_{1\leq m\leq R}(m,p^{\infty})|\lambda_{\pi}(\frac{m}{(m,p^{\infty})})|^{2}\ll(NT)^{\epsilon}R,

which is exactly
[
Sah17
, Lemma 3.15]
. One arrives at

\mathcal{S}_{\geq 1}\ll(TN)^{\epsilon}\cdot(T^{\frac{1}{6}}+\frac{(N_{0}T)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{y^{\frac{1}{2}}}).

We turn towards
\mathcal{S}_{0}
. There are two cases to consider. First, if
-1
is not a square in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, then it is shown in
[
Ass19a
, Lemma 5.12]
that we have the bound
|W_{p}(a(m)g_{-4n,2n,v(g_{p}^{\prime})})|\ll 1
. In this case we can also argue as above and obtain the strong bound

\mathcal{S}_{0}\ll(TN)^{\epsilon}\cdot(T^{\frac{1}{6}}+\frac{(N_{0}T)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{y^{\frac{1}{2}}}).

Putting everything together, we see that

\varphi^{\prime}(g_{\infty}g_{p})\ll_{\epsilon}(TN)^{\epsilon}\cdot(T^{\frac{1}{6}}+\frac{(N_{0}T)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{y^{\frac{1}{2}}})

if
p\equiv 3\text{ mod 4}
, i.e. if
-1
is not a square in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
.

Finally, we assume that
p\equiv 1\text{ mod }4
. Thus, there is
i_{p}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
with
i_{p}^{2}=-1
. In this case we can not estimate
W_{p}(a(n)g_{-4n,2n,v(g_{p}^{\prime})})
trivially, since it can take large values.

By
[
Sah17
, (3)]
we have

|W_{p}(g_{-4n,2n,v})|=|W_{p}(g_{-4n,2n,v^{\prime}})|

for
v-v^{\prime}\in p^{2n}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Furthermore, if
(m,p)=1
, then we view
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
and one obtains

|W_{p}(a(m)g_{-4n,2n,v})|=|W_{p}(g_{-4n,2n,vm^{-1}})|.

Thus we define a function

f\colon(\mathbb{Z}/p^{2n}\mathbb{Z})^{\times}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},\,m\mapsto|W_{p}(g_{-4n,2n,v(g_{p}^{\prime})m^{-1}})|.

Using Cauchy–Schwarz, (
A.4
), and the definition of
R
we conclude that

(A.8)

\mathcal{S}_{0}\ll(NT)^{\epsilon}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq m\leq R\\
(m,p)=1\end{subarray}}f(m)^{2}Te^{\pi T}|K_{iT}(2\pi\frac{my}{N_{0}})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

In
[
Sah17
, Lemma 3.12]
one can find average bounds for
f(m)^{2}
. However, these will not be sufficient for our purposes. We extract more detailed properties of
f(m)
from the proof of
[
Ass19a
, Lemma 5.12]
. See also the proof of
[
Ass19
, 3.4.16]
for more details.

The size of
f
is dictated by the discriminant

\Delta(m)=1+\frac{4v(g_{p}^{\prime})^{2}b_{\chi}^{2}}{m^{2}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}.

Here
b_{\chi}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
depends only on
\chi
. We first record the bound

f(m)\ll p^{\frac{n}{3}},

which is stated in
[
Ass19a
, Lemma 5.12]
and holds for all
m
. On the other hand, examining the argument in
[
Ass19a
, Case IV, Lemma 5.11]
shows that we also have

f(m)\ll p^{\frac{v_{p}(\Delta(m))}{2}}.

We set
v_{\pm}=\pm 2i_{p}v(g_{p}^{\prime})b_{\chi}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
and observe that

\Delta(m)=\frac{1}{m^{2}}(m-v_{+})(m-v_{-}).

Thus, if
v_{p}(\Delta(m))=\delta
, then

m\equiv v_{+}\text{ mod }p^{\delta}\text{ or }m\equiv v_{-}\text{ mod }p^{\delta}.

Put
\alpha_{r}=r/2
if
r<\frac{2n}{3}
and
\alpha_{r}=n/3
if
r\geq\frac{2n}{3}
. In view of these observations and (
A.8
) we can now estimate

\mathcal{S}_{0}\ll(NT)^{\epsilon}\left(\sum_{\pm}\sum_{r=0}^{\lceil\frac{2n}{3}\rceil}p^{2\alpha_{r}}\mathcal{S}_{r}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

for

\mathcal{S}_{r}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq m\leq R\\
m\equiv v_{\pm}\text{ mod }p^{r}\end{subarray}}Te^{\pi T}|K_{iT}(2\pi\frac{my}{N_{0}})|^{2}.

Note that for
0\leq r<\frac{2n}{3}
we could have restricted the sums further to
m\not\equiv v_{\pm}\text{ mod }p^{r+1}
. We have dropped this condition because it is irrelevant for our estimates. The sums
\mathcal{S}_{r}
can be estimated by

\mathcal{S}_{r}\ll T^{\frac{1}{3}}+\frac{R}{p^{r}}.

This follows by a slight modification of the argument from
[
Sah17
, Lemma 3.13]
. Inserting this above yields

\mathcal{S}_{0}\ll(NT)^{\epsilon}\left(\sum_{\pm}\sum_{r=0}^{\lceil\frac{2n}{3}\rceil}(p^{2\alpha_{r}}T^{\frac{1}{3}}+Rp^{2\alpha_{r}-r})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\ll(NT)^{\epsilon}((N_{0}T)^{\frac{1}{6}}+R^{\frac{1}{2}}),

where we have used that
2\alpha_{r}-r\leq 0
. This was the last sum we had to estimate and it is of the predicted size. Thus, the proof is complete.
∎

The statement from Proposition
A.1
follows from Lemma
A.3
and Lemma
A.4
after recalling from Remark
4.6
that these cover all cases that can occur.
